<meta>The text appears to be from a theological or philosophical treatise by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, likely 'On the Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences' or a related work on divine wisdom. It follows a discussion on the nature of true knowledge and revelation.</meta>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...[revela]tion, a certain sure law of life sanctifying the soul of man and directing his life toward God, demonstrating what must be done and what must be omitted. We call this wisdom by another name: **Theology** <note>Agrippa defines Theology here not merely as academic study, but as a practical, sanctifying "law of life."</note>. This wisdom, and the true knowledge of God—indeed, a certain essential contact with God that is superior to mere intellectual knowledge—is divinely delivered in the Gospel. For neither is God himself truly known without the Gospel, nor is the Gospel truly understood without divine grace. For it is manifest that those things which are handed down from God cannot be understood except through God, just as the Prophet says:

<margin>Psalm 35 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 36:9: "In your light we see light."</note></margin>
<margin>Pymander 1 <note>Refers to the 'Pymander', the first book of the 'Corpus Hermeticum' attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note></margin>
*In your light we shall see light*, which light **Hermes Trismegistus** calls the "Mind" of the divine essence, the very light rising from God. Yet our intellect, unless it is illuminated through that divine Mind, is not immune from error and labors in vain in divine matters. Whence Paul says:

<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
*We are not sufficient to think anything as if from ourselves, but our sufficiency is from God* <note>Original: "Non sumus sufficientes aliquid cogitare quasi ex nobis, sed sufficientia nostra ex Deo est."</note>, whom we must invoke and to whom we must pray at the beginning of every undertaking, but most especially in Theology. The holy **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic and philosopher.</note> commands that this must be done.

<margin>Dionysius, On Divine Names</margin>
<margin>Matthew 7</margin>
<margin>Luke 2 <note>Though the margin says Luke 2, the quote "Ask and it shall be given" appears in Luke 11:9.</note></margin>
<margin>Pymander 9</margin>
Truth itself, Christ, also said: *Ask and it shall be given to you, knock and it shall be opened to you, seek and you shall find*; namely, by seeking in faith and firmly believing. For to believe, as **Hermes** says, is itself to understand. Finally, by asking in hope with firm and indubitable expectation, and by praising and adoring Jesus Christ, from whom such most divine knowledge descends into our soul, so that he may illuminate us with the light of his spirit. Those who knock, however, [will find] min— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "minus" or "minime" on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa argues that true Theology is a divine gift rather than a human science, requiring divine grace and the "Mind" of God to be understood. He synthesizes Christian scripture with the mystical teachings of Hermes Trismegistus and Pseudo-Dionysius to emphasize that faith and prayer are the only paths to genuine divine knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Theology, Divine Grace, Hermes Trismegistus, Pseudo-Dionysius, Illumination, Faith and Reason, Gospel, Pymander</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding how one attains true knowledge of God, contrasting the path of "imitation of Christ" with the "contentious disputes" of academic scholars.</meta>

# ->ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...the working of charity with vigils and fasts, and an ardent desire throughout one’s whole life through the imitation of Jesus Christ. As John says, <margin>1 John 2</margin> "whoever says they remain in Christ ought to walk just as He walked," <margin>Galatians 5</margin> which Paul calls "faith that works through love."

Therefore, they run in vain who pursue divine matters with any sort of litigious disputes; armed with the evasions of <term>sophisms</term> <note>clever but misleading arguments used in formal logic</note> and the deceptions of <term>dialectics</term> <note>the formal system of reasoning and debate taught in medieval universities</note>, they think they can break down the doors of the sacred scriptures. Disputing great things, they are always searching, yet they find nothing because they lose themselves; and as Paul says, they are <margin>2 Timothy 2</margin> <note>The text cites Chapter 2, but the verse is 2 Timothy 3:7; original: "semper discētes, & nunq̃ ad sciētiā ueritatis puenientes"</note> "always learning, and never coming to the knowledge of the truth."

Hence this same Paul commands the <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> Corinthians that they obey and stand firm in faith, and beware lest they be deceived through dialectics and philosophy, which are empty, deceitful, and the inventions of men. They follow the elements of this corruptible world, where all knowledge comes from the senses; from these, reason takes all its material for knowledge—by discursive reasoning, composing, dividing, and gathering universal propositions from experiences <note>The author is criticizing the Aristotelian idea that all knowledge begins in the senses, arguing it is insufficient for understanding the divine</note>.

But God and Jesus Christ are above the world. The Creator of the world is above all natures, qualities, shapes, numbers, orders, and actions. As <margin>Dionysius, On Mystical Theology</margin> <term>Dionysius</term> <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian who taught that God is "beyond-being"</note> says, He is above all speech, every position and removal, above every affirmation and negation <note>This refers to the "Apophatic" tradition, which claims we can only describe what God is *not*, rather than what He *is*</note>. He is even above those celestial angels, He who ascends upon the wings of the winds <margin>Psalm 17</margin> <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 18:10-11</note> above the <term>cherubim</term> <note>a high order of angelic beings</note>, and has made the cloud His hiding place. He is the King of kings and Lord of lords, <margin>Romans 4</margin> of both the things that exist and the things that do not.

G 3

<summary>This page argues that God cannot be reached through academic logic or sensory experience, but only through the imitation of Christ and love. It invokes the "Mystical Theology" of Dionysius to describe God as a being who transcends all human language and rational categories.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, Pseudo-Dionysius, scholasticism, imitation of Christ, dialectics, transcendence, apophatic theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s discourse on the divine nature and the limitations of human intellect. The previous context likely discussed the incarnation or the descent of the Divine into the material world.</meta>

->**HENRICUS CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

<margin>Judges 6 / Psalm 71</margin>
...who bowed the heavens and descended like rain upon a fleece <note>original: "pluvia in vellus"; a reference to the miracle of Gideon’s fleece in Judges 6, often used as a metaphor for the Incarnation</note>, and took human nature upon himself. In that nature, he was made wonderful and marvelous among men in all his works, by his supernatural and divine power. Therefore, to truly know him, <term>dialectics</term> <note>The formal art of logical argumentation and debate</note> and 
<margin>Gregory Nazianzen, On Theology, Book 2</margin>
philosophy are unable to ascend, being hindered by reason, which is the enemy of holy faith. Wherefore Gregory Nazianzen <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his influential Greek theology</note> says in the second book of *Theology*: "For what will you suspect to be so divine, if you trust entirely in logical speculations? Or where will a violent or scrutinized reason lead you—you who boast about the infinite?" Faith, 
<margin>Hebrews 1</margin>
therefore, is more excellent than all cognition, insofar as it rests not on empty commentaries but entirely on divine revelation. Descending immediately from the <term>First Light</term> <note>A Neoplatonic term for the ultimate source of all being and intelligence, i.e., God</note>, faith alone can apprehend those things which are above the world. For faith itself understands the beginning of the world, as Paul says: "By faith we understand that the worlds <note>original: "secula," often translated as "the ages" or "the universe"</note> were prepared by the word of God." It ascends above the established limits of nature and wanders in that widest field, in the very author of nature. 
<margin>Romans 1 / 1 Corinthians 2</margin>
In this faith, Paul says that he received his apostleship and preaches God. And writing to the Corinthians he says: "My preaching is not in the persuasions of human wisdom, but in the demonstrations of the spirit and the truth of the faith of Jesus Christ." For faith alone is the instrument and the medium by which alone we can know God, and as the <term>Platonists</term> <note>Followers of Plato, whose philosophy Agrippa frequently blends with Christian mysticism</note> say, by which alone we approach God and obtain divine protection and virtue. But let us see what soul, when, and how it can freely use this in—

<summary>Agrippa argues that human reason and philosophy are insufficient to understand God, asserting that only faith—derived from divine revelation—can transcend natural limits to reach the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Faith vs. Reason, Gregory Nazianzen, Apostle Paul, Neoplatonism, Divine Revelation, Dialectics, First Light</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of the human mind and its capacity to know God, likely following a section on the limitations of physical senses or basic reason.</meta>

->**CONCERNING THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**<-

...by no instrument, by no art except that which, when with the whole intention of reason ascending into the mind—its own head and supreme portion—it is entirely turned toward it. Just as sometimes, because of the love of inferior and sensible things, it is entirely turned toward the imagination <note>original: "phantasia"; referring to the faculty that processes sensory images</note>, we certainly know that the human mind exists as an image of the heavenly countenance and a light inscribed within us. This mind, migrating from the fountain of truth, grasps and embraces truth alone; but the whirlwinds of phantasms <note>disturbing mental images or sensory distractions</note> do not indeed overshadow it in itself, but in us they so greatly overshadow, distract, dissipate, and disperse it, that the soul is scarcely able to enter the narrowest gate of truth. 

Therefore, enclosed as it is in our corruptible flesh and submerged in too much commerce with it, unless it overcomes the way of the flesh, attains its original nature, and emerges as a pure mind—almost like an angel—it labors in vain in divine matters. But what soul is this, if not the one which, with certain hope and desire for the heavenly divinity, commands the imagination to be silent? And which, having firmly embraced true faith, no longer trusts in the accustomed discourses of natural reason? And which, adhering to God with ardent love, lives by the mind alone, has emerged as an angel, and receives God with its whole heart? 

Whence that saying of Jeremiah: *Let him who glories, glory in this: that he knows me.* <margin>Jeremiah 9</margin> From this, Zoroaster <note>The legendary ancient Persian prophet, highly regarded by Renaissance philosophers as a source of "primeval" wisdom</note>, that most ancient philosopher, says: *The soul of man, in a certain way, draws God into itself when, retaining nothing mortal, it is entirely intoxicated by divine draughts.* And then also such a soul often exults in the harmony of the body, when it leaps back after contemplation, returning to bodily duties, and produces in these the fruits of faith and the food of justice. Therefore, of such a soul, John says it is born <note>of God; see 1 John 2:29</note>... <margin>1 John 2 etc.</margin>

                                        G 4

<summary>This page describes the mystical ascent of the soul, arguing that the mind must turn away from sensory distractions (phantasms) and natural reason to embrace divine truth. It cites the prophet Jeremiah and the ancient philosopher Zoroaster to illustrate how a pure soul can "draw God into itself" and return to earthly duties with spiritual fruit.</summary>
<keywords>contemplation, phantasia, divine light, Zoroaster, Jeremiah, Neoplatonism, purification of the soul, faith and reason</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa on the soul's ascent to God and the attainment of prophetic power, likely from Book III of "De Occulta Philosophia."</meta>

->### OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...again from God, since the light of the Most High God—much like a ray of the sun that thins the body, drawing it upward and converting it into a fiery nature—flows down through angelic minds even to our own soul. It daily urges the soul immersed in the flesh so that, stripped of all carnality, it may lay aside all animal and rational powers and operations <note>In Renaissance psychology, "animal" refers to basic sensory/motor functions, while "rational" refers to human logic. Agrippa argues for rising above both to reach "intellectual" or divine light.</note> and, living by the mind alone in noble hope, direct faith, and burning love, turned entirely toward God and made fruitful in God by God's regenerating power, it may become a "son of God" and give birth to a new **Emmanuel** <note>Hebrew: "God with us." Here, it refers to the birth of divine presence or the "Christ-self" within the individual soul.</note>. And such a soul, as often as it retreats into itself—having dismissed outward actions—and bends itself to contemplate the eternal God, is then no longer sluggish from any earthly impulse. Instead, supported by the **Father of Lights** <note>original: "patre luminum." A reference to James 1:17, meaning God as the source of all illumination.</note>, it ascends to the high peak of divine knowledge. There, it is continually filled with prophetic oracles, and is often even chosen as God's instrument for performing miracles. The prayers of such a soul, even concerning the public changes of the world itself, are not made in vain.

<margin>James 5</margin> 

Just as James warns us, saying: **Elijah** was a man similar to us, and subject to suffering, and praying he prayed that it might not rain upon the earth, and it did not rain for three years and six months; and again he prayed, and the heaven gave rain, and the earth gave its fruit. O, great miracle! A man—but especially a Christian man—who, while placed in the world, may know those things which are above the world and the author of the world himself. Then, in that very Author, he discerns and understands all lower things: not only those which *are*, and which *have been*, but also those which *are not*, and those which *are to come*. Great...

<summary>Agrippa describes how the soul, through divine light and the shedding of carnal desires, can ascend to a state of prophetic insight and miraculous power. He cites the biblical example of Elijah to show that humans can influence the natural world through spiritual union with God.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, soul's ascent, Elijah, prophecy, Emmanuel, divine light, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, miracles, James 5</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discussion on the nature of faith and the divine power granted to the "perfect" Christian, transitioning from the general nature of God to the miraculous potential of the believer.</meta>

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

Certainly, the Christian human being is a miracle; though established within the world, they rule over the world, and they perform operations <note>original: "operationes"; here referring to divine or supernatural acts</note> similar to those of the Creator of the world himself. These works are commonly called "miracles," and the root and foundation of them all is faith in Jesus Christ. Through this faith alone, a person is made as one with God and enjoys the same power, just as Christ promised, saying: "Truly I say to <margin>John 14</margin> you, he who believes in me, the works that I do, he shall do also; and greater than these shall he do, because I go to the Father. And whatever he shall ask the Father in my name, I will do, and whatever he shall ask of me, I will do, so that the Father may be glorified in the Son." <note>original: "Amen dico uobis... ut glorificetur pater in filio." These are the words of Jesus to his disciples during the Last Supper.</note> And elsewhere he says: "If you have faith as a <margin>Matthew 17</margin> grain of mustard seed, and you say to this mountain, 'Throw yourself into the <margin>Luke 17</margin> sea,' it shall be done. Therefore I say to you, whatever you ask for in prayer, believe that you will receive it, and it shall be yours." 

It is for this reason that truly Christian people, devoted to God, speak in tongues, predict the future, command the elements, drive away clouds, summon the rains, order the winds, turn away storms, heal the sick, give sight to the blind, cure the lame, cleanse lepers, cast out demons, and often raise the dead and other such things. Thus the Prophets, thus the Apostles, and thus many holy bishops <note>original: "pontifices"; used here for high-ranking church officials or saints</note>, priests, doctors <note>meaning "Teachers of the Church"</note>, and other men of God have shone, and continue to shine, with the greatest powers. 

Therefore, that power is most fitting for those who are more perfect in faith, to whom Paul says he speaks only of wisdom and <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> preaches the Gospel separately. For the Gospel, just like the Mosaic law, has another aspect in its heart <note>This suggests an "esoteric" or hidden inner meaning to the scriptures, a common theme in mystical theology.</note>—

-> F 5 <-

<summary>This page discusses the miraculous power granted to Christians through faith in Jesus Christ, claiming that believers can command nature and perform healings just as the Apostles did. It concludes by suggesting that the Gospel, like the laws of Moses, contains deeper, "perfect" wisdom intended for the spiritually mature.</summary>

<keywords>miracles, faith in Christ, divine power, spiritual perfection, healing, Apostles, Gospel vs Law, esoteric wisdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa's discussion on the distinction between the literal, public interpretation of scripture and its hidden, mystical essence. It specifically references the writings of St. Paul to justify this dual approach to Christian knowledge.</meta>

### HENRI[CUS] COR[NELIUS] AGRIP[PA]
<note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a German polymath, legal scholar, and soldier, best known for his influential works on the occult and "hidden" philosophy.</note>

...[the former] is proposed for the weaker ones, while the other is in the <term>marrow</term> <note>original: "medulla"; the deep, hidden essence of a text, as opposed to its literal surface.</note> which is revealed separately to the <note>In the mystical tradition, the "perfect" (perfectis) are those who have attained a high level of spiritual maturity and are ready for deeper truths.</note> perfect ones, just as Paul speaks of those things to the Hebrews, calling them: "These are milk for infants, and the elementary beginnings of the words of God," but the others he calls "solid food, the word of justice, and the perfect doctrine of Christ," saying: "If you wish to be carried toward the perfect doctrine of Christ, the word of initiation must be left behind," in which, clearly, the principles and foundations of divine wisdom are treated—namely, repentance from dead works, baptism, the sacraments, the laying on of hands, the authority of absolution, the resurrection of the dead, and eternal judgment. All of these things are contained in the <term>shell</term> <note>original: "cortex"; literally the bark of a tree or the skin of a fruit, representing the external, literal meaning of the Gospel.</note> of the Gospel, and are handled in schools by the <note>Academic divines of the Middle Ages who used logic and philosophy to explain Christian dogma.</note> scholastic theologians, and are reduced to problems to be disputed and discussed. 

But those matters pertaining to the higher wisdom and perfect doctrine—namely, what the heavenly gift is, and the "hidden manna" <note>A biblical reference to Revelation 2:17, signifying secret spiritual nourishment.</note> which no one knows except he who receives it, and what the "good word of God" is (which is better than that which is commonly delivered outside in parables), and that the mystery of the kingdom of God is given to be known only to the more secret disciples—as well as the powers of the world to come, the origin and end of the soul, the ministries of angelic spirits, and the condition and quality of that immense glory and happiness which we await, which "no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man"—all these things are contained in the <term>marrow</term> and the kernel of the Gospel. They are not known except by the more perfect ones, to whom is given the knowledge of the powers and

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa distinguishes between the "shell" of Christianity—the basic rituals and doctrines taught in schools—and the "marrow," which contains the hidden, mystical secrets of the soul, angels, and the kingdom of God.</summary>
<keywords>esoteric Christianity, St. Paul, Scholasticism, mysticism, secret knowledge, marrow vs shell, soul’s origin, angelic ministries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on how the knowledge of God is mediated through spiritual gifts and the leadership of the Church, specifically focusing on the roles of those chosen to guide the faithful.</meta>

->CONCERNING THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...of virtues, miracles, and prophecy, and the rest, which men cannot investigate by their own strengths, unless they have been subjected to the power of the Holy Spirit. For this reason, certain men are chosen and assigned to exercise leadership in the church, so that they themselves—enlightened in faith and knowing the will of God, and being instructed through the Gospel—may be, according to the words of Paul: leaders of the blind, a light to those who are in darkness, <margin>Romans 2</margin> instructors of the foolish, teachers of infants <note>original: "infantium"; here meaning those new to the faith or "spiritual babes."</note>, having the form of knowledge and truth in the Gospel. Such men in the church are the pontiffs, bishops, prelates, doctors <note>original: "doctores"; referring to learned teachers of theology.</note>, and those to whom the care of souls and the building up of the church has been committed. To these men, Paul, writing, says: <margin>1 Corinthians 14</margin> **He who speaks in tongues speaks not to men, but to God; for no one hears him, but in the spirit he speaks mysteries. For he who prophesies speaks to men for their building up, and exhortation, and consolation.** <note>In this context, 'prophesying' refers to the inspired interpretation of Scripture for the congregation.</note> **He who speaks in a tongue builds up himself, but he who prophesies builds up the church of God.** And he follows in the same place: **Tongues are for a sign, not to believers, but to unbelievers; prophecy, however, is not for unbelievers, but for believers. If, therefore, the whole church comes together into one place and everyone speaks in tongues, and an unlearned person <term>idiota <gloss>a layperson or someone without formal theological training</gloss></term> or an unbeliever enters, will he not say, "Are you not out of your minds?" But if everyone prophesies, and an unbeliever or unlearned person enters, he is convinced by all and judged by all; for the secrets of his heart are made manifest, and so, falling on his face, he will worship God, declaring that truly God is among you.** And finally, he concludes: **If anyone seems to be a prophet...**

<summary>This page outlines the role of Church leaders as divinely inspired guides and explains St. Paul’s teaching on the superiority of prophecy (preaching) over speaking in tongues for the edification of the community.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, Church leadership, spiritual gifts, prophecy, tongues, St. Paul, Romans, 1 Corinthians, edification, clergy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the requirements for spiritual authority and the true nature of a theologian, likely following a section discussing Pauline epistles.</meta>

### ->CONCERNING THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...to be, or is spiritual, let him recognize the things I write to you, because they are the commands of the Lord; but if anyone ignores this, he will be ignored. <note>original: *si quis autem ignorat, ignorabitur*; a citation of 1 Corinthians 14:37-38, where Paul asserts his authority</note> Behold, the Apostle presents these matters not as mere advice, but as a divine mandate and precept. Therefore, if the <term>pontiffs|High-ranking church officials or bishops</term>, prelates, and our teachers of divine wisdom do not possess the <term>prophetic spirit|The gift of divine inspiration or the ability to interpret God's will</term>, and do not prove their remarkable profession before the Church by the effect of some divine power, then certainly their spirit [lacks] the divine mind <note>The OCR 'liuinæ' is likely a transcription of 'divinae' (divine)</note>. Their faith in Christ is weak, and it languishes because the flesh dominates the spirit far too much.

For this reason, all those people—possessing, as it were, sterile souls of impiety and injustice—will be judged and condemned by God. Here then you have a description of what kind of person he ought to be who desires to attain the knowledge of God, and who truly deserves to be called a **theologian**, who desires to speak with God and to meditate on His law day and night. For it was in this way that **John the Evangelist** was named "the theologian" by <term>Dionysius|Referring to Dionysius the Areopagite, an early Christian author whose mystical writings defined 'theology' as a direct experience of God</term>.

<summary>This passage argues that religious leaders must possess a genuine "prophetic spirit" and divine power rather than mere titles, defining a true theologian as one who meditates constantly on divine law.</summary>
<keywords>Theology, St. Paul, 1 Corinthians, Prophetic Spirit, Clergy, John the Evangelist, Dionysius the Areopagite, Divine Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper way to know God, contrasting true spiritual understanding with the "contentious" methods of worldly scholars and logicians mentioned in the previous sections.</meta>

-> ### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD <-

...of whom Paul writes to the Philippians, saying: "Some preach Christ out of envy and contention, but others out of good will." <margin>Philippians 1</margin> And these contentious ones are those very debaters <note>original: *argumentatores*</note> who pursue the knowledge of God through arguments and questioning, of whom the Psalmist says: "They are corrupt and have become abominable in their pursuits." <margin>Psalm 13</margin> <note>In modern Bibles, this is typically numbered as Psalm 14.</note>

And Jude the Apostle says: "But these men blaspheme whatever they do not understand; and whatever they know naturally, like mute animals, in these things they are corrupted." <margin>The General Epistles</margin> Isaiah speaks to them again, saying: "Your wisdom and your knowledge, it has deceived you; you have failed in the multitude of your counsels." <margin>Isaiah 47</margin> For all their doctrine is carnal and worldly; they are an ambitious and arrogant people, trusting in their own wits, believing that they can know God by their own strength and can find the truth in every matter.

They believe nothing can be brought up in conversation about which they cannot argue most eloquently on both sides and offer a plausible opinion. These are clever people, overflowing with foreign learning <note>original: *alienis literis*, likely referring to secular or pagan philosophy rather than scripture</note>, and at the same time insolent, relying on a certain artificial <term>dialectic|The formal art of logical debate and reasoning used heavily in medieval universities</term>. While they know nothing at all, they desire to appear learned.

Therefore, they dispute openly in schools <note>original: *gymnasijs*</note>, strengthened by <term>sophisms|Clever but false arguments intended to deceive</term>, speaking through evasions and thinking themselves wise. But they are miserably deceived by these follies and the cunning of a fickle mind. What they think is a help to them is actually a hindrance; they vanish in their own thoughts and are handed over by God to a <term>reprobate mind|A mind abandoned by God to its own errors, unable to discern right from wrong</term>. Just where they think they see most clearly and can find the truth, they are most especially obscured...

<summary>This page criticizes "contentious" scholars who attempt to use human logic, sophistry, and worldly philosophy to understand God, arguing that such arrogance leads to spiritual blindness rather than truth.</summary>
<keywords>Knowledge of God, Dialectic, Sophistry, Scholasticism, Pride, St. Paul, Isaiah, Psalm 14</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from the introductory sections of "De incertitudine et vanitate scientiarum et artium" (On the Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences and Arts) by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, where he establishes a theological argument against human intellectual pride.</meta>

->HENRICUS CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

<margin>Romans 1</margin> Their foolish heart was darkened; though they have power among men, they are powerless before God. Claiming to be wise, they became fools. For he who speaks in <term>sophistry</term> <note>the use of clever but false arguments to deceive</note> is hateful, as the book of <margin>Ecclesiasticus 37</margin> says: Grace was not given to him by the Lord, for he was defrauded of all wisdom. Indeed, the greatest proof of foolishness is to consider oneself wise. Concerning this kind of wisdom, the <margin>1 Corinthians 3</margin> Apostle says: The wisdom <note>original: "Prudentia"</note> of the flesh is foolishness with God. And <margin>Proverbs 9</margin> Solomon calls her a foolish and noisy woman, full of allurements, knowing nothing at all, whose guests are in hell; and whoever joins them will descend to the underworld. Therefore the <margin>Isaiah 29</margin> Lord says: I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> prudence of the prudent I will reject. 

For true wisdom does not consist in noisy disputes, but is cultivated in silence and religion through faith in our Lord Jesus Christ, the fruit of which is eternal life. This is what Paul calls the knowledge which is according to godliness, for which he received his apostleship according to the faith <margin>Titus 1</margin> of God's chosen. But there is another kind of knowledge based on conjecture, about which he warns his disciple <margin>Titus 3</margin> Titus, advising him to avoid foolish questions, and genealogies, and contentions, and strivings about the law; for they are unprofitable and vain. 

Writing upon this, <margin>Jerome</margin> <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a church father known for his translation of the Bible and his critiques of secular philosophy</note> says: Dialectics <note>the formal art of logical debate</note> and Aristotle, who is the prince of these things, are accustomed to stretch out nets of arguments, and to trap the wandering freedom of theology within a thicket of <term>syllogisms</term> <note>a logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two linked premises</note>. These men, therefore, who spend whole days and nights in this, either to question or—

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa argues that human intellectualism and logical disputes (sophistry) are forms of spiritual blindness, contrasting the "wisdom of the flesh" with the true, silent wisdom found in Christian faith.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, vanity of sciences, sophistry, Aristotle, dialectics, theology vs philosophy, St. Jerome, biblical wisdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the dangers of over-relying on logic and "dialectic" in matters of faith, following a section likely quoting a Church Father regarding the behavior of professional debaters.</meta>

->**ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**<-

...they respond or offer a premise, or they receive, assume, confirm, and conclude; they call those people "contentious" who think they should argue as they please—not with reason, but with spite <note>original: "stomachus" - literally "stomach," but used here to mean irritable temper or resentment.</note>. If, therefore, those whose very craft is contention do this, what should a Christian do, if not flee from contentions entirely? These are his words. Therefore, if we wish to truly know God, we must set aside all turbulent reasoning, all sophistic argumentation, and all dialectical <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions through logical systems.</note> inquiry. For reasoning and inquiry suggest a certain desperation and a lack of trust. Faith, however, must be firm and tranquil; for this reason Ambrose says in his book *On the Trinity*: <margin>Ambrose in the book *On the Trinity*</margin> "Remove arguments where faith is sought; even in their own schools <note>original: "gymnasijs," referring to places of training or debate.</note>, let dialectic now be silent; belief is given to fishermen <note>A reference to the Apostles, whom the Church Fathers often contrasted with sophisticated Greek philosophers.</note>, not to dialecticians." <margin>The same to Gratian *On Faith*</margin> The same author says to Gratian in *On Faith*: <margin>Urban, and found in Distinction 37</margin> And Pope Urban, writing to Charles, says: "It did not please God to save His people through dialectic." For the Kingdom of God is in the simplicity of faith, not in the contention of speech. For there is no greater plague to the soul than reasoning, altercation, and disputation regarding divine things—which overturns reason, perverts the intellect, and casts down faith. <margin>Colossians 2; 1 Corinthians 1 and 2; 2 Timothy 2; Titus 3; James 3</margin> Therefore Paul commands that such things be avoided above all, and James calls this wisdom "earthly, sensual <note>original: "animale," meaning belonging to the lower soul or animal nature rather than the spirit.</note>, and demonic." From this comes errors, from this doubts, from this lies, from this heresies; and from this the first sin in the human race arose. Moreover, the inventor of this so pestilential faculty is the devil—that first cunning and ruinous sophist—who proposed trivial questions, invented disputations, and as if...

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s critique of human knowledge, moving from a general distrust of the sciences to a theological argument centered on the Fall of Man. This specific passage uses the story of Eve and the Serpent to illustrate how intellectual debate and "reasoning" can lead to spiritual ruin.</meta>

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

[The serpent] established a certain school. Not content that he had destroyed himself, he discovered a craft by which he might also destroy others, so that he might increase and propagate his own evil. Therefore, not permitting man to stand in simple faith, he wished to propose a question regarding the commands of God. Judging this to be the most effective engine for overthrowing man, like a <term>sophist</term><note>A "sophist" in this context refers to someone who uses clever but false arguments to deceive others. Agrippa is comparing the devil to the academic debaters of his own time.</note>, 

<margin>Genesis 3</margin>

he first approaches Eve and challenges her to a contest of questioning and reason, asking: "Why did God command you that you should not eat from every tree of paradise?" If Eve had not disputed with him, she would not have been deceived. But because she truly descended into an <term>altercation</term><note>A formal dispute or legalistic argument.</note> with the devil, she was deceived by a single false and sophistical reason. She did not only fall from faith in that moment, but she simultaneously lost her reason. From this point, she first began to interpret the words of God falsely.

From which she also committed a lie, as soon as she presumed to doubt and distrust the utterances of God. For she responded in this way regarding the fruits of the trees that are in paradise: "We may eat of the fruit, but concerning the fruit of the tree which is in the middle of paradise, God commanded us that we should not eat it and that we should not touch it, lest perhaps we die." In this, she falsely interpreted the command of God by saying "us" in the plural, whereas God commanded Adam alone in the singular before Eve was created. Furthermore, she added "neither should we touch it," <note>original: "ne tangeremus"</note> lying in both respects. Finally, she even expressed doubt when she added, "lest perhaps" <note>original: "ne forte." In the biblical text, God's warning was absolute ("you shall surely die"), but Eve's phrasing implies the possibility that they might not.</note>. You see how that crafty and diabolical debate, proposed through questions, deceived reason; and reason, in turn, overthrows faith. This is the fruit, this is the utility, and this is the end of sophistical disputations, which...

<summary>Agrippa argues that the fall of humanity began not with a simple act of eating, but with the devil's use of academic-style "sophistry" to make Eve question God's word. He claims that engaging in intellectual debate over faith leads to the corruption of both reason and belief.</summary>

<keywords>Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Fall of Man, Eve, Sophistry, Scholasticism, Genesis 3, Reason vs Faith, Theology, Deception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a critique of scholastic theology, contrasting it with the "ancient" theology of the early Church Fathers.</meta>

-># ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

At this time, certain more recent speculative theologians and lovers of display <note>original: "theosophistis ac philopompis." The author is using these terms pejoratively to describe scholars who prefer complex theories and outward prestige over simple faith.</note> practice their trade, tending toward total vanity. These are men who, relying on a poorly translated Aristotle and various commentaries, as well as **Peter Lombard**—whom they call the **Master of the Sentences** <term>Master of the Sentences</term> <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1096–1164) wrote the "Sentences," which was the standard theological textbook of the Middle Ages. Humanist scholars often complained it replaced the study of the Bible itself.</note>—worship these works as the very archetype of all theology. In doing so, they neglect the Gospel of Christ and the apostolic teachings, having looked only at those other sorts of books.

Relying then on their own sophistic insolence, they believe they can attempt, attack, unravel, and interpret anything. Rushing in with their follies, defilements, babblings, and quarrelsome disputes—a craft for which they now have tongues well-armed—they have rendered everything that is simple, sincere, and pure in faith and religion into something complex, dark, and filthy. They have confused, disturbed, and polluted all theology with their absurd alterations and futile wordiness. They have invented a theology that is neither divine nor even human, but rather some invention of their own; I do not call it theology, but a squalid, hateful, quibbling, and diabolical vanity—a rhapsody of human opinions and philosophical trifles.

In contrast, there is that true and **ancient theology** which emanated from the first holy and true Christians: primarily from Christ and the apostles. They were followed among the Greeks by **Dionysius** <note>Historically identified as Dionysius the Areopagite, a convert of St. Paul, though the writings mentioned here are now attributed to a 5th-century author known as Pseudo-Dionysius.</note>, whose most divine writings exist, though not all have survived. Likewise, the saintly **Origen**, a most accomplished theologian; of his nearly innumerable writings, very few remain because of the corruption of his rivals <note>Origen (c. 184–253) was one of the most prolific early Christian scholars, but many of his works were destroyed or suppressed in later centuries due to theological controversies.</note>. Likewise **Basil**, surnamed the Great, **Athanasius** of Alexan[dria]...

<meta>This page continues a list of authoritative Greek Church Fathers before transitioning to the Latin Fathers and a critique of modern scholastic theology.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

<note>original: "drinus episcopus"</note>... bishop of Alexandria, who disputed with such constancy against the **Arians**. <note>The Arians were followers of Arius, who challenged the orthodox view of the Trinity; Athanasius of Alexandria was his chief opponent.</note> **Cyril**, bishop of the same cathedral, who presided over the **Council of Ephesus**, and whose excellent commentaries on *John* are still extant. **Didymus**, nicknamed "the Blind," who wrote on the *Procession of the Holy Spirit*, a work which **Jerome** translated into the Latin language. **Eusebius of Caesarea**, who wrote the *Preparation for the Gospel*. <note>original: "praeparationem in euangelicam ueritatem"</note> **John**, nicknamed **Chrysostom** <note>original: "Chrysostomus"; the name means "Golden-mouthed" in Greek, referring to his legendary preaching skills.</note> because of his eloquence, was also of great use to us in history. Likewise, **Gregory Nazianzen**, and many others.

Furthermore, from the Latins: **Cyprian**, **Lactantius**, **Tertullian**, **Ambrose**, **Rufinus**, **Jerome**, **Augustine**, **Leo**, **Gregory**, **Bede**, **Anselm**, **Bernard**, **Cassian**, and those others whom those earlier times produced. These holy doctors—this theology, I say—they completely push aside, reject, and mock; yet without them, they can know nothing correctly, even in their own subjects.

The inventor of such a ruinous mastery was that ancient <term>sophist</term> <note>In this context, a "sophist" refers to someone who uses clever but fallacious reasoning to deceive.</note> serpent who deceived **Eve**. Imitating him, certain recent <term>theosophists</term> <note>Agrippa uses "theosophist" here as a derogatory term for scholastic theologians who prioritize intellectual hair-splitting over spiritual devotion.</note> have stood out in this century as the leaders, authors, and promoters of such a great disgrace, whom countless other men of the same kind miserably follow every day. From this has arisen that horrid and tangled forest, and that murky grove of disputes, in which they labor constantly with wretched toil and damnable zeal, with little fruit. They do not invite **Christ** with faith, hope, or charity; nor do they seek, search, or knock with prayers, fasts, and vigils, so that the door of divine knowledge might be opened to them...

->### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...[of the] sea, but like Titans <note>In Greek mythology, the Titans were giants who famously rebelled against the gods of Olympus. The author uses this to characterize the arrogance of his opponents.</note> warring against God <margin>Concerning</margin>
with Daedalic <note>original: "Dædalicis." Referring to Daedalus, the mythical Greek inventor; here it implies "overly-complex" or "convoluted" logic.</note> machines of sophistry <note>original: "sophismatum machinis." Clever but fallacious arguments.</note>, these enemies of the sacred scriptures
believe they can break them apart. From this, whatever has been said by a
philosopher or by some theologian that falls into their hands,
they do not resolve it according to right principles, but lead it
further away—not toward the primary sources from which it flowed, but
with their follies and silly distinctions <note>The author is criticizing the Scholastic method of "distinctiones," which he views as hair-splitting.</note> they tear it apart, scatter it,
and crush it into powder as if in a mortar, so that with all its
strength lost through extreme dilution, when placed before the light and the air,
it vanishes with the slightest breeze. From this comes that proverb
used among them, in which they say, "With a single breath, I shall dissolve
your argument." Rightly indeed! A truly and wisely
spoken thing. For among those debaters, there is nothing but
breath <note>original: "flatus," which can also mean "hot air" or "wind."</note> meeting breath, and dispersing it. Hence, when they have gone
a little way from their schools <note>original: "gymnasijs," referring to the universities or places of formal debate.</note>, they sit mute and brooding,
like dullards and lifeless logs; and like a dry fig tree, they
have nothing to say, nor fruit [to give]...

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa's critique of contemporary scholars, contrasting their derivative methods with the authentic wisdom of the ancient church fathers.</meta>

->HENRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...not so that they may teach others, but so that they themselves may hunt for the praise of their own memory and appear to have read many things. They do not consider that if one were to return to each original author what is rightfully theirs from these men's sayings or writings, nothing of their own would remain. Instead, wandering in that error and the poverty of their own natural talent—like some rustic sect <note>original: "rustica quædam secta"; likely referring to unrefined or unoriginal groups of thinkers who lack true philosophical depth</note>—since they know nothing themselves and cannot produce anything of their own, they consume all their efforts in excerpting and compiling. Content with these things, though they know nothing at all, they recklessly claim the name of wisdom for themselves by using the labors and examples of others. It is a wonder how much they please themselves by this plan, and how exceptionally learned they consider themselves to be. 

Those ancient theologians did not act in this way; they were men weighty in wisdom, venerable in authority, and holy in life, such as those we have mentioned below. In their writings, the citation of scripture is found to be so simple and so rare wherever something must be recorded—specifically those citations from the Old Testament, from the Gospels, and from the Apostles—and they boast of nothing from remote antiquity. These were men who truly placed their confidence in divine grace alone; they were the best of teachers, conscious of their own wisdom, fearing the judgment of no one, and truthful. They did not look "upon the face of men" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning they were not swayed by status or social pressure; they were impartial.</note> but bestowed gifts upon us from their own treasures. In this, they were imitating Christ, who—just like a good <term>head of a household</term> <note>original: "paterfamilias"; a Roman legal term for the male head of a family, used here in a biblical sense to describe one who provides for his dependents.</note>—brought forth new and old things from his storehouse <margin>Matthew 13</margin>. In all their words and deeds, they bore fruit among men: the fruit of true religion and faith, leading toward eternal salvation. 

But let us return to where we digressed. How much, therefore, in the knowledge of the divine...

<summary>Agrippa criticizes contemporary scholars for being mere compilers who lack original thought, contrasting them with the ancient theologians who relied on divine grace and authentic scripture rather than vanity.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of contemporary theological methods, contrasting the "new" discordant doctrines with the "ancient theology" associated with figures like Hermes Trismegistus.</meta>

**ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**

Those who sin, presuming they can know God through a rush of mental excitement <note>original Latin: *exhilirationis discursu*, referring to a frenzied or superficial intellectual movement</note>, reveal nearly countless divisions within their own traditions. For no question, however trivial, is proposed to them that they do not entangle in flawed arguments and Daedalean labyrinths; and they condemn one another like dogs, with rabid barking and biting, as their writings and volumes demonstrate more than enough. But if, according to Aristotle’s opinion, the nature of truth is such that it must be in harmony with itself on all sides, and if the harmony of opinions is a trace of the truth, it necessarily follows from the opposite that what is everywhere discordant cannot be true. Therefore, among these debaters and <term>modern theosophists</term><note>The author uses "theosophist" here to describe contemporary speculative theologians who claim divine wisdom but lack the clarity of the ancients.</note>, no truth can exist, nor even a single trace of truth. And it is inevitable that this unorganized and monstrous heap—which is nothing but a collection of human opinions about divine matters—will eventually collapse under its own weight. 

But alas, wretched ignorance still spreads widely throughout the world; no one with a pious mind seeks the knowledge of God; nearly all of us have professed ignorance through this "new theology," new teachers, and new doctrine. Nothing is ancient, nothing is holy, nothing is truly religious; and what is worse, if there are any who dedicate themselves to this <term>ancient theology</term><note>Likely referring to the *Prisca Theologia*, a Renaissance concept that a single, true theology exists which threads through all ancient religions.</note> and religion, they are called insane, ignorant, irreligious, and sometimes even heretics. And as <term>Hermes</term><note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage believed to be the author of the *Hermetic Corpus*.</note> says in the *Poimandres*, book 9, <margin>Poimandres 9. Asclepius 9.</margin> they are hated, even placed in capital danger, afflicted with insults, and often deprived of their lives. Nevertheless, to return to the first sources and the pure water...

-> **H 3** <-

<summary>The author critiques contemporary theologians for their constant bickering and "new" doctrines, arguing that truth must be harmonious. He advocates for a return to "ancient theology" despite the persecution faced by those who follow the Hermetic path.</summary>

He is the <term>Angel of Great Counsel<note>original: "magni consilij angelus" — a title for Christ derived from the Septuagint (Greek) translation of Isaiah 9:6, emphasizing His role as the messenger of God's plan.</note>, who truly illuminates our minds with light. ## CHAPTER SIX

**S**ince, therefore, the nations <note>In this context, "nations" or "gentiles" refers to non-Jewish, non-Christian peoples who rely on natural reason.</note> now recognize God as the first cause and the producer of all things from the very condition of created things, that condition itself shows who established it, the handiwork itself points to who made it, and the world makes manifest who arranged it. The Jews also—first from the tradition of the <term>first-formed man<note>original: "protoplasti" — referring to Adam, the first human created by God.</note> and subsequently from Moses and the Prophets—accepted this same God as the maker of heaven and earth, and knew Him through the ministry of the Law. We, however, have received perfect knowledge in the Gospel from the apostles: that He is the same God above all gods, and His name is above every name. His Word, which is by nature invisible, was made flesh as a visible and tangible man, and humbled Himself even unto death—even death on a cross. We have learned that those who believe in Him will be like Him—incorruptible and <term>impassible<note>original: "impassibiles" — a theological term meaning incapable of suffering, decay, or being affected by external sensations; the state of the resurrected body.</note>—and will receive the kingdom of heaven. For this reason, every man who is ignorant of God is without excuse, and cursed is he who, knowing Him, does not worship Him. For it is impossible, as the Apostle says, for those who have once been illu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("illuminati"), likely referring to Hebrews 6:4 regarding those who have been "enlightened."</note>

<summary>This page outlines three stages of divine revelation: the Gentiles' recognition of God through nature, the Jews' knowledge through the Law and Prophets, and the Christians' "perfect knowledge" through the Incarnation of Jesus Christ.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of God, beginning with the conclusion of a scriptural quotation from Hebrews 6:4-6 and transitioning into a formal confession of the Trinity and the dual nature of Christ.</meta>

->### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a Latin translation of Hebrews 6:4-6.</note>...[illumi]nated and have tasted the heavenly gift, and were made partakers of the Holy Spirit, and have tasted the good word of God and the powers of the world to come, and have fallen away, to be renewed again to repentance. We, therefore, warned by the testimony <note>original: "dictamine," suggesting the "dictation" or instruction provided by the natural order</note> of creatures, and taught by the announcement of the Prophets, and informed by the preaching of the Apostles, dare to say: That there is one only true God, uncreated, immense, eternal, omnipotent: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; three persons indeed, co-eternal and co-equal to one another, yet one <term>essence</term> <note>the fundamental "whatness" or being of God</note> and <term>substance</term>, and a nature entirely <term>simple</term> <note>In historical theology, "simple" means not composed of different parts; God is unified and indivisible</note>; so that we may thus confess one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity, neither confusing the persons, nor separating the substance. 

For the Father from eternity begat the Son, and gave His substance to Him, and yet nevertheless retained it; the Son also, by being born, received the substance of the Father, yet did not assume the Father’s own person, nor did the Father transfer His person into the Son; for they are both of one and the same substance, but of different persons. 

This Son also, although He is co-eternal with the Father, begotten from the substance of the Father before the ages <note>meaning before time itself began</note>, nonetheless was born in time <note>original: "in seculo," literally "in the age"</note> from the substance of the Virgin, and His name was called Jesus, who is the Christ: perfect God, perfect man, subsisting of a rational soul and human flesh, to whom nothing human was lacking except for sin. He is one person, two natures; begotten as God before the ages without a mother, and born in time as man without a father from the Virgin, who remained uncorrupted <note>a reference to the doctrine of the perpetual virginity</note> before and after the birth.

# CONCERNING THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

suffered on the cross and died, but on the cross he restored life, and by death he destroyed death; <note>The Latin *resolvit* suggests "unloosed" or "dissolved," implying that Christ’s death broke the binding power of mortality.</note> he was buried, and he descended to the underworld, but he led back the souls of the fathers <note>This refers to the *Limbus Patrum* (Limbo of the Fathers), the theological belief that the righteous souls who died before Christ's resurrection were waiting in the underworld for his arrival.</note> from the underworld, and he rose again on the third day by his own power, and ascended into the heavens, and sent the Holy Spirit, the Comforter, <note>original: *paracletum*. **I** can scarcely express <note>The original text begins with a decorative initial "V," illustrating a bearded man, used here for the Latin "Vix" (scarcely).</note> (most distinguished Prelate) with what great joy your letter has filled me. In it, you indicated to me the goodwill and generosity of the Royal Majesty <note>Likely referring to the French King, Francis I, whom Agrippa served for a time.</note>; furthermore, you have bound me to you with such great devotion, having done so much for me with that same Royal Majesty, and promising to do even greater things in the future. And so, as I was considering by what evidence of my studies I might show some gratitude of my heart toward you for such a great favor, and while looking through the works kept hidden among my "paper furniture" <note>original: "chartacea mea suppellectile," a playful metaphor Agrippa uses for his library and unpublished manuscripts</note>, there suddenly presented itself a certain speech—or should I call it a letter—with which I once decided to respond to the persistent requests of certain friends. In it, I sought to discourage them from the study of **Pagan Theology** <term>Gentile Theology: The study of non-Christian classical Greek and Roman philosophy and mythology</term> and turn them toward **Gospel doctrine**. Rereading this, I revised it with a few notes, judging it a worthy subject to present to you during this brief span of time, in which I do not have the opportunity to write more extensive works, nor for you, who by your office...

<meta>This page continues Agrippa's dedicatory address, shifting from personal greetings to the specific purpose of the enclosed text.</meta>

## Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa

...at the Royal Majesty <note>Latin: *R.M.*, likely abbreviating *Regiam Maiestatem*</note>, you are now occupied with other studies and cares, and leisure is available to read more extensively. I therefore thought it best to send a small book on a useful and profitable matter, rather than a massive volume on cheaper and more trivial concerns. 

Accept then, under these happy auspices, our literary gift—however small it may be (though your virtue deserves much greater things). Judge it not by the slimness of the volume, but by the usefulness and truth of its teaching, and by the respect of my devotion to you. I have chosen to address this **Dissuasion** <note>original: *Dehortationem*; a formal argument intended to discourage a specific course of action, here likely referring to the study of pagan philosophy</note> to you so that you might act as a patron for its message. I hope you will persuade your **Most Christian King** <note>A traditional title for the King of France</note> that, in keeping with the excellence of his name and his duty to this most Christian kingdom, he should command and protect the free teaching of Christ’s word. 

It must be cleansed of all pagan, foreign, and corrupted doctrine, and set free from the traps of the sophists <note>In this context, "sophists" refers to university scholars who used complex, often circular logic rather than simple faith</note>. By doing so, he will prove himself a King most worthy and pious of his Most Christian title. 

Farewell most happily, and continue with the same love you have shown me—

<summary>Agrippa presents a small but significant book to a royal official, urging him to influence the King to purge Christian teaching of pagan influences and scholarly "traps."</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s "Dehortatio," where he addresses friends who have asked him to lecture on Hermetic philosophy. There is no previous page for continuity as this is the title and opening address.</meta>

# **HEINRICH**
# **CORNELIUS AGRIPPA’S DISSUASION**
from Pagan Theology, once delivered to certain
friends as a speech.

You have now often entreated me to explain to you, during this time of leisure, the book of Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "ter maximi Mercurij"; literally "Thrice-Greatest Mercury," the legendary Hellenistic figure credited with ancient secret wisdom.</note> concerning the wisdom and power of God (which I lectured upon publicly several years ago in the famous university of Pavia <note>The Latin "Tyanensi" likely refers to the University of Pavia (Ticinum), where Agrippa famously lectured on Hermes in 1515.</note>), and from that same book to reveal to you the light of the Poimandres <note>original: "Pimandri"; the "Shepherd of Men," a central text in the Hermetic tradition representing the Divine Mind.</note> (that is, of the Divine Mind). However, since I perceived that this request would not be useful for you, and judged the intention itself to be incorrect, I refused. Nevertheless, so that you might fulfill your vows and reach the limit of your desire, I offered to explain the Epistles of Paul to you. But now, having rejected those, you persist yet again, singing the same old song. I am greatly amazed and stunned—and indeed angry—that you, who have been baptized in the Gospel of Christ <margin>A rebuke</margin> and who perform the duties of the priesthood in the Church of Christ, would seek knowledge of God from pagans after holy baptism and after the sacred <term>chrism: a consecrated oil used in baptism and other sacraments to signify the gift of the Holy Spirit</term>, as if the Gospel were incomplete. To think that sinning against the Holy Spirit might be forgiven! For whoever thinks that the knowledge of God is to be obtained from anywhere other than the Gospel truly attacks the Holy Spirit <margin>Matthew 12</margin> <margin>2 Kings 1</margin> <note>Agrippa cites Matthew 12:31 regarding the unforgivable sin, and 2 Kings 1 (labeled 4 Kings in the Latin Vulgate) regarding the sin of seeking guidance from false gods like Baal-Zebub.</note>; this is a blasphemy that is forgiven neither in this age nor in the one to come.

<summary>In this opening address, Agrippa rebukes his friends—who are priests—for wanting him to lecture on the pagan Hermetic texts instead of the Christian Epistles of Paul, arguing that seeking divine knowledge outside the Gospel is a sin against the Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Hermes Trismegistus, Poimandres, Pagan Theology, Gospel of Christ, Holy Spirit, Pavia, Priesthood</keywords>

<meta>This page appears to be the opening of a discourse or letter by Henry Cornelius Agrippa. It uses a biblical precedent to warn against seeking forbidden knowledge or "pagan" philosophy (Hermeticism) over Christian doctrine.</meta>

### Henry Cornelius Agrippa

Have you not read about that great Ahaziah, King of Samaria <note>Ahaziah was a king of Israel mentioned in the Bible (2 Kings 1) who reigned in the 9th century BCE.</note>, who, while lying sick and dying in his bed, sent messengers to consult Baal-zebub, the god of Ekron <note>Ekron was one of the five ancient city-states of the Philistines. Baal-zebub is often translated as "Lord of the Flies."</note>, regarding his life and health? The prophet of the Lord, Elijah the Tishbite, met them after being warned by an angel and said to them: "Is there no God in Israel, that you go to consult Baal-zebub, the god of Ekron? Therefore, the Lord says to the king: You shall not come down from the bed upon which you have climbed, but you shall surely die."

Could I not deter you with this example, or at least warn you by saying: are there no teachers in the Church who can instruct you abundantly and properly concerning God, that you must go to consult Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurium Trismegistum." A legendary figure associated with the "Hermetic" writings, which Agrippa elsewhere studied deeply, though here he frames him as a "philosopher of demons."</note>, that philosopher of the demons of Egypt? Do you not know that God is your father, who created you, and the Church is truly your mother, who gave you new life in baptism? 

Why then, like runaway sons, do you beg for the nourishment of wisdom from sources other than your parents? You, who once renounced Satan in baptism, why do you rebel against God for Satan’s sake again? And you, who were bought into freedom through the blood of Christ, why do you expose yourselves again as prey to the enemies of faith and truth? Watch and take care, lest while you seek power and the wisdom of God from somewhere other than God himself, you commit a wicked and inexpiable crime against that very power and wisdom. For this, you may finally be abandoned to a depraved mind and lose your life along with your salvation. For if the evil demon once steals the words of salvation from your memory, even if you wish to be saved, you will not <note>The Latin text cuts off here at "non po," likely "non poteritis" or "non potestis" (you will not be able).</note>

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa uses the biblical story of King Ahaziah to warn his readers against seeking spiritual wisdom from Hermetic or "pagan" sources instead of the Christian Church. He frames the pursuit of non-Christian philosophy as a betrayal of one's baptism and a dangerous path toward losing one's salvation.</summary>

<keywords>Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Ahaziah, Elijah the Tishbite, Baal-zebub, Hermes Trismegistus, baptism, spiritual warning, occultism vs. Christianity</keywords>

->EXHORTATION TO THE GENTILES ON THEOLOGY<-

...[you have sought], for you have lost the way through which one arrives at salvation. Therefore the Apostle warns you to beware lest anyone <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> <note>While the margin cites 1 Corinthians 2, this is a famous paraphrase of Colossians 2:8: "See to it that no one takes you captive through philosophy and empty deceit."</note> despoil you through philosophy. If therefore a desire for divine things holds you, it is not necessary to extract these from the pagans, you who have set before you in the open the testimony concerning God and the Gospel of truth, knowing that the scriptures are indeed perfect, being given by the Word of God and His Spirit. But <margin>Objection</margin> you say here that many things are found in the books of the pagans which agree with Gospel doctrine. Furthermore, [you argue] that these matters are treated by them with greater elegance of words, harmony of sentences, and sweetness of speech, and that these also provide you with a great argument for believing our faith and apply a spur <note>A "spur" or "calcar" refers to an incentive or motivation to increase one's devotion.</note> [to your belief]. You are completely mistaken <margin>Response</margin> if you think you can imitate the principles of the Christian life from the writings of philosophers and be raised to virtue; beware lest the philosophy of the pagans has such power over you that Christian reason and doctrine are held by you in contempt, even though many of their ideas agree with ours. But what madness is this, what enchantment, that the Gospel doctrine pleases you more [when found] in Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus.</note>, Plato, Plotinus, Aemilius <note>Likely Amelius Gentilianus, a student of Plotinus.</note>, Iamblichus, Proclus, and other philosophers of this sort <note>original: "huius farinae," literally "of this flour/grain."</note>, than in the Apostles, in the Evangelists, and in Christ Himself. Are you not now like those who, distrusting the words and miracles of Christ, believed the voices of demons and of the possessed <margin>Luke 4. Mark 1 & 5.</margin>, having more faith in the demons confessing Christ as the Son of God than in Christ Himself? Do you likewise doubt to believe those things from Christ God, which He Himself...

<summary>This page argues that Christians should rely solely on the Scriptures rather than seeking spiritual truths in pagan philosophy. The author counters the contemporary trend of valuing Neoplatonic thinkers like Plotinus and Proclus for their eloquence, comparing those who prefer philosophical versions of the Gospel to those who believed demons over Christ.</summary>

...taught about himself, unless these things are confirmed by pagan <term>ethnic</term> <note>original: "ethnicis"; Agrippa uses this to mean non-Christian, specifically Greco-Roman, philosophies</note> disciplines; it is as if they grant so much to the philosophers, as though those men had discovered more by themselves than the Apostles had learned from Christ, who is God. Let them conquer rather than deceive, while they boast of the divine ark with vain voices, as if wisdom were located not in the heart, but on the tongue.

<margin>Luke 16</margin> Is this not that wisdom of the philosophers of which Christ says, "for the children of this world are wiser in their generation than the children of light"? Does this wisdom please you more than the "crude and unlearned" <note>original: "crassula & idiotica"; in this context, "idiotica" refers to the simple, non-academic language of the common people rather than a lack of intelligence</note> dogmas of the Evangelists and Apostles? But this is not enough, for such understanding leads to death, and its doctrine to destruction; whoever applies himself to it shall descend to the underworld, and whoever withdraws from it shall be saved. 

<margin>Proverbs 2 and 9</margin> This [philosophy] is that strange and foreign woman, foolish and clamorous, full of allurements, who softens her words, offering stolen waters and hidden bread; who, having left the guide of her youth, has forgotten the covenant of her God. Her house sinks down toward death, and her paths lead to the underworld where the giants are, and her guests are in the depths of hell. 

Are you ignorant of how, in the Gospel, Abraham responded to the rich man buried in hell? When the rich man asked for a messenger to be sent to his brothers, Abraham told him that whoever did not wish to descend into that place should listen to Moses and the prophets.

<margin>Objection</margin> But far be it, you might say, that we should give the victory of faith to pagan dogmas, or receive them as the Word of God; rather, we treat them as the opinions and decrees of philosophers and learned men, so that we might compare them with the divine scriptures and adorn the latter by means of the former.

He rejects the idea that pagan philosophy should be used to "adorn" or explain the Holy Scriptures.</summary>

->**ON KNOWING GOD**<-

...where you think you see most clearly, it is there that you are most blinded to the light of truth itself, just as Christ spoke of having come into the world, so that those who do not see might see, and those who see might become blind <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 9:39</note>. Do you not err in your blindness if you believe that you are decorating the sacred scriptures through these means, when by them they are rather defiled and polluted?
But how can you judge that you are able to compare these things with those, <margin>2 Corinthians 6</margin>
when there is no agreement between Christ and Belial <note>original: "belial"; a term used to personify wickedness or the devil</note>, light and darkness, or the faithful and the unfaithful? Are you not like a sick man who invites both a physician and poisoners alike for his treatment? While the poisoners can certainly do harm but cannot cure, the physician refuses to mix his medicines with their poisons, lest either destruction be attributed to the good remedies, or health be attributed to the harmful ones. Do
you not yet understand that while you wish to join these two together, this very thing is the subversion of your salvation? Granted, Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus: A legendary Hellenistic figure whose writings on philosophy and magic were highly influential during the Renaissance</note> is a weighty author; you have also considered Plato and Plotinus <note>Plotinus: The founder of Neoplatonism, whose philosophical system influenced many early Christian thinkers</note>...

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the vanity of human knowledge, specifically contrasting pagan philosophy with Christian scripture. The text likely follows a discussion on the limitations of ancient "wise men" or sibyls.</meta>

-># HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...wise men <note>original: "sophi"; referring to the ancient Greek sages or philosophers</note> are said to have spoken, it might indeed be of some value for refuting the empty claims of the pagans, yet it is not enough to justify embracing their authority. For there is as great a distance between the authority of the Prophets and the sacrilegious curiosity of those who commit errors as there is between the preaching of angels and the confession of demons regarding the coming of Christ <note>Agrippa suggests that even if pagans or demons speak the truth about Christ, their source and intent remain untrustworthy compared to divine revelation</note>. 

But if much weight must be given to the authority of the most serious men, to whom, I ask, do you think more should be deferred? To Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with hermetic philosophy and magic</note>, Plato, Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher</note>, and similar philosophers? These men, relying on their own devised opinions and with darkened intellects, did not perceive the highest secrets <term>archana: hidden mysteries or secret knowledge</term> of God and nature. They delivered teachings concerning God and divine matters that are neither supported by reason nor suggested by the Holy Spirit, but are mere delusions <note>original: "deliramenta"</note>, unworthy even of sensible men. 

Or should you defer to Moses, David, Solomon, the prophets, the Evangelists, and the Apostles? These men were filled with the divine spirit and proved their teachings through every kind of holiness, through oracles, miracles, the risking of their very lives, and the testimony of their shed blood. 

Wherefore, if you set these aside and run back to the philosophers, laboring to learn from pagan masters of error those things which you ought to receive from Christ and the Holy Spirit, will you not be insane and impious at the same time? If, therefore, you are now Christians, learn the writings of Christ. For he himself prescribed this mark for his own: <margin>**John 14**</margin> **"He who loves me,"** he says, **"hears my words."** From him, therefore, it must be learned what should be understood about God; for the righteousness of God is not found in the traditions of philosophers, but in the Gospel...

<summary>Agrippa argues that Christian scripture and the testimony of the Apostles are infinitely superior to the "delusions" of pagan philosophers like Plato and Hermes Trismegistus. He warns that seeking divine truth in secular philosophy rather than the Gospel is both foolish and impious.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Philosophy vs. Theology, Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Plotinus, Gospel, Biblical Authority, Paganism, Christian Doctrine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the source of divine knowledge, transitioning from a discussion of the "Word" to a critique of using classical pagan philosophy as a substitute for scripture.</meta>

-># ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

In Him <note>The "Word" or Christ, as the only true mediator of divine knowledge.</note> you have not known God; do not hope to know Him in others at all, for no one else can explain the things of God the Father except His own Word. For who else has known the mind of the Lord, or who has been made His counselor? <note>A reference to Romans 11:34 and Isaiah 40:13.</note> But now you take refuge in examples and <margin>Objection.</margin> say: "Will it not be permitted for us to imitate the holy Fathers—Jerome, Augustine, Cyprian, Hilary, Lactantius, Eusebius, Victorinus, and many others—whose books are full of the flowers of the pagans <note>original: "gentilium flosculis"; referring to the rhetorical ornaments and wisdom of non-Christian Greeks and Romans.</note> and the opinions of philosophers, and overflow with them everywhere?" And this is the single cause of evils (as Seneca says) <margin>Response.</margin> <margin>Seneca's [words].</margin> to live by examples, and not to be guided by reason, but to be led away by imitation. I know Jerome and Augustine, and I am familiar with the other doctors <note>The "Doctors of the Church," authoritative ancient theologians.</note> of the Church; but who are you, who desire to imitate them in those matters for which they themselves felt no small regret for the effort spent? Indeed, they all agree on this: that this pagan literature is the plague of religion and of the law of Christ. Learn, therefore, from their warnings to avoid these things; do not imitate the earlier error of those who wandered, but rather the later repentance of those who became wiser with age. But if examples have a place with you, let this one also have a place: Jerome, because he was reading Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator.</note> and Plato too greedily, was struck by an angel; while many saints were striking him, he was at last barely released upon these words of an oath: *Lord, if I ever possess secular books, if I read them, I have denied you.* <margin>Jerome, in the letter to Eustochium.</margin> <note>Jerome famously recounted a vision where he was hauled before the judgment seat of God and accused: "You are a Ciceronian, not a Christian."</note> Nor do I truly forbid us from imitating those saints, but I do not praise your proposing to yourselves that which is the least and lowest in them; indeed, I even urge...

<summary>The author argues that true knowledge of God comes only through His Word, dismissing the excuse that Church Fathers used pagan philosophy by pointing out that they later regretted doing so. He cites Jerome’s famous vision of being punished for reading Cicero as a warning against secular learning in a religious context.</summary>

<margin>Matthew 16.</margin>
