...you, and I command you to strive for that which is first and highest among them; indeed, follow those very first and highest ones with a firm step and a clear conscience—those, I say, who saw and knew God himself, Jesus Christ, in this light. They were taught all truth, not from flesh and blood <note>original: *ex carne & sanguine*; a biblical idiom meaning human intuition or physical nature</note>, but from the Father who is in heaven. They were caught up into the heavens and saw divine secrets <term>secrets: *archana*, or arcana, referring to mysteries revealed only to the initiated or the holy</term> which no man is permitted to speak. They heard what existed from the beginning, they saw it, and they touched it with their hands, and the Word of Life was made manifest to them. These are the ones who will provide you with certain and irrefutable knowledge of God.

But if the "fornicating love" <note>Agrippa uses this provocative metaphor to describe an obsessive or illicit attraction to secular curiosity over spiritual truth</note> of human curiosity is so deeply rooted in your souls that what I have already said does not deter you from the profane teachings of the gentiles, I would at least offer you this advice, which Jerome discusses regarding the captive woman: namely, that her hair must be trimmed and her nails shaved. By this, he means that whatever there was of idolatrous pleasure or error should be removed from this "illicit union" <note>Jerome famously argued that pagan wisdom could be used by Christians only if it was "purified," just as the Law of Moses required a captive woman to be shaved before marriage</note> so that you might father legitimate children for the God of Hosts.

Just as the prophet Hosea fathered a son by a prostitute, who was called the "Seed of God," if you are able to do this—if you purify the literature of the heathens and translate it for Christian learning, and if you seize the rich spoils from the Egyptians <note>A common medieval and Renaissance metaphor for taking useful philosophical truths from "infidel" sources, based on the Israelites taking gold from Egypt during the Exodus</note> as if from unjust possessors, whether openly or by stealth, and enrich the Church of God with their wealth—then I no longer strongly discourage it. In that case, I would even advise you to study heathen literature. However, I fear you may attempt this in vain; therefore, I command you to follow Augustine’s counsel: namely, that Holy Scripture should come first, so that foreign opinions do not take hold of a weak mind.

<summary>Agrippa argues that while the direct revelation of the Apostles is the only certain knowledge, secular or "pagan" philosophy may be studied if it is "purified" and subordinate to the Bible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the preparation of the mind for theological study, beginning mid-sentence with the justification for maintaining intellectual purity.</meta>

->## EXHORTATION TO THE THEOLOGICAL COMMUNITY<-

For to reach a truthful knowledge of divine matters, a pure mind is needed, one not infected by wicked opinions. For if the mind is preoccupied by certain incorrect opinions, then through them—under the guise of some sort of knowledge—the error of ignorance becomes more deeply rooted within it than it can be easily pulled out. It then happens that a person believes they know what they actually do not, and they defend what is actually an error or heresy as being true and <term>universal</term> <note>original: "catholicum". In this context, it refers to the established, orthodox faith of the Church.</note>. 

Therefore, in your theological studies, nothing should be more important to you than the Gospel doctrine; this is the path you must enter, <margin>2 Thessalonians 2</margin> even if you do not understand all the mysteries to the letter. <margin>To Titus 3</margin> Use the commentaries of those accepted in the church, specifically the ancient holy fathers <note>The "Church Fathers," early influential Christian theologians like Augustine, Jerome, or Ambrose.</note>; afterward, consult the <term>modern scholars</term> <note>original: "neotericos", referring to more recent or contemporary Renaissance theologians.</note>. However, completely avoid prying inquiries about God and divine things, as well as arguments over questions and the battles of disputation, according to the Apostle's <note>The "Apostle" usually refers to St. Paul in this type of literature.</note> command. 

But once your mind has been strengthened and fortified by the Church's doctrine, then you will walk safely by its light through all the shadows of error. Then you will be able to freely penetrate all types of academic disciplines. Then, like Ulysses <note>The Latin name for the Greek hero Odysseus, known for his long journey home and his ability to survive many dangers through wisdom.</note>, if you enter the cave of the Cyclops or descend to the underworld, you will return unharmed. If you are carried toward the Lotus-eaters or the <term>quicksands</term> <note>original: "Syrtes", referring to the dangerous sandbanks off the coast of North Africa famous in classical literature.</note>, you will return safe. If you drink Circe’s cup, you will not be transformed <note>In Greek mythology, the sorceress Circe turned men into pigs with a magical potion.</note>. If you sail past Scylla, you will not be in danger. If you hear the Sirens, you will not fall asleep, but you will be "judging all things," as the Apostle says, <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> "and judged by no one." Then, the teachings of the <term>pagans</term> <note>original: "ethnicorum", referring here to non-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers and poets.</note> will be of the greatest help to you, and their...

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's primary exhortation regarding the study of theology and introduces a short poetic tribute to the work "On the Threefold Way of Knowing God."</meta>

By this means, you will then easily ascend to the pinnacle of theology. But if you enter upon this journey in a reversed order, you will ruin yourselves and your studies. Like those whose eyes have been gouged out, you will grope about blindly; unable to see the straight path, you will wander here and there in uncertainty, stumbling against every stone. Once you have turned aside from the right way, you will not be able to be set right again, even with the most <term>keen-nosed hound</term> <note>original: *nasutissimo cane*. In the Renaissance, a "keen-nosed dog" was a common metaphor for a person of sharp discernment or a sagacious guide.</note> as your leader.

Therefore, believe me now—though I am your junior—while you still have the time to learn. If you do not believe me now, you will believe me too late in the end, and you will mourn your error.

->THE END<-

## ON THE LITTLE BOOK CONCERNING THE THREEFOLD
### way of knowing God. <note>original: *De triplici ratione cognoscendi Deum*. This refers to a specific theological framework involving knowing God through nature, through the law, and through grace/faith.</note>

If you, a mortal soul, seek to know the powerful and true 
God, in whose image you are said to be made:
Read this work, which Cornelius <note>The author is likely Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa von Nettesheim (1486–1535), a famed German polymath whose works blended theology, philosophy, and occultism.</note> has built with marvelous skill,
whose noble genius also forged this work.
He wisely urges you to know yourself first, and then adds
the method by which you may know God.

<summary>The author warns students that studying theology out of order leads to irreversible confusion and concludes with a poem praising "Cornelius" for his work on the three methods of knowing God.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
To the **REVEREND FATHER IN CHRIST** and lord, doctor of sacred theology, Lord Dietrich, Bishop of Cyrene <note>Dietrich Wichwael (died 1519) was a Dominican theologian and the titular Bishop of Cyrene, serving as a suffragan (assistant) bishop in Cologne.</note>, and most worthy administrator of the Archbishopric of Cologne in sacred duties by suffrage, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa commends himself.

**[D]** Concerning original sin and the first transgression of humankind, various opinions are offered among the most learned and excellent interpreters of sacred letters, both ancient and more recent. When I frequently turned these over in my mind, they seemed to me to have written nothing but certain obscure and ambiguous things. Therefore, I have not despised their opinions, but I have set them aside to bring forward another new and personal opinion of my own (offered merely as a matter for debate, and perhaps not false). It would be a crime if anyone else had thought the same before me; but if such a person exists, I do him no injury if I call this opinion mine, insofar as I have rescued it from the shadows of neglect by no other means than the diligence of my own intellect and the examination of the Scriptures.

I have therefore published a brief and concise declamation of my opinion, which I dedicate to your reverend and sincere paternity, so that by your authority it might emerge more secure from the coming storm of attackers. For I know how great a storm of logic-chopping <term>syllogisms: formal arguments moving from general premises to a specific conclusion</term> might fall upon that ruin among certain "theophists" <note>original: *theophistis*. Agrippa's derisive term for scholastic theologians who prioritize rigid logic over spiritual simplicity.</note>—among those, I say, with whom there is no room for simplicity, but only ambitious contention reigns. Neither will the subject matter fall, nor will it be provoked by their logic, unless it is defended by your assistance against such Egyptian gnats <margin>Psalm 143 <note>Likely referring to Psalm 144:11 in modern numbering: "Deliver me... from the hand of strange children, whose mouth speaketh vanity."</note></margin> and frogs <note>original: *sciniphes, ratasq;*. The OCR reads *ratas* (certain/fixed), but contextually this is almost certainly a typo for *ranas* (frogs), referring to the plagues of Egypt as metaphors for annoying critics.</note>. Farewell.

I 3 <note>This is a "signature mark," a guide for the bookbinder to ensure the pages were folded and bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>In this dedicatory letter to the Bishop of Cyrene, Agrippa introduces his treatise on original sin, claiming to offer a fresh perspective derived from his own study of Scripture rather than existing scholastic traditions.</summary>

**HENRY**
**CORNELIUS AGRIPPA ON**
**original sin: a declamation on a**
**debatable opinion.**

[Decorative initial 'I' in a square frame]
<margin>Genesis 1</margin> In the beginning of the book of Genesis, it is written that God said: "Let us make man in our image and likeness." And God made man; in his own image and likeness he perfected him. We believe this was spoken concerning the <term>inner man</term> <note>Agrippa follows a Platonic and Hermetic tradition that distinguishes the "inner man" (the soul) from the physical vessel.</note>, which is the rational soul, created in the likeness of the divine Trinity as well as its Unity. Once this inner man was thus divinely established, God bestowed upon it a suitable garment and dwelling—namely, the human body—as the following <margin>Genesis 2</margin> text shows when it says: "God formed man from the mud of the earth." And again the Scripture adds: "and He breathed into his face the breath of life," which is a certain power by which the intellectual soul and the earthly body are joined and united into one whole man. Thus, man was composed of a divine soul, an earthly body, and a celestial spirit; he was immortal in his body until, by his own will and contempt for the divine command, he fell into a mortal body. We shall explain in order the nature of this fall, and how it affected both the inner and the outer man. <margin>Genesis 2</margin> Therefore, after man was first created (as we have already said), God placed him in the middle of paradise. Paradise, for us, [signifies] a garden of delights—

<summary>In this opening, Agrippa defines humanity as a composite of a divine soul, a celestial spirit, and an earthly body, arguing that the biblical 'image of God' refers specifically to the rational soul. He introduces his thesis that man's mortality was a result of a voluntary 'fall' caused by contempt for God's command.</summary>

There were in paradise various fruit-bearing trees <margin>Genesis 2 & 3</margin>, and above the others were the tree of life and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, which was in the midst of paradise. Whence Solomon says: "Wisdom is a tree of life to those who have laid hold of her." <margin>Proverbs 3[:18]</margin>

The tree of the knowledge of good and evil is the <term>affection of the flesh<note>The author uses this to mean carnal desires or an emotional attachment to the physical world over the spiritual.</note></term> and the "prudence" of earthly things. Its fruit is the neglect or ignorance of God, from which comes the death of both body and soul. This was...

...belongs to faith, and faith must not succumb to reason, but must firmly hope in God. And for that reason, God condemned Adam <margin>Genesis 3</margin> because he believed the woman; nor indeed should we argue about divine matters, but firmly believe and hope. Regarding those things that were created, however, it is permitted for us to philosophize, debate, and draw conclusions through the service of reason, but not to place our faith and hope in them. 

From this we can now further see how foolish they are who pursue the "revolutions" of worldly loves, births, <note>The author is listing technical branches of astrology: "questions" (horary astrology) and "elections" (choosing auspicious times for events).</note> questions, elections, and any similar artifice of images, intentions, divinations, and the like. They believe in such vanities and hope for future things from them, placing their confidence in the powers of created things—whether natural, celestial, or spiritual—such as <term>Physics: At this time, the study of the natural world and its inherent properties.</term> Physics, Alchemy, <term>Mathematics: Often used in this period to refer specifically to astrology or the calculation of horoscopes.</term> Mathematics, Astrology, and Magic. 

Although these may be sciences proven through the discourse of reason or varied sensory experience, they very often deceive the minds of men, pervert the judgment of conscience, and cast the soul headlong. Therefore, the faith and hope of mankind should not be defiled by them. This is what Isaiah himself reproaches the Babylonians for, saying: <margin>Isaiah 47</margin> "Your wisdom and your knowledge have deceived you." And: "Let the astrologers of the heavens save you" <note>original: "astrologi coelorum"</note> (for this is a more accurate reading than "the diviners of the heaven") "from whom things to come shall happen to you; you have grown weary in the multitude of your counsels." 

Thus much has been said concerning the tree of life, or the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, and the fall of the inner man; it seemed right to explain these first according to their moral sense. It remains for us to show what "original sin" effectively was in the thing itself, on account of which man himself [was driven] from paradise...

driven out, he was made subject to the death of the body and the soul, and was made liable to all other evils. Many theologians, both ancient and modern, have held many opinions on this matter, and each one of them <margin>Romans 14</margin> <note>Agrippa refers to Romans 14:5: "Let every man be fully persuaded in his own mind."</note> is fully convinced in his own mind. Under this condition, it shall now be permitted to opine freely; and even if (for I am a man of immature age, and of lesser talent and learning) I do not abound in my own mind with as many testimonies of the scriptures as the matter itself requires, perhaps certain more learned men will follow me, to whom this opinion of ours will not be displeasing. I trust I can be helped in no small measure by these men, insofar as they might make our opinion stronger by their more valid reasons and arguments.

<margin>Agrippa’s Opinion</margin> Our opinion itself is this: that **original sin was nothing other than the carnal copulation of man and woman.** We have previously suggested this in a certain dialogue of ours concerning man, using a few conjectures from the sacred scriptures; but now we shall show that same thing a little more at length (yet still succinctly). <margin>Genesis 3</margin> For Moses himself, explaining the fall of our first parents through a parable <term>parable: a symbolic story intended to teach a spiritual lesson</term> friendly to those who seek deeper contemplation, teaches that a certain wicked demon—cunning, crafty, and envious of human salvation—clung to man. And let us say that this very genital organ of man’s carnal lust—a creeping member, a snakelike member, a slippery member, and twisted with various windings—is what tempted and deceived Eve. To which the name and likeness of a serpent rightly applies. Therefore, we believe that no other demon tempted Eve than that one of whom Job says: *Whose strength is in his loins and power is in his navel* <margin>Job 40</margin> <note>original: "Cuius uirtus est i lūbis, & potestas i umbilico." This is that Satan who persuaded her regarding the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, concerning which there was a true prohibition. It was not that God envied man the knowledge of good, but He forbade the knowledge of evil; He did not forbid man to be wise <note>The Latin "sapere" means both "to be wise" and "to taste," a pun the author uses to link the eating of the fruit with the acquisition of forbidden wisdom.</note>, but to be wiser than is proper. 

For that knowledge of evil—that knowledge of superfluity—is none other than the knowledge of the flesh, the knowledge of immodesty. Paul teaches us this: *not to be wiser than is proper* <margin>Romans 12</margin>, but *to be wise unto chastity* <note>original: "sapere ad castitatē." The author is arguing for a specific translation of Romans 12:3.</note> (not "unto sobriety," as Jerome testifies is wrongly translated in the Latin manuscripts) <margin>Jerome, *Against Jovinian*</margin>. Therefore, that which exists outside of chastity, in the pollution of the flesh, is this evil and superfluous knowledge. Once man had fed upon this carnal knowledge, the Almighty was indignant, and *He regretted that He had made man*. 

God regretted it <margin>Genesis 6</margin>, because *the virgin Israel has fallen* <margin>Amos 5</margin>, and there is none to raise her up; He regretted it because, although God can do all things, He cannot restore a virgin after her fall <note>This is a provocative theological claim, suggesting that even divine omnipotence does not "undo" the physical or ontological loss of virginity once it has occurred.</note>. 

Thus, as the divine light and the spirit of chastity became indignant and withdrew, the knowledge of the flesh grew strong <margin>Genesis 3</margin>, in which *the eyes of both were opened*, and they knew evil. What then did they know? What did they see? They truly knew their own evil, their own sin, their own death on account of their immodesty; and they saw that they were naked, and they felt shame, and

They hid themselves from the face of the Lord, and they hid the members in which they sinned through immodesty; because of the <term>lust they committed</term><note>The author refers to the "libido" or sexual desire that, in this theological context, was believed to have triggered the Fall.</note> they blushed, and having covered their private parts, they were ashamed of their nakedness. <margin>Romans 6</margin> Paul addresses them with these words: "What fruit therefore did you have in those things of which you are now ashamed? For the end of them is death." <note>original: "quem ergo fructum habuistis in illis, in quibus usque nunc erubescitis, nam finis illorum mors est"</note> For human nature, without any doubt, is ashamed of this lust even to this day. It is not without shame even between husbands and wives; nor is that which is granted to us with a wife—due to the frailty of our flesh—so just or lawful that we are not ashamed to do it openly. 

But if anyone, following the <term>dogma of Diogenes the Cynic</term><note>Diogenes of Sinope (c. 412–323 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who famously advocated for living according to nature, which included performing all bodily functions, including sexual acts, in public.</note>, dared to attempt it openly, even with his wife, I believe he would be overwhelmed not so much by the weapons of those throwing stones as by the filth of dung and the spit of those who despise him. 

Therefore, because of this shameful lust of the flesh, man was cast out from the paradise of God and handed over to death, because flesh and blood (as Paul says in <margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin>) cannot possess the kingdom of God, nor can corruption possess incorruption. For the corruption of the flesh cannot possess the rewards of modesty. 

Now, <term>corruption</term><note>In this context, "corruption" refers to the physical decay and moral degradation that the author associates with sexual intercourse following the Fall.</note> is sexual intercourse and pertains to intercourse, the reward of which is death; but the reward of chastity is eternal life, the kingdom of God, and a dwelling in paradise. For we know that Adam and Eve inhabited paradise as long as they remained virgins, but they were cast out from paradise once their marriage was consummated through the flesh. Just as virginity granted them paradise:

# ON ORIGINAL SIN

...thus are marriages on earth. In paradise, there were marriages of virginity, to be consummated in the word and spirit of God; but outside of paradise, there are marriages of the flesh, consummated in corruptible intercourse, through which all who are born are corrupted. According to the scripture: *Whatever a man sows, that he will also reap.* He who sows <margin>Galatians 6</margin> in his flesh, shall from the flesh reap corruption; but he who sows in the spirit of chastity <note>The original Latin *spiritu castitatis* emphasizes a life of purity or celibacy as a spiritual harvest.</note> shall reap eternal life. But man, while he was in the honor of virginity, did not wish to understand how <margin>Psalm 48</margin> to live chastely, but through the lust of the flesh he became like the beasts who have no reason, and through death he was made equal to them. God said to men: *Do not become* <margin>Psalm 31</margin> *like the horse and the mule in which there is no understanding.* But they themselves (according to the word of the Prophet) became horses raging after women; <margin>Jeremiah 5</margin> each one neighed after his neighbor's wife. Of these the canonical epistle speaks, saying that <margin>2 Peter 2</margin> they walk after the flesh in the desires of pollution, and like irrational beasts they are prone to the belly and to lust; therefore, in their own corruption, they shall be destroyed. 

And so, while man began to procreate after the manner of brutes out of the pleasure of the flesh, he lost the spiritual seed of <term>intuitive intelligence</term> <note>original: "intuitiuæq; intelligentiæ." This refers to a direct, angelic-like understanding of God that was supposedly lost at the Fall.</note>, for God said: *My spirit shall not remain* <margin>Genesis 6</margin> *with man because he is flesh.* His eyes were opened instead in the darkness of the senses, from which reason now gathers all the material for its knowledge; and his eyes grew dim to the light of pure intellect. This is just as what usually happens to those coupling in the dark: once the carnal seed is emitted, all things they previously saw clearly become blurred, and when they return to the bright light, their vision is dazzled and overwhelmed.

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the nature of Original Sin, specifically how it is transmitted through the physical act of procreation and the "infection" of the flesh.</meta>

->### BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

Therefore, that which is born of the flesh was corruptible and mortal flesh, participating in nothing of the intellect from the parent, but only in carnal sensibility. For only the sensible and carnal soul comes from the progenitor; the intellect, created by God, is simultaneously dampened and suffocated within this soul through the infected flesh. Hence, it is necessary for all who are born of the flesh, and who reproduce through the lineage of the flesh and the senses, to contract original guilt and corruption. <margin>Poimandres, chapter 1</margin> This is what Hermes also shows in the *Poimandres* <note>original: "Pimandro." The *Poimandres* is the first and most famous book of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a collection of mystical and philosophical texts attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus.</note> in a sufficiently long discourse: namely, that man has fallen from divine contemplation into the nature of the generation of the world's mortal and irrational creatures. From this, man himself produced a form lacking reason, perceiving the evils of death through his senses. 

But perhaps these things that have already been said will seem to have been explained by too violently twisting the scriptures to support our opinion, unless we bring forth further testimonies through which it may be seen more clearly <margin>Genesis 3</margin> that the mingling of the flesh was displeasing to God. This is first shown now by the curse of childbirth, so that what the mother conceived in the pleasure of the flesh, she brings forth in pain. It is as if it were said: <margin>Psalm 7</margin> "Behold, he has conceived injustice, labored in pain, and brought forth iniquity; he opened a pit and dug it out, and fell into the ditch he made; let his pain be turned upon his own head, and let his iniquity descend upon his own crown." <note>original: "Ecce concæpit iniustitiā..." Agrippa is quoting Psalm 7:14-16, applying the imagery of a hunter falling into his own trap to the act of carnal procreation.</note> Added to this is a certain indignation of nature, since a virgin is not violated, nor does a woman give birth, without blood. <margin>Genesis 4</margin> But let us see what followed. Adam fathered two sons, the first being Cain...

<meta>This page continues a theological discussion on the nature of the first humans' progeny following the Fall, focusing on the symbolic and moral differences between the lineages of Cain and Seth.</meta>

**ON ORIGINAL SIN.**

...who was conceived through transgression, a son of sin, a son of blood, envious, a murderer, despairing of the mercy of God; for he could not be born good, who had been conceived with such great evil. <note>The author is referring to Cain, the firstborn of Adam and Eve, here characterized as the embodiment of inherited sin.</note> But the other son was born, Abel, conceived after the repentance for the transgression, a son of mercy, and righteous. This man, a virgin, is killed—the first sacrifice acceptable to God for the atonement of the flesh—who washed away the filth of carnal marriage with the blood of martyrdom. <note>Medieval theology often viewed Abel’s death as a "baptism of blood" and a prefiguration of Christ's sacrifice.</note>

A third son follows him, Seth, <margin>Genesis 4, 5</margin> whose name signifies resurrection; <note>The author derives this from a traditional etymology of the Hebrew name Seth (Sheth), meaning "appointed" or "substituted," here interpreted as a "rising again" of the righteous line.</note> for from him Jesus Christ was to be born, through whom we were eventually to rise again from such a great fall. <margin>Genesis 5</margin> Regarding this, it should be noted that while it is read that Seth fathered many sons and daughters, Scripture expresses no female offspring by name. In the genealogies of Cain, however—the sons of wrath—the last female born is named Naamah. <margin>Genesis 4</margin> <note>original: "Noema"</note>

By this, it is elegantly signified that the generation of Cain (which is described through the carnal coupling of males and females) is a generation of sin leading to death, from which are born sinners destined to perish in the flood; but the generation of Seth is a generation of chastity and resurrection to life, who fathered Enos, which is interpreted as "true man," <note>original: "homo uerus"</note> for then the name of the Lord began to be invoked, and this generation is saved from the flood of waters and from the death of sinners. 

The generation of Cain, therefore, from Adam ends in the number of eleven persons under the flood, which is the number of sin and transgression; for the number ten is the number of the law and of righteousness, in which Noah the righteous was born, the tenth from Adam through Seth. Thus, the number eleven, by departing from ten, signifies the transgression of the law and the commandments, in which number the generation of Cain perished. <note>In medieval numerology, 10 represented perfection or the Decalogue (Ten Commandments). 11 was seen as "transgressing" that perfection, hence its association with sin.</note>

<summary>This page provides a moral and numerological contrast between the lineages of Cain and Seth, associating Cain with sin and the number eleven, while Seth and Noah are linked to righteousness and the number ten.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s discourse on the theological history of women, specifically focusing on the origins of sin and the symbolic meaning of names and numbers in the book of Genesis.</meta>

->### OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA###<-

From this it should be noted that, just as the first transgression and the "flood of sins" began through a woman <note>Agrippa refers here to Eve and the Fall of Man</note>, so too was the flood of waters completed in a woman—specifically in Naamah, the final daughter of Lamech the bigamist. Her name, Naamah, signifies "pleasure." <note>original: "Noema uoluptatē sonat." The author is using a traditional Hebrew etymology where Naamah (No'ema) is linked to "pleasantness" or "delight."</note> From this, we can reasonably conjecture that the first transgression and the subsequent flood of sins arose from carnal pleasure.

Furthermore, the lineage of Cain, descending from Adam, is washed away in the eighth generation. This number <note>the number eight</note> exceeds the number seven <term>septenary|The number seven</term> in the same way that eleven exceeds ten. Holy teachers call the number seven the "number of virginity" because it is the first number that neither generates nor is generated. <note>In Renaissance numerology, 7 was considered "virgin" because it cannot be produced by multiplying any two numbers between 1 and 10, and when doubled (14), it exceeds the "perfect" limit of 10.</note> It cannot be divided into two equal parts so as to be "born" from a repeated number, nor does it "give birth" to any other number when doubled that remains within the limits of ten—which is well known to be the primary limit of all numbers.

Thus, we can easily discern from this that the sin of our first parent was the violation of virginity through carnal pleasure. This was first punished by the Great Flood, yet it could not be entirely abolished. For those who were saved in the flood—Noah and his sons—were themselves born through the flesh and they went on to father children. Nevertheless, as long as Noah and his sons inhabited the Ark of Salvation (which serves as a figure for the Earthly Paradise and a <term>Type|A symbolic foreshadowing or representation</term> of the Church), they were separated from their wives. From this, it is revealed to us that in Paradise, our first parents were forbidden from carnal union. This is the same principle that Paul, the herald of the Christian religion, proclaims to the heavens: "It is good," he says, "for a man not to touch a woman." <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>

<summary>Agrippa argues that the biblical Great Flood was a punishment for carnal pleasure, using numerology and the etymology of the name 'Naamah' to link sin to the physical body. He suggests that sexual abstinence was the original law in Paradise, as symbolized by the separation of the sexes on Noah's Ark.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Naamah, numerology, virginity, Noah's Ark, original sin, symbolism of numbers, 1 Corinthians 7, Lamech</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a theological discussion on the nature of marriage and celibacy in relation to the doctrine of original sin. It draws heavily on Pauline epistles to argue for the spiritual superiority of virginity over the "unclean" nature of physical generation.</meta>

**ON ORIGINAL SIN.**

"Do not touch," he does not say "do not have," but "do not touch," because there is danger in the touch—not the touch of the hands, but of the serpent, <margin>Job 40</margin> <note>The author references Job 40:16, where the strength of the beast (Behemoth) is in its loins; medieval commentators often interpreted this as a symbol for sexual temptation or the devil's power over human lust.</note> whose strength is in the loins and power is in the navel. 

Regarding those who marry because of the "tribulation of the flesh," <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin> he speaks by way of concession: "if a virgin marries, she does not sin." He does not say that she does well, but grants that she does not sin; for it is one thing not to sin, and another to do well. Concerning which he says elsewhere, "he who keeps his virginity does well." And if someone should say, "he who joins his virgin in marriage does well," the Apostle responds, "he who does not join her does better." <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>

Finally, he says to those who are married: "It remains that those who have wives should be as if they had none." And elsewhere, explaining that passage: "The two shall be in one flesh," he adds, "this <margin>Ephesians 5</margin> is a great sacrament." <note>original: 'sacramentum'; here used in the sense of a 'mystery' or a sacred sign, referring to the union of Christ and the Church.</note> But I say this regarding Christ and the church: for how can that which is of the flesh—which is unclean—be a "great sacrament"? For compared to the purity of the body <margin>1 Peter 1</margin> of Christ, of which we have been made members, in whom we are <margin>James 1</margin> reborn, not from corruptible intercourse, but through the word of the living and enduring God: who (as James says in his epistle) by his own will brought us forth by the word of truth. 

Therefore, compared to the purity of this sacrament—of this union, I say, and of this spiritual generation—all sexual intercourse is unclean. Thus the Apostle says: "Husbands, love your wives, <margin>Ephesians 5</margin> as Christ loved the church," namely in sanctification and chastity. Concerning which it is read elsewhere: "Let each one know how to <margin>1 Thessalonians 4</margin> possess his own vessel <note>original: 'vas suum'; a biblical idiom likely referring to one's own body or perhaps one's spouse.</note> in sanctification and honor, not in uncleanness and the passion of the desire of the flesh, even as the Gen—

<margin>Leviticus 12</margin> Instead, they were given the commandment of **purification after childbirth**.

For in a state of virginity our first parents were immortal; however, through the violation <note>original: "praevaricationem," implying a transgression or turning aside from a duty</note> of virginity, they brought forth death for both their nature and their descendants. In virginity, they enjoyed the knowledge of the Good itself; but once virginity was lost, with their intellect growing dark and the eyes of their flesh being opened, they knew evil. Now from these mysteries of the scriptures, one can be sufficiently persuaded: that the coupling of the flesh was the <term>original sin</term> <note>Agrippa's controversial view was that the "Fall of Man" was specifically the act of sexual intercourse, rather than just eating a physical fruit.</note>, and many will easily agree with me in this opinion because of what has already been said, unless a single <term>scruple</term> <note>A small doubt or theological objection.</note> stands in their way. 

For they will say, if the mixing of the flesh was forbidden, how were the first <margin>Genesis 1</margin> parents to fulfill that blessing: "Increase and multiply"? How were humans to be born so that the seats of the angels who fell with Lucifer might be filled? <note>A common medieval and Renaissance belief that God created humanity to replace the one-third of the angels who fell from Heaven.</note> This is indeed a difficult and thorny objection, the solution to which is all the more difficult the more deeply this mystery is investigated. This is that mystery of which <margin>John 16</margin> Christ says: "I still have many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now," and elsewhere says of the same: "Not everyone can <margin>Matthew 19, Luke 8</margin> grasp this word." # HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA'S
## Surest **Antidotes** <term>Antidotes</term> <note>In the 16th century, an "antidote" was not just a cure for poison, but any preventative medicine or regimen used to protect the body from the "corrupt air" of the plague.</note> against the plague, addressed to Lord Theodoric of Cyrene, administrator by suffrage in sacred matters of the **Archprelacy of Cologne**. <note>Theodoric (Dietrich) held a high-ranking administrative position within the Archbishopric of Cologne, one of the most powerful ecclesiastical territories in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>

-> [A decorative woodcut initial **Q** containing a bird-like figure] <-

**W**hat regimen for living should be observed during times of pestilence, physicians have handed down in many canons; from these, I will give you the principal and most necessary points in a compendium, but a very brief one. 

Therefore, the first necessity is to abstain from all excess of external heat—such as from the sun, from fire, from [too many] clothes, and from hot air—and also from internal heat, such as from the excessive eating of salt, spices, medicinal herbs, and foods of intense heat, and from potent and "smoky" wine. <note>original: "uino potenti & fumoſo" — "Smoky" wines were thought to send heavy vapors to the brain, disturbing the body's humors.</note> Likewise, one must abstain from heavy exercise—and especially from sweating and sexual intercourse—and from the **passions of the mind**. <term>Passions of the mind</term> <note>Renaissance medicine emphasized that strong emotions like fear, anger, or sadness could physically weaken the heart and make a person more susceptible to infection.</note> One must also abstain from all over-filling with food and drink, as well as from hunger and thirst, from **constipation of the bowels**, <note>original: "ſtipticitate uentris"</note> and from long sleep, especially during the day. 

Which foods are to be chosen above others, and which are to be avoided, I pass over here; for even if it were easy to describe these things, it is not easy for everyone to observe them. I only advise this: that in the seasoning of foods... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

especially indeed <note>original: "pisaum reantium"; likely a corrupted OCR transcription of "praesentium," meaning "those remedies currently at hand."</note>, among other things do not omit **tormentil**, **dittany**, **gentian**, **zedoary**, **sandalwood**, and **roses**; also use vinegar, especially rose vinegar, and the juice of the citron fruit, or of oranges, or of lemons, and the juice of sorrel, as well as <term>verjuice</term><note>The tart juice of unripened grapes, used to balance the "heat" of disease.</note>, and wine made from pomegranate seeds and barberries. For all things with a sour and acidic taste resist the pestilential poison, for they cool, dry, comfort, and preserve the body. If, however, the chest is harmed by these, they should be tempered with sugar; if the stomach is affected, use a small portion of pepper, for pepper itself resists the delay of <term>putrefactions</term><note>In early modern medicine, the plague was often thought to be caused by the "rotting" or putrefaction of humors within the body.</note>, aids digestion, and strengthens the stomach. Provided it is not reduced to too fine a powder, but is instead coarsely crushed, it does not harm the liver. Furthermore, among seasonings, onion, radish, <term>sweet flag</term><note>original: "acorea"; a wetland plant used as a stimulant and for stomach ailments.</note> and capers are suitable, for they also oppose the poison.

You shall purge your dwelling and your clothes with a clear flame, especially one raised from the wood of juniper, pine, cypress, or ash. You shall also temper the air with sprinklings of rose water mixed with vinegar, herbs, and flowers, and even with fragrant fumigations; you shall also treat your garments, undershirts, and linens with the same. Furthermore, you should wash your hands with these several times a day, and do not leave your home or go out into public unless you are holding a root of dittany or zedoary, or some other aromatic confection in your mouth, and carrying a <term>pomander</term><note>original: "pomo odorifero"; literally an "odoriferous apple," a ball of ambergris, musk, and herbs held to the nose to ward off miasma.</note> in your hands to be smelled. # AGAINST THE PLAGUE.

Take half a pound of walnuts, cleaned of their inner skins; a quarter pound of the leaves of domestic rue <note>Garden rue (*Ruta graveolens*) was a staple of medieval anti-plague recipes, believed to ward off "miasma" or foul air.</note>; and one and a half drams <note>A dram is a historical unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams.</note> of ground sea salt. Let all these be thoroughly mixed together in a mortar with clarified honey, or with syrup of citron juice or currants. A quantity of this, the size of a hazelnut, should be administered on an empty stomach with a little vinegar and rose water. There are those who add other spices and roots to this <term>electuary</term> <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note>, though there is not much need for them.

There is also another effective medicine, which they call the **electuary of the egg**, the composition of which is as follows: A fresh egg is emptied of its white through a hole in the shell, leaving the yolk behind. It is then filled with powdered oriental saffron. After the hole is closed, it is roasted over a slow fire until it is reduced to blackness. It is then powdered in a mortar, and an equal weight of the powder of rocket seed (that is, white mustard) is added to it, incorporating them well through long grinding. A quantity of this electuary the size of a maple seed <note>original: "grani aceris"</note> is administered in the morning with a little wine and rose water.

Truly, later practitioners—not without successful experimentation—added to this composition white dittany and tormentil (two drams of each) and one dram of <term>nux vomica</term> <note>The seed of the Strychnine tree; used in very small doses in historical medicine, though highly toxic.</note>. Each of these is first powdered separately, then incorporated with the egg through long grinding. There are even those who still add the roots of pimpinella <note>Also known as burnet-saxifrage.</note>, zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger.</note>, angelica, and camphor. By adding <term>theriac</term> <note>A famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, often including opium and viper flesh.</note> in an amount equal to the weight of all the other ingredients, they create an electuary in the form of an opiate. This is administered, as we have already said, for protection, but also even...

->**HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

...presented for healing, up to one <term>drachm</term> <note>A unit of weight in the apothecaries' system, roughly equivalent to 3.9 grams or 1/8th of an ounce.</note> with white wine, if the disease has attacked with a cold chill, or with rose water or scabious water <note>Water distilled from the Scabiosa plant, traditionally used to treat "scabby" skin conditions and fevers.</note> if it attacks with heat. It is necessary, however, that it be administered within the twelfth hour from the onset of the illness if it is to be of any help. And these are the most useful, safe, and proven antidotes among all others that I have discovered for the present time, and which you may use healthfully, provided you do not neglect to also take the <term>Pills of Rufus</term> <note>original: "pillolis ruffi"; these were a famous ancient remedy attributed to Rufus of Ephesus, typically composed of aloe, myrrh, and saffron, and used as a preventative during epidemics.</note> once or twice a week. Take the weight of one <term>scruple</term> <note>A small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams.</note> at dawn, abstaining from any other medicine on that day. 

And note this: if the weather is intensely hot, then the aloes and myrrh, which are included in the composition of the said pills, should be diligently washed with rose water, and you may add as much of the purest camphor as is equal to a third part of the myrrh. But in cold times, and for those with a cold <term>complexion</term> <note>In the medical theory of the time, "complexion" referred to a person's unique balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile).</note>, one must abstain from these specific preparations; instead, those pills are to be taken without camphor and without the washing process. 

Furthermore, because these same pills sometimes open the bowels and loosen the system too much, it is useful to add a small portion of <term>Armenian bole</term> <note>A medicinal red clay from Armenia, used as an astringent to "bind" the bowels and counteract the laxative effect of the aloes.</note> to them, or to roll the pills in its powder. Because they also sometimes harm the stomach, this ill effect should be corrected with a modest mixture of choice <term>mastic</term> <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree, used to soothe the digestive tract.</note>. Beyond this, it is best to take old <term>theriac</term> <note>A complex "universal" antidote made of dozens of ingredients.</note> or <term>mithridatum</term> <note>A legendary semi-mythical remedy against poisons, similar to theriac.</note> at a weight of two or three scruples every ten days, taken with a draft of rose or sorrel water, while also observing the usual rules of the physicians. But if anyone feels themselves actually struck by the plague, if their age and strength permit, let them be <term>phlebotomized</term> <note>The practice of bloodletting, intended to remove "corrupt" blood from the body at the first sign of infection.</note> as soon as possible...

<meta>This page continues medical instructions for treating the plague, specifically detailing an onion-based remedy following the mention of bloodletting or chirurgical intervention.</meta>

-># AGAINST THE PLAGUE<-

From a suitable vein <note>referring to bloodletting from a specific part of the body to balance the "humors"</note>, and soon after let the following medicine be offered; and the sooner the better, even if bloodletting <term>phlebotomia: the surgical opening of a vein to withdraw blood</term> has not been performed beforehand—for instance, if a surgeon is not immediately present. Because this evil [the plague] grants no truce, it is therefore fitting to offer the following drink immediately, and if necessary, to repeat it often according to the patient's strength.

The potion is as follows: take six white onions, hollow them out at the top, and after removing the core, fill them with old theriac <term>theriata/theriac: a famous ancient "cure-all" paste made of dozens of ingredients, often including opium and viper flesh</term> mixed with a little powder of the roots of dittany <note>original: dictami; an herb believed to draw out toxins</note> and tormentil <note>original: tormentillæ</note>. Wrap them in damp paper and roast them under hot ashes until they are cooked through and soft. Then, set aside a portion of them; for once these are crushed into a poultice, you shall place them over the abscess <note>the "buboes" or swollen lymph nodes characteristic of the plague</note>. This is a very safe antidote, drawing out and destroying the poison. 

But to return to the drink: take the remaining cooked onions as mentioned, crush them with one ounce of lemon syrup <note>original: siropo acetositatis citri</note> and a little vinegar, and press the mixture through a cloth. Give three ounces of this warm strained liquid to the sick person. Let him be placed in bed, well-covered, abstaining from food and drink for six hours, and taking every care not to sleep. For the power of this cup is most effective in driving the poison from the vital organs toward their "cleansing channels" <term>emunctoria: parts of the body used to expel waste, like the armpits or groin where plague swellings often appeared</term> and evacuating it through sweat and other critical purgings.

If you mix into this drink a small portion of the "electuary of the egg" <note>a medicinal paste, likely the one mentioned on a previous page</note> described above, it will be wonderful. Finally, it must be noted that if the patient is plethoric <term>plectoricus/plethoric: having an excess of blood</term> or "cacochymic" <term>cacochimicus: having an imbalance of "bad humors" or bodily fluids</term>, that is to say, filled with bad humors...

It emphasizes speed of treatment and the importance of preventing the patient from sleeping while the medicine works to expel "poison."</summary>

->HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...or perhaps he has not been <term>bled</term> <note>original: phlebotomatus; the practice of bloodletting was a standard medical treatment to balance the bodily humors</note>, or it is not appropriate to reduce the blood, then laxative medicines must be added to that potion. For example, for those of more delicate constitutions, use half an ounce of *trifera persica* <note>a complex medicinal paste containing peach kernels, used as a gentle purgative</note> with one drachm of the <term>Electuary of Hamech</term> <note>a bittersweet purgative compound made from myrobalans, rhubarb, and colocynth</note>. For stronger individuals, however, let it be given without the *trifera*; instead, give it with the Hamech confection, an equal amount of the Electuary of Rose Juice, an equal amount of the <term>Indian Electuary</term> <note>original: electuarii indi; likely a preparation containing exotic spices and sugar</note>, and two drachms of *diafenicon* <note>a laxative electuary primarily made from dates</note>. In both cases, however, the potion should be served warm with scabious water, sorrel water, or rose water.

Regarding what remains of these matters, I pass over them, for these admit of both more time and further consultation. I judge, therefore, that I have done enough if I have described only those things which do not permit delay. 

But I wish to remind you further that the supreme and final remedy for this evil is the **"Adam-earth,"** <note>original: terram adamam; a reference to the "first matter" or the red clay from which the first human was created, often cited in alchemy as a universal medicine</note> namely the primary matter of our creation. For this alone is the unique substance that bestows a healing power than which there is none greater. Nor will its effect be small once it has been purified through the artifice of fire and restored to its simplicity by a suitable washing. 

But of these things, I shall speak at another time.

(With all due respect) <note>original Latin: "salva pace tua," a polite formula meaning "if you will allow me to say so."</note> it was quite clear. My dear Cornelius, I have thoroughly examined your opinion concerning that original flaw <term>original sin</term> <note>The author refers to *originale vitium*, the theological concept of the inherent "stain" or corruption inherited by humanity.</note> within us, searching through it with great care. Perhaps you expect some judgment from me on the matter; however, this subject carries immense weight. Nevertheless, it is the harmonious opinion of all those who study theology that the original stain cannot exist where there is no rational soul.

**L**etters from you (reverend father) I recently received in the city of Metz <note>Latin: Metensi; at this time, Agrippa was serving as the town advocate and orator in Metz</note>, together with letters from my mother, bringing me—alas—the all-too-sad news of my father's death. I grieve for this most vehemently. But I find one single solace for this pain, and no other: that one must yield to the divine ordinance. For I know that God grants to men things that, although they are not always pleasant and are very often even adverse, are nevertheless always useful, whether to be of benefit here in this life or in the heavenly homeland. For God acts according to what His nature dictates, namely according to His essence, which is all goodness; thus He works nothing but what is good and salutary—

...considered, yet (such is my human frailty) I grieve
vehemently, and I am troubled within myself. But your
most welcome letter—you who have been left to me as a surviving
second father—has tempered my grief in no small
measure. And so, I must now respond to this; for
you say that you rejoice greatly, and are moved by
immense pleasure, that I am still among the living, I who
some years ago was reported to have perished in the war.
Certainly, for several years now, by Imperial <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Emperor's forces.</note> command,
and by my own duty as a soldier, I followed the
Imperial and royal camps. I was present in many conflicts,
and not lazily; death preceded before my face,
and I followed as its servant. My right hand was
steeped in blood, my left divided the spoils; my belly
was sated with prey, and the steps of my feet were upon
the corpses of the slaughtered. I became unmindful of my own
honor, and was entirely defiled under a hellish <note>original: "tartarea," referring to Tartarus or the underworld.</note> shadow.
But the grace of God abounded over my iniquities, and
delivered me from the mouth of the lion <note>A biblical metaphor for escaping certain death or the devil, frequently used in the Psalms and the New Testament.</note>; that grace preserved
me unharmed from the swords and snares of so many enemies,
amidst hostile crowds and bloodthirsty people,
among whom no one is a victor unless he is a
murderer, and no one is defeated unless he is dead.
Therefore, I rejoice with you that I have finally returned
alive to Cologne <note>original: "Coloniam"</note>, and have turned the sad
and mournful news given to my parents and friends into
joy. And I confess that I owe immortal thanks also
to your love and benevolence toward me. Furthermore,
as you now congratulate me on my marriage, because I
have taken a wife who pleases me, from whom I hope for
progeny—I indeed give countless thanks to Almighty God, who [has given me] a wife...

...[God] joined to me, according to my own heart, a noble maiden, well-mannered, young, and beautiful, who lives so much in accordance with my own habits that not a single insulting word has ever passed between us. I would call myself most happy in this: that however matters turn, in prosperity and in adversity, she is always equally kind to me, affable, constant, possessing a most upright mind and sound counsel, and always remaining true to herself. <note>The author is likely describing Louisa Tixier, whom Cornelius Agrippa married in 1521. His description follows the Renaissance ideal of a "harmonious" marriage based on shared temperament.</note>

Furthermore, you praise me because, having cast aside secular matters as best I could, I have devoted myself to sacred letters <term>sacred letters: a common Renaissance term for the study of the Bible and theology</term>. You advise that, by offering chaste prayers to God, I should guard against diabolical, seductive machines and errors—by which I think you mean **dialectic** <note>original: "dialecticā"; the formal study of logic and argumentation</note> and **philosophy**. Indeed, you know in part how, after my investigation of logic and natural things, I furthermore surveyed the entire "host of the heavens" <note>An allusion to astrology and celestial magic</note> using those two great "wisdoms" of **Albert the Great** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher whose works on nature and the cosmos were highly influential.</note>, which he describes in his *Mirror* <note>original: "speculo"; referring to the *Speculum Astronomiae*, a 13th-century text often attributed to Albertus Magnus that discussed which astrological books were compatible with Christianity.</note>, a little work that is not altogether praised.

Finally (to confess the truth), after spending much time and labor, and with the loss of the goods of my soul, body, and fortune, I have gained nothing from all these things except sin; for indeed, all these things are not of faith. But by the grace of God, finally recognizing such great vanities of human sciences—and after studying both **laws** <note>Canon and Civil Law</note> and **medicine** (to satisfy the desire of my kin, who prefer me to be a "Doctor" rather than "learned") and having received the caps and rings in the scholastic fashion—I turned myself with all my zeal to sacred letters, however late. I embrace these entirely (though far removed from contentions and quarrelsome **syllogisms** <note>The rigid logical structures used in Medieval university debates, which many Humanists found tedious and dry.</note>), and I cannot express how much my soul is delighted and finds rest in them at the same time.

But lest I, still a novice in this infinite abyss of divinity and walking beyond the limit...

<meta>This page continues an intellectual exchange regarding the theological definition of original sin. The author is addressing a high-ranking Church official (a Bishop) to seek a formal judgment on his theories.</meta>

...lead my opinions, I appoint you—as a sacred prelate, an elder, and the most dear friend of all my studies and actions—to be my censor and judge. For I know that it belongs to the sacred order and office of Bishops to approve or condemn, declare, commend, or tolerate for debate new opinions concerning morals and faith. Therefore, by that authority and censorship, I have addressed to you our opinion on **original sin** <note>original: "de originali peccato"</note> to be evaluated, so that you might indicate how you agree or disagree with me. Rest assured that I would rather disagree with myself alongside you than agree with myself against your judgment. 

Yet I ask your pardon for imposing such a burden on you, since this matter, as you say, is of no small weight. But now, because I have once begun to speak, I beg you not to be offended; I respond to you again in this way. You say in your letter that the common opinion of theologians is that the stain of original sin cannot exist where the rational soul is not present. 

<margin>He responds to the objection.</margin>

I hold to this opinion. But since original sin was such that it cast down the whole man—namely, the being composed of a divine soul, a celestial spirit, and an elemental body—altogether and separately, in the whole and in each of its parts, from his original nobility, I would say that original sin was such a thing in which all and singular <term>powers of man</term><note>The author uses a traditional Aristotelian framework where the soul has different "powers": rational (reason), sensual (perception/desire), and vegetative (involuntary biological growth).</note>, the rational as well as the sensual, and the vegetative and the corporeal, truly concurred together in falling away from the given law in this actual deed. So be it, then, that original sin had its seat in the rational power, which, yielding to the senses and the flesh, consented to the fault; nevertheless, it was necessary that this consent, already corrupted, was also carried out into the physical action of the body. Thus, original...

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of sin, specifically moving from the theoretical or internal aspects of sin to its physical manifestation in the body, linking ancient idolatry to carnal lust.</meta>

...sin, an effect or some act of a depraved reason or will, but brought into bodily practice, where that act is already real—not feigned, not merely thought, and not imaginary. I find no other such sin besides the sin of the flesh, as the Apostle Paul says: <margin>1 Corinthians 6</margin> "Every sin whatever a man does is outside the body, but he who commits fornication <note>original: *fornicatur*; in this context, it refers to any sexual immorality or illicit physical union</note> sins even against his own body." But someone will say, <margin>Matthew 5</margin> even he who has lusted after a woman has already committed fornication with her; I say he has committed fornication with her in his mind, yet this effect of lustful desire in the reason and the image in the imagination is not the same as the reality of the thing in its actual existence.

Rather, let us now examine this also in our capacity for reason, and let us seek what kind of "fall" that was. The supreme citadel and unique act of reason is religion, which was clearly commanded from the beginning—namely, the true and unique spiritual worship of the one, only, and true God the Creator. However, when reason was depraved with the serpent as its teacher, soon all religion collapsed into nothing but the lust of the flesh. Men pursued this as if it were the highest happiness, and the most filthy <term>Priapus</term> <note>The Roman god of fertility and gardens, often depicted with an oversized, permanent erection; used here by the author as a symbol for the deification of sexual organs and lust</note>, the organ of primordial sin, was held as a God. To him they sacrificed; to him all the first temples were dedicated, and his most filthy images were the first of all to be built. Him those first "architects" of religions worshipped: the Chaldeans, Egyptians, Assyrians, Babylonians, Arabs, Scythians, Phoenicians, Ethiopians, and likewise all Asia, Africa, and Europe; nor was it lawful for any priest to be made who had not first been initiated into the rites of Priapus. This is that <term>Belphegor</term> <note>Also known as Baal-Peor, a Moabite deity mentioned in the Bible (Numbers 25), often associated by Renaissance scholars with licentious rites</note>, the oldest idol of all, which was also called <term>Chemosh</term> <note>The national god of the Moabites</note>, named after Ham <note>original: *Chamo*; Ham was the son of Noah, often vilified in early modern exegesis as the progenitor of "corrupt" nations</note> the son of Noah, who, according to the testimony of Berosus <note>Berosus was a Babylonian priest and historian; however, the author likely refers to the "Pseudo-Berosus" texts popular in the 16th century which claimed Ham was a practitioner of magic and illicit arts</note>, was therefore surnamed Esenna. This is—

--- Page 67 ---
The shameless, or ignominious propagator. <note>The author likely refers to Ham, the son of Noah, who in Renaissance theology was often blamed for reintroducing paganism and "forbidden knowledge" after the Great Flood.</note> For he was the first who restored this foul religion of depraved reason—received from those ancient giants before the flood—once again after the flood. From this source came those orgies, bacchanals, <term>priapalia</term> <note>Festivals or rites dedicated to Priapus, the Greek god of fertility, often characterized by lewdness.</note> and other most shameful rites; from this source Venus, Cupid, the private parts of Osiris, Pan, and Satyrs—and finally even the stinking goat, because it was lecherous in its lust—were accepted as gods.

Behold, you see now from the fall of rational power, that no sin can be more ancient than that of lust and fornication, and that human reason preferred the worship of lust to divine contemplation. Hence, while all and individual members of a human move only by the command of the rational soul’s will, lust has so constrained the genital member alone for its work—and sin has so enslaved it under its own command—that if lust is absent, or if it is not further aroused and risen, it cannot be moved to its work at all. <note>This reflects a common theological argument from St. Augustine: that because of the Fall, humans lost "voluntary" control over their bodies, specifically in regards to sexual arousal.</note>

Therefore, the act of lust itself seems to me to have been this primordial sin of human nature, and the corruption of all human nature, for which humans are ashamed even to this day, and they avoid the eyes of onlookers by blushing. And as <margin>Augustine</margin> Augustine says: A man more easily bears a crowd of onlookers when he is unjustly angry with another man, than the sight of even one person when he is lawfully joined with his wife. <note>original: "Magis fert homo spectantium multitudinem quando iniuste irascatur homini, quam vel unius conspectum quando iuste miscetur uxori." Reference to *The City of God*, Book 14, Chapter 18.</note> Regarding what remains of these matters, it stays stored deep in my mind <note>original: "alta mente repostum" — a phrase from Virgil’s Aeneid referring to deeply held grievances or thoughts.</note> until I speak with you.

It remains now to satisfy your request, which is that I send some preservative antidote against the plague. This matter is difficult, and not entirely safe, in which a more learned and skilled physician than I ought to be consulted. Nevertheless, I will by no means hide from you those things which I use in my own practice, if I first advise you of this one thing, which is the safest of all...

to be, to flee far away, and indeed to those places which the

☞ Whoever, therefore, shall neglect this most ancient and most sacred institution, being contemners of divine and human laws, shall be judged like dry and sterile trees for the perpetual fire. And therefore, under the Old Law, those who did not take a wife when they were of mature age were cast out from the temples; their offerings were rejected, and they were held incapable of receiving inheritances and legacies. They were deemed unworthy of divine and human aid, who—despising divine and human laws—refused to accept the help created for them by God and sanctified by human laws.

if the grace of God in them be made void, they mock the sacrament to which all honor, reverence, and liberty is due. It pertains to you, most illustrious Princess, that this execrable custom be first removed and uprooted from your domain, and finally from the entire most Christian kingdom of France. Indeed, you can serve God in no greater way, nor better benefit the Christian commonwealth, than by acting so that <margin>Augustine, Book 4</margin> good things are commanded and evils are banished: not only those which pertain to human society, but also those which pertain to <margin>Chrysostom</margin> divine religion.

[Pilcrow symbol] Therefore, whoever you are who wishes to take a wife, let love be the cause, not wealth; choose a wife, not a garment. Let the wife marry you, not the dowry. With this mind, call upon Almighty God, who alone gives a true wife. Seek also the consent of parents, showing them due obedience, with all greed, ambition, envy, and fear removed. By mature deliberation within yourself, with free consent and a fervent but rational and chaste love, so take a wife—commended to you forever by the hand of God—for your perpetual companionship, not for servitude. --- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory dedication of the work, following the author's return to his homeland. The author is finalizing his decision to present a long-held manuscript to a royal patroness.</meta>

...confident that the book would not lose its mistress. 

Now, therefore, having returned to this fatherland, I thought it proper to fulfill my pledge, and that I should no longer delay in offering that book to our princess, which is owed to her by agreement and vow by the most equitable right. And so that she may know that in the meantime I have neither forgotten her, nor ever deserted my devoted loyalty, nor has the wickedness of others mattered more to me than the constancy of my own mind, which favors her virtues and praises intensely. 

If now your wisdom does not disapprove of this plan of mine, I will see to it that this little book, along with many of my other works, comes out in public, even if I see how slight this matter is and how it is rendered with no elegance of speech. But I wish this little book—written once in my childhood, and now revised only hurriedly <note>original: "tumultuario," implying a rushed or disordered preparation</note> as you see in this copy—to be offered to its princess (as is the custom of speaking among those they call <term>Canonists</term> <note>Scholars or judges specializing in Canon Law, the internal religious law of the Church.</note>) "from now as if from then," <note>original: "ex nunc ut a tunc." A legal phrase meaning a current action is treated as if it had happened at an earlier date.</note> even at the risk of my reputation. In the meantime, being now older, I shall prepare more sublime and worthy things for her highness with a weightier and fuller argument. 

Nor indeed would I want the Princess herself to measure my talent by these trifles of my childhood; if she wishes to test it, it could be of use to her even in the greatest matters of both peace and war. Therefore, lest someone more arrogant or boastful of their learning, out of contempt for my mediocrity, should ungratefully despise, slander, bite, or tear apart this work of ours, I commend it to your <term>Magnanimity</term> <note>A formal address to a person of high rank and great spirit.</note> to be protected and defended, together with the splendor of female nobility and the glory of womanly excellence. 

And I hope that I will easily obtain pardon for this cause—that I have preferred women to men—because I wrote these things for such a great Princess, and published them with your greatness encouraging and protecting me. 

Farewell. 

From Antwerp. April 16, 1529. 
<note>original: "xvi. Kalendas Maij, An. M.D.XXIX." In the Roman calendar, the 16th day before the Kalends of May is April 16th.</note>

I shall await your judgment.

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication and introduction to Agrippa's famous 1509 treatise, "De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus" (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Feminine Sex). He is addressing Margaret of Austria.</meta>

# OF HENRICUS CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

While I myself hesitated between a strange fear of ingratitude and a similar dread of sacrilege, my shame was overcome, and I became bolder in writing. I feared I would appear even more arrogant if I remained silent, interpreting it as a good omen—as if the responsibility for this subject, which the assembly of scholars until now seems to have utterly neglected, had been left and decreed to me by the powers above. I shall therefore proclaim <note>The OCR "Annuā" is likely a misreading of the Latin "Enuntiābo."</note> the glory of woman and I shall not hide her honor. So far am I from being ashamed of the argument I have taken up, or thinking I should be blamed for preferring women to men, that I hardly believe I can be excused for having treated so sublime a subject in a style humbler than is fitting—unless I am protected by the shortage of time, the difficulty of the task, and the inherent justice of the cause. Furthermore, I have undertaken this work with no desire for flattery or seeking favor. Thus, my aim was not so much to decorate my words with rhetorical fictions and "dutiful lies" <note>A common Renaissance term for the flowery, often exaggerated praise used in courtly dedications.</note> but to demonstrate the matter itself through reason, authority, examples, and the testimonies of Sacred Scripture and **both laws** <term>Refers to "Utrumque Ius," the study of both Canon (Church) Law and Civil (Roman) Law.</term>.

But to you, most Serene Margaret <note>Margaret of Austria (1480–1530), daughter of Emperor Maximilian I and Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands. She was one of the most powerful and educated women of the era.</note>, whom—among the illustrious women of this age throughout the whole world—Apollo, Diana, the Day, Aurora, and Vulcan <note>Agrippa uses these five deities as symbols of various types of light and celestial brilliance to emphasize Margaret's fame.</note> have not shone upon another equal in nobility of lineage, excellence of virtue, and glory of deeds; to you I dedicate and devote this work of mine. I do this so that you (who have ascended to such a height of virtue that you have surpassed in your life and character all things praised about the female sex) might serve as a living example and most faithful witness. You are the honor and glory of your sex, shining more brilliantly than any sun...

<summary>In this dedication to Margaret of Austria, Agrippa introduces his treatise on the excellence of women, arguing that the subject has been unfairly neglected by scholars and promising to prove women's superiority using legal and religious authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Henricus Cornelius Agrippa, Margaret of Austria, feminism, Renaissance humanism, gender equality, Canon Law, Civil Law, Sacred Scripture</keywords>

...shine forth. Farewell, most happy one, who are at once the honor, ornament, and glory of the noblest women and princes, perfect in every way.

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA'S
# DECLAMATION ON THE NOBILITY AND PREEMINENCE OF THE FEMALE SEX.

[Decorative initial D] GOD, the best and greatest creator of all things, the father and the source of good for both sexes, most full in fruitfulness, created humankind in his own likeness; male <margin>Genesis 1</margin> and female he created them. Indeed, the distinction between these sexes consists only in the differing arrangement of the parts of the body, in which the function of procreation required a necessary diversity. Yet he granted the same and entirely identical <note>original: "indifferentem," meaning without difference in quality or nature</note> form of the soul to both the male and the female. Between these souls there is absolutely no distinction of sex. The woman herself has been allotted the same mind, reason, and speech as the man; she moves toward the same goal of blessedness <note>meaning eternal salvation or ultimate spiritual fulfillment</note>, where there will be no exception made for sex. For according to the truth of the Gospel: Those rising again <margin>Luke 20</margin> <margin>Mark 12</margin> in their own sex shall not perform the duties of sex, but the likeness of angels is promised to them <margin>Matthew 22</margin>. Therefore, based on the essence of the soul, there is no preeminence of nobility of one over the other between man and woman. Rather, both share an equal, innate liberty of dignity. However, regarding those things in a human being that exist beyond the divine essence of the soul, in those the illustrious lineage of women excels the harsh race of men almost infinitely, which then...

A 4

<summary>Agrippa begins his treatise by arguing that men and women possess identical souls and the same capacity for reason and salvation. He asserts that while biological differences exist for procreation, they do not create a spiritual or intellectual hierarchy.</summary>
