--- Page 77 ---
->**BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

Finally, this will be established as valid and firm when it—which is our objective—is demonstrated not with counterfeit or deceptive speeches, nor with the logical snares <note>The original Latin uses *tendiculis*, meaning "little snares" or traps used to catch birds, suggesting that sophists use logic to "trap" the mind.</note> by which many sophists <note>Professional teachers in the ancient world often criticized for using clever but fallacious arguments to win debates.</note> are accustomed to trap people, but with the support of the best authors, truthful histories of past events, and clear reasoning, as well as the testimonies of the Holy Scriptures and the decrees of both laws <note>In the Renaissance, "both laws" referred to Canon Law (the law of the Church) and Civil Law (Roman Law).</note>.

To begin, then, to enter the matter itself: Woman was made as much more excellent than man as she received a more excellent name than his. <margin>Genesis 3</margin> For "Adam" sounds like "earth" <note>original: *Adam terram sonat*; Agrippa refers to the Hebrew *adamah*, meaning ground or red earth</note>, while "Eve" is interpreted as "life" <note>original: *Eua aut̄ uita interpretatur*</note>. Just as life itself is more excellent than the earth, so woman is to be preferred over man. Nor should it be said that this is a weak argument—to judge the reality of things by their names. We know that the supreme Architect <note>The Latin *artificem* implies a master craftsman or creator.</note> of things and names knew the nature of things before He named them; since He could not be deceived, He fashioned names in such a way as to express the nature, property, and use of each thing. <margin>In the *Authentica* <note>A collection of legal decrees by Emperor Justinian.</note> concerning the descent of... column 3, at the beginning; in the *Institutes* on Donations; and in the *Digest* there is another.</margin> For such is the truth of ancient names—as the Roman laws also testify—that they are in harmony with the things they describe and signify them aptly. Therefore, an argument based on names carries great weight among both theologians and legal experts. As we read in the scriptures regarding Nabal: "According to his name he is a fool, and folly is with him" <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 25:25. The Hebrew name *Nabal* literally means "fool."</note>. <margin>To the Hebrews, chapter 1</margin> From this, Paul, in his letter to the Hebrews, intending to show the excellence of Christ, uses this very argument: "Because he was made so much better than the angels," he says, "as he hath by inheritance obtained a more excellent name than they." <margin>To the Philippians, chapter 2</margin> And elsewhere: "He gave him a na—" <note>The text breaks off mid-word; it refers to Philippians 2:9: "God... hath given him a name which is above every name."</note>

<summary>Agrippa begins his defense of women by arguing that names are divinely ordained to reflect the essence of a being, noting that 'Eve' (Life) is naturally superior to 'Adam' (Earth).</summary>

...name which is above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow—of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth. <note>original: "ut in nomine Iesu omne genu flectatur, coelestium, terrestrium, & infernorum." This is a quote from the Bible, Philippians 2:9–10, emphasizing the power inherent in a name.</note> Add to this the significant power of both laws <note>The "both laws" (Latin: *ius utrumque*) refers to the two pillars of medieval and Renaissance education: Civil Law (Roman law) and Canon Law (Church law).</note> regarding the obligations of words: in the meanings of words, in conditions and demonstrations, in the setting of conditions, and in similar disputes and legal categories, as can be seen in those and other similar titles of both laws. <margin>Canon Law: Case 16, Question 1, "If you desire"; Case 23, Question 1, "Shameful things"; On Penance, Distinction 3, "Penitence."</margin> For in law, we argue from the interpretation of a name. Likewise, we argue from the force of a word and a noun. <margin>Digest: On the meaning of words, the law "Of the hut."</margin> Furthermore, we argue from the <term>etymology</term> <note>The study of the origins and historical development of the form and meaning of words.</note> of a name and from the reason behind a name, and from the order of words. <margin>Canon Law: Case 29, Question 1, at the beginning. Codex: On bishops and clergy, the law "We decree."</margin> For the laws themselves carefully consider the meanings of names so that they may interpret something from them. <margin>Codex: On the office of the urban prefect, law 1. On the betrothal of minors, "Those of age."</margin>

Cyprian <note>Saint Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258 AD), an early Christian writer and bishop.</note> also argues against the Jews that the first man obtained his name from the four corners of the world: <term>Anatole</term> <note>East</note>, <term>Dysis</term> <note>West</note>, <term>Arktos</term> <note>North</note>, and <term>Mesembria</term> <note>South</note>, <note>In the original Greek, these four words for the cardinal directions begin with the letters A, D, A, and M, spelling "Adam."</note> which mean East, West, North, and South. <margin>On prebends, "Since according to." On elections, "Foundations," paragraph "Therefore," Book 6. Canon Law: Case 12, Question 1, "The cleric who."</margin> And in the same book, he interprets the same name "Adam" to mean "earth made flesh," although such an explanation differs from the tradition of Moses, since among the Hebrews it is written not with four letters but with three. <note>In Hebrew, the name Adam (אדם) consists of only three letters: Aleph, Dalet, and Mem. The author is noting that the Greek-based acronym used by Cyprian does not work in the original Hebrew text of the Bible.</note> <margin>Distinction 21, chapter "Clerics." Digest: On the Aquilian Law, "Thus wounded."</margin> Yet this explanation in so holy a man is not to be blamed, for he had not learned the Hebrew language—which many saints and commentators on the sacred scriptures have been ignorant of without much fault. <margin>Cyprian, *On the Mountains of Sinai and Sion*.</margin> But if I am not granted a similar license of patience, so that I might be allowed at my discretion to devise a similar etymology in praise of the female sex for the name of Eve, at least permit me to say this one thing...

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Henricus Cornelius Agrippa’s argument for the superiority of women, transitioning from linguistic/mystical proofs to the chronological order of creation.</meta>

->HENRICUS CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

From the mystical symbols of the Kabbalists <note>Kabbalists were followers of a school of Jewish mysticism. Agrippa was famous for blending these traditions with Christian philosophy.</note>, it is shown that the very name for "woman" has more in common with the ineffable name of divine omnipotence—the <term>Tetragrammaton<note>original: τετραγράμματον. This refers to the sacred four-letter name of God, often transliterated as YHWH.</note></term>—than the name of "man" does. The man’s name matches the divine name neither in its characters, its shape, nor its number. But for now, we will set these matters aside, for they are read by few and understood by fewer still; they require a much more extensive explanation than would be appropriate to write down here.

In the meantime, we shall investigate the excellence of woman not only from her name, but from the actual facts, her duties, and her merits. Let us therefore "search the scriptures" (as the saying goes) and, taking our starting point from the very beginning of creation, let us discuss what degree of dignity woman herself was granted above man in the original order of production.

We know that all things made by God—the Best and Greatest—differ chiefly in this: some remain eternally incorruptible, while others are subject to decay and change. In creating these things, God proceeded in an order that began with the "noble" and ended with the "most noble." Thus, He first created the incorruptible angels and souls. Indeed, Augustine <margin>Augustine on Genesis, book 7</margin> argues that the souls of our first parents were created together with the angels before their bodies were produced. Furthermore, He created incorruptible bodies, such as the heavens and the stars. He also created the elements, which are indeed incorruptible but subject to various transformations. From these elements, He established all other things that are subject to decay, starting from the lowlier things and ascending through each individual grade of dignity to the universal—

<summary>Agrippa argues that the Hebrew name for woman is more divinely aligned than man's and begins a theological argument that because God created woman last, she is the "most noble" pinnacle of creation.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippa, Kabbalah, Tetragrammaton, Augustine, Creation, Order of Nature, Nobility of Women</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the hierarchical order of creation, moving from the simplest elements toward the most complex and perfect. The previous section likely established the "nobility" of woman based on her placement in the divine timeline.</meta>

->### ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<-
...proceeding toward perfection. From this, minerals first came forth, followed by vegetables, plants, and trees; then <term>zoophytes</term> <note>Organisms like sponges or corals that were historically thought to be intermediate between plants and animals.</note>, and finally the brute animals, in order: those that crawl, those that swim, those that fly, and those with four legs. 

At last, He truly created two humans similar to Himself: the male first, and finally the female. <margin>Genesis 2</margin> In her, the heavens, the earth, and all their <term>adornment</term> <note>The Latin 'ornatus' refers to the stars, plants, and animals that "dress" the world; the finishing touches of the cosmos.</note> were perfected. For when the Creator reached the creation of woman, He rested in her, as if He had nothing more honorable left to create. In her, all the Creator’s wisdom and power were concluded and consummated; beyond her, no other creature is found, nor can one even be imagined. 

Since, therefore, woman is the last of all creatures, the goal, and the most perfect fulfillment of all God's works—the very perfection of the universe itself—who can deny that she is most worthy of excellence above every other creature? Without her, the world itself (though otherwise finished <term>to the fingernail</term> <note>original: "ad unguem." A Latin idiom meaning "to the highest polish" or "perfectly."</note> and complete in every part) would have remained imperfect. Such a world could only be perfected through the most perfect of all creatures. 

Indeed, it is contradictory and absurd to believe that God would finish such a great work with something imperfect. Since the world was created by God like a most whole and perfect circle, it was necessary for it to be completed in that part which would join the first of all things with the last of all things in a most unified bond. Thus, while the world was being made, woman was the last in time among all created things; yet, in authority and dignity within the very concept of the Divine Mind, she was the first of all. As it is written of her by the Prophet: "Before the heavens were created, God chose her and pre-elected her." <note>original: "Antequam coeli crearentur elegit eam Deus, & praeelegit eam." This is a theological paraphrase of Wisdom literature, suggesting woman existed as an idea in God's mind before the physical world.</note>

<summary>This page argues that woman is the pinnacle of creation because she was created last, serving as the "finishing touch" that completes the universe. The author posits that while woman was last in chronological time, she was first in God's intention and dignity.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Genesis, Perfection, Hierarchy of Nature, Divine Wisdom, Teleology, Biblical Interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s argument regarding the excellence of women, transitioning from abstract philosophical principles to specific theological and physical evidence.</meta>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<margin>Aristotle, book 2, end of "On Audibility"</margin> It is indeed a well-known opinion among philosophers (to use their own words) that the **end** <note>original: "finem"; here meaning the ultimate goal or purpose</note> is always first in **intention**, but last in **execution**. Woman, however, was God’s final work. She was introduced by God into this world like its **queen** into a palace already prepared and decorated for her, and completed with every possible gift. Deservedly, therefore, every creature loves, venerates, and respects her; and deservedly is every creature subject and obedient to her, who is the queen of all creatures as well as their end, their perfection, and their glory, complete in every way. 

For this reason, the Wise Man <note>A traditional reference to King Solomon, the reputed author of the Book of Wisdom.</note> says of her: *The nobility of woman is glorified by her sharing the company of God, and the Lord of all things has loved her.* <margin>Wisdom 3</margin> <note>original: "Generositatem mulieris glorificat contubernium habens Dei, sed & omnium Dominus dilexit eam." This is a paraphrase of Wisdom of Solomon 8:3.</note>

Holy scripture testifies most abundantly to how much she exceeds man in the nobility of her kind by reason of the **place** in which she was created. For woman was formed in **Paradise**, a place most noble as well as most pleasant, together with the angels; but man was made outside of Paradise, in the rural fields with the brute animals. It was only afterward, for the purpose of creating woman, that man was brought into Paradise. 

Therefore, woman possesses a certain peculiar gift of nature: as if she were accustomed to the exalted heights of the place of her creation, she does not suffer from **vertigo** no matter how far she looks down from on high, nor do her eyes grow dim, as is prone to happen to men. Furthermore, if it should happen that a woman and a man are equally in danger in the water, and all external help is removed, the woman will float longer than the man, who sinks more quickly and seeks the bottom. <margin>Digest, on the Edict of the Aediles, law "if he is unwilling," section "those slaves." Digest 2...</margin> <note>Agrippa uses citations from Roman Law (the Digest) to provide "scientific" weight to his claims about the physical differences between the sexes.</note> That the dignity of a location contributes to the nobility of a person, both civil laws and sacred <term>canons</term> <note>Church laws or regulations.</note> confirm quite clearly—

<summary>Agrippa argues that woman is superior to man because she was the final, crowning achievement of creation, born in the "palace" of Paradise rather than the "rural fields" of the outside world.</summary>

...humanity, and the custom of all nations. This principle is most carefully observed not only regarding human beings, but in the evaluation of any animals or even inanimate things: that the more worthy their place of origin, the more noble they are considered to be. For this reason, Isaac commanded his son Jacob not to take a wife from the land of Canaan, but from Mesopotamia in Syria, which was of a better condition. It is not dissimilar to what is found in the Gospel of John <margin>John 1</margin> when Philip said: "We have found Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth," and Nathanael replied to him, "Can anything good come out of Nazareth?" <note>Nathanael’s skeptical response to Philip reflects the common ancient belief that a person’s character was tied to the reputation and quality of their birthplace.</note> Now let us proceed to other points: Woman excels man in the very material of her creation, for she was not created from some inanimate thing or from **vile clay**, as man was, but from a material already purified, life-giving, and animated—possessing, I say, a soul that shares in the rational and <term>divine mind</term><note>Latin: *mentem participante divinam*. Agrippa argues that because Eve was made from Adam’s living flesh rather than the "dead" dust of the earth, her physical and spiritual substance is more refined.</note>. <margin>Genesis 2</margin> Add to this that man was made by God from the earth, which by its own nature produces all kinds of living things through the cooperation of <term>heavenly influence</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, "heavenly influence" (*coelesti influxu*) refers to the way stars and planets were thought to guide the natural growth of earthly things.</note>. Woman, however, was created by God alone, above all heavenly influence and the natural readiness of the world, without any other power cooperating; she was made consistent, whole, and perfect in all things, while the man meanwhile suffered the loss of one rib. It was from this rib that woman was formed—namely, Eve from the sleeping Adam—and his sleep was so profound that he did not even feel the rib being plucked out, which God took from the man and gave to the woman. Man is therefore a **work of nature**, but woman is a **workmanship of God**. <note>Agrippa makes a sharp distinction here: Adam is a product of natural processes, but Eve is a direct, special creation of the Divine Artist.</note> For this reason, woman is generally more capable of receiving **divine splendor** than man, and she is often full of it, as can easily be seen even now from her wondrous purity and beauty. For since beauty itself is nothing...

<summary>Agrippa argues that women are superior to men because they were created from living, purified matter (Adam's rib) rather than common dust, and were a direct creation of God rather than a product of natural forces.</summary>

Because of this, every creature stands in awe of her, and loves and venerates her for many reasons, so much so that we see it happens that even incorporeal spirits and demons very often fall desperately in love with women—an opinion which is not a false one, but a truth known through many experiences. 

And to pass over those things which the poets have handed down to us regarding the loves of the gods and their mistresses—such as Apollo’s Daphne, Neptune’s Salmonea, the mistresses of Hercules: Hebe, Iole, and Omphale; as well as the mistresses of the other gods and the very many of Jove [Jupiter] himself—this so divine gift of beauty, beloved by both gods and men, is celebrated and commended in many places by the Sacred Eloquence <note>The "Sacred Eloquence" refers to the Holy Scriptures or the Bible.</note> in women above all other gifts of grace.

<margin>Genesis 12</margin>
We also read of Abraham’s Sarah that she was beautiful beyond the other women of the earth—indeed, most beautiful. 

<margin>Genesis 24</margin>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s "De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus" (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Feminine Sex), a 16th-century defense of women. This section focuses on famous women from the Hebrew Bible whose beauty was paired with wisdom or divine favor.</meta>

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

The wife of Nabal (a most wicked man) was wise and prudent, as well as beautiful. Because of this, she saved the life and property of her husband from the rage of David. Thus, a bad man was saved by a beautiful woman. For it was with these words that David addressed her: "Go in peace to your house; behold, I have heard your voice and have honored your face." <note>original: "Vade pacifice in domum tuam, ecce audiui uocem tuam, & honorificaui faciem tuam"</note> Since all beauty is either spiritual, vocal, or physical, Abigail was beautiful in every way: in the wisdom of her <margin>2 Samuel 25 <note>Agrippa cites 2 Samuel, but this story is found in 1 Samuel 25.</note></margin> spirit, the eloquence of her speech, and the loveliness of her body. For this reason, after her husband Nabal died, she became one of the wives of David.

And Bathsheba was a woman of such extraordinary beauty that David, captured by love for her, elevated her to queenly dignity above all others <margin>1 Kings 1</margin> after she was wed following the death of her husband. Likewise, Abishag the Shunammite, because she was a most beautiful girl, was chosen for the purpose of restoring warmth to the body of the now-aging King David by lying beside him. Therefore, the aged king wished to honor her with the highest distinctions, and after the king's death, she was regarded as a powerful queen.

To this point also belong the things we read concerning the marvelous <margin>Esther 1 & 2</margin> beauty of Queen Vashti, and of Esther, who was preferred over her and was more outstanding than she, being exceedingly beautiful and fair of face. We also read of <margin>Judith 8 & 10</margin> Judith, whose beauty the Lord increased to such an extent that those who saw her were struck with wondering amazement.

Finally, we read of <margin>Daniel 13 <note>Susanna's story is an addition to the Book of Daniel found in the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon.</note></margin> Susanna, who was exceedingly delicate and beautiful in appearance. And what of the fact that we also read <margin>Job, last chapter, at the end</margin> how, after the various trials of Job and his endured hardships—besides the other rewards he earned through his supreme patience—the Lord gave him three daughters <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to Job's daughters who were said to be the most beautiful women in the land.</note>

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa catalogs famous biblical women—such as Abigail, Esther, and Judith—arguing that their physical beauty was a reflection of their wisdom and divine favor, often resulting in the salvation of men or nations.</summary>

->### ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<-

...most beautiful, far more pleasing than the three <term>Graces</term><note>The Charites or Graces were three goddesses in Greek mythology representing charm, beauty, and creativity.</note>, because women more beautiful were nowhere found in the whole earth. Let us further read the histories of the holy virgins; we will surely marvel at how the Catholic Church, in its solemn praise, sings of their wondrous beauty and most exquisite form above the rest of the children of men.

But the leader of all, far and wide, is the immaculate Virgin Mary, Mother of God, whose beauty the sun and moon admire. From her most beautiful face shone such a chastity and holiness of beauty that, although it captured the eyes and minds of all alike, no mortal ever fell into temptation by her charms, not even in the smallest thought. I have reviewed these things—even if quite broadly—from the Holy Bible, where mention of beauty is made so often and in almost these very words, so that we may clearly understand that the beauty of women is honored and loaded with prestige, not only among men but also before God.

Therefore, we also read elsewhere <margin>Numbers 31</margin> in the Sacred Scriptures that God commanded every male, even the children, to be killed, but the beautiful women to be saved. <margin>Deuteronomy 21</margin> And in Deuteronomy, the sons of Israel are permitted to choose for themselves a beautiful woman from among the captives to be a wife. 

Besides this admirable beauty, woman is also endowed with a certain dignity of modesty which does not happen to men: For a woman's hair is allowed to grow to such a length that it can cover all the more <term>private parts</term><note>original: "pudentiores"; literally the 'shameful' or modest parts of the body.</note> of the body. Add to this that in the natural functions of the body, it is less necessary for a woman to handle these parts, whereas for men it is a matter of common necessity...

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the physical and moral superiority of women over men, specifically focusing on anatomical modesty and facial beauty.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...it is never necessary to come into view. Finally, nature herself has arranged the loins of women with a wonderful decency, not protruding as they do in men, but remaining within, set apart in a more secret and safer place. Furthermore, nature has bestowed more modesty upon women than upon men. For this reason, it has very often happened that a woman, suffering from a dangerous abscess of the loins, has chosen death rather than exposing herself to the sight and touch of a male surgeon to be healed. And this honorable modesty they retain even when dying and dead, as is most evident in those who perish in the water. For <margin>Pliny</margin> <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD) was a Roman author and naturalist. This reference is to his *Natural History*, where he records folk beliefs about the human body.</note> is the authority, and experience is the witness, that a drowned woman lies face down <note>original: *prona*</note>, nature sparing the modesty of the deceased; but a man floats face up <note>original: *supinus*</note>. 

Added to this is the fact that the most noble member in a human—by which we differ most from the beasts and which indicates our divine nature—is the head, and in it especially the face. The head in men is indeed deformed by baldness, whereas woman, by a great privilege of nature, does not go bald. Furthermore, the face in men is often so disfigured by a beard—which is most hateful to them—and covered with filthy hairs, that they can scarcely be distinguished from wild animals <note>original: *beluis*</note>. In women, by contrast, the face remains always pure and graceful. Hence, by the Law of the Twelve Tables <term>Twelve Tables: The foundation of ancient Roman law, written around 450 BCE.</term> it was provided that women should not shave their cheeks, lest a beard might eventually grow out and their modesty be hidden <note>Agrippa interprets this law as a way to preserve the natural, hairless beauty of the female face, which he associates with "pudor" (modesty/shame).</note>. Even the cleanliness and purity of the woman herself is proven to everyone by this most evident argument: that once a woman is cleanly washed, as often as she is rinsed afterward with pure water, the water itself receives no trace of impurity...

<summary>Agrippa argues for female superiority based on the "decency" of female anatomy, the natural modesty of women even in death, and their lack of "beastly" traits like baldness and facial hair.</summary>

->ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<-
<note>The full title is *De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus* (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex), written by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa in 1509 and published in 1529.</note>

A man, however much he has been washed, every time he washes again, he muddies and fouls the water. In addition, by nature's order, women expel superfluities <note>In Renaissance medicine, "superfluities" referred to excess bodily fluids or "humors" that needed to be purged to maintain health.</note> every month through more secret places; for men, these are continuously emitted through the face—a much more noble part of the human body. <note>Agrippa is arguing that women’s bodies are cleaner because they purge impurities privately, whereas men's impurities manifest as facial hair or sweat on the face.</note> Furthermore, while among all other living things it is granted to humans alone to lift their faces toward heaven, nature and fortune have wonderfully provided for and spared women in this: if by chance or accident they fall, women almost always fall on their backs, and never or only rarely fall face-forward or on their heads. <margin>Ovid</margin> <note>original: "Ovidius"; a reference to the Roman poet Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, which describes man as the only animal that looks up at the stars.</note>

Why—not to omit this—do we not see in the procreation of the human race that nature preferred the woman to the man? This is most clear because only the female seed, according to the testimony of Galen and Avicenna, is the matter and nourishment of the fetus; <margin>Galen, *On Seed*, Book 2</margin> <margin>On the Use of Parts, Book 14</margin> that of the man is not so at all, for it enters the process, in a sense, as an "accident" to the "substance." <note>Agrippa uses Aristotelian logic here. "Substance" is the essential core of a thing, while an "accident" is an external or non-essential quality. He is provocatively reversing the common medieval view that the male provided the "soul" (form) and the female only the "matter."</note> For the greatest and chief duty of women, as the law says, <margin>Avicenna, Doctrine 5, Fen 1 of the First [Book]</margin> is to conceive and to protect what has been conceived. For this reason, we see that most children resemble their mothers because they were created from their blood. This is true most often in the physical habit of the body, but always in character; for if mothers are foolish, the sons become foolish; if mothers are wise, their sons reflect their wisdom. On the contrary, in the case of fathers, if they themselves are wise, they mostly produce foolish sons, and foolish fathers produce wise sons—provided the mother is wise. Nor is there any other reason why mothers...

<margin>Psalm 147</margin> God becomes the book of the law and of the divine utterances which He gave to the Jews, announcing His word to Jacob, and His justice and judgments to the people of Israel. He has not done so for every nation, and His judgments He has not manifested to them <note>original: "Non fecit taliter omni nationi..." — a common biblical citation used to argue for the unique spiritual status of the Hebrew people</note>; for they, above the philosophers, had prophets instructed by spiritual and angelic creatures, and they knew God through them. <margin>Acts 7</margin> Whence Stephen the first martyr said to them: "You who received the law by the dispensation of angels." <margin>Dionysius</margin> And Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic believed to have written on the celestial hierarchies</note> says that the glorious seers of the Hebrews attained prophecy and divine vision through the mediation of the heavenly virtues. For this reason, the entire school of the Cabalists <term>Cabalists: practitioners of Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism involving the esoteric interpretation of the Bible</term> teaches that the primary intention of the Law is concerned only with the angelic choir, while the sublime and ineffable trinity of the Essence would remain unknown until the coming of the Messiah. Finally, therefore, God sent to us a third book, namely the book of the Gospel given to Christians, we who have known God through the very Son of God, co-eternal with the Father, made man, our Lord Jesus Christ. Whence Paul says: "In these last days, God has spoken to us in His Son, whom He appointed heir of all things, through whom He made all things," and we have teachers, the apostles, taught by the Son of God, Jesus Christ.

### CHAPTER THREE

Now, therefore, let us treat these matters individually; and first, let us see how God may be known through His creatures. But let us not understand this to mean that we can know God here as He is in His furthest and solitary nature...

[...wo]man <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing from the previous page's discussion of women's early maturity.</note>, for that sacred duty of procreation (as is evident to everyone) is capable even at ten years of age and below, whereas the male's power comes much later. Furthermore, it is obscure to no one that among all living creatures, only the woman—even after she is pregnant and has begun to carry, and not long after she has been delivered of her child—is inclined again toward the work of reproduction. Her vessel (which they call the <term>matrix</term> <note>The womb or uterus.</note>) is so inspired toward human conception that one may occasionally read of a woman conceiving without intercourse. These and many other such things she digests without harm, converting them into healthy nourishment for the body. No one will wonder at how many other miracles nature delights in producing in women if they have read through the volumes of philosophers and physicians; I shall provide just one example that is ready at hand.

In menstrual blood, there is a power that not only frees one from <term>quartan fevers</term> <note>A form of malaria where fever recurs every four days.</note>, from hydrophobia <note>original: "hydrophorbia"; a historical term for rabies.</note>, from the <term>falling sickness</term> <note>original: "morbo comitiali"; epilepsy.</note>, from leprosy <note>original: "elephantia".</note>, from melancholic impressions, from mania, and from many other most pernicious illnesses of that kind. It also performs many other things no less worthy of admiration: among other wonders, it extinguishes fires, calms storms, wards off the dangers of the waves, drives away all harmful things, dissolves <term>maleficia</term> <note>Evil spells or witchcraft.</note>, and puts <term>cacodaemons</term> <note>Evil spirits or demons.</note> to flight. <meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, specifically drawing upon Hermetic and Neoplatonic sources to discuss the knowledge of God through the created world.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

<margin>Asclepius, 10.</margin> ...not where He may be in a place, nor what He may be in quality, nor how great He may be in quantity, but illuminating man by the intelligence of the mind alone. <margin>Pymander, 2.</margin> And elsewhere he says: God fabricated all things for this reason, that you might perceive Him through every individual thing; this is the goodness of God, this is His power, that He shines through all things. There is nothing, even among incorporeal things, that is invisible; the mind itself is seen in the act of understanding, but God is beheld in His operation. <margin>Pymander, 5.</margin> Whence elsewhere Hermes <note>original: "Mercurius," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic corpus is attributed</note> says: finally, when you wish to see God, look up at the sun, my son, observe the courses of the moon, look up at the motions of the rest of the stars; who preserves the perpetual order of these? Who assigns the measure of motion to each? Who pulls the machine of the world? Who uses this instrument? Who has circumscribed the sea within its boundaries? Who stays and balances the weight of the earth? In the midst of these, there is some author and lord by his art. Whence also Dionysius <margin>Dionysius, On Divine Names</margin> <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher</note> says: perhaps we say this truly, that we do not know God from His own nature—for that is indeed unknown and surpasses all reason and sense—but from the most orderly disposition of all creatures produced by Him, as if they were certain images and likenesses of His own divine patterns; by this path and order, we climb according to our strength toward that which transcends all things. Thus far did the philosophers of the nations <note>The "Gentiles" or non-Christian philosophers of antiquity</note> ascend from the mere apprehension of creatures; that is, they embraced by their own intelligence all things which are created on the earth, in the waters, in the elements, in the heavens, and those things which are furthermore above the heavens. At last, they arrived at the first mover, and the true [source] of all...

<summary>Agrippa cites Hermes Trismegistus and Dionysius the Areopagite to argue that while God's nature is unknowable, He can be perceived through the order and beauty of the physical universe. This "natural theology" allowed ancient philosophers to ascend toward a realization of a First Mover.</summary>

Agrippa uses this poetic imagery from Virgil to suggest a natural, almost divine fertility inherent in the feminine.</note>

<margin>*Pimander*, 12</margin> Now, what shall I say of speech, that divine gift, by which alone we
most surpass the beasts? Trismegistus Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the "Hermetic" writings are attributed.</note> considers it of 
the same value as immortality. And Hesiod <margin>Hesiod</margin> calls it the best 
treasure of man. Is not a woman more eloquent in speech than 
a man, and more articulate and abundant? Do we not all, as many as 
we are, learn to speak for the first time only from our mothers or our 
nurses? Surely nature herself, the architect of all things, 
looking wisely upon the human race in this regard, 
has gifted this female sex so that a mute woman is hardly 
found anywhere. It is surely a beautiful and laudable thing to excel men 
in that very quality by which humans most surpass all other living creatures. 

But let us return from secular to sacred writings, as if by <term>postliminio</term> <note>A legal term referring to the right of a person to return to their original status after being a prisoner of war; here used metaphorically to mean returning to the "homeland" of scripture.</note> 
and let us begin the matter from the very fountains of religion. We 
know first of all, without doubt, that God blessed man 
on account of woman; a blessing which man—being as it were unworthy—
had not deserved to have until after woman had been created. 
With this agrees that proverb of Solomon: "He who finds a <margin>Proverbs 18</margin>
good woman, finds a good thing, and draws a blessing from the Lord." And 
that of Ecclesiasticus: "Blessed is the husband of a good woman; the number <margin>Ecclesiasticus 26</margin>
of his years is doubled." And no man can be compared 
in dignity to him who has been worthy to have a good woman. 
For as Ecclesiasticus says: "A good woman is a grace <margin>Ecclesiasticus 26</margin>
above all grace." And therefore Solomon in the Proverbs <margin>Proverbs 12</margin>
calls her the "crown of the husband." And Paul calls her the "glory of the man." <margin>1 Corinthians 11</margin>
Moreover, "glory" is defined as the consummation and perfection of a thing.

                                B 4

<summary>In this passage, Agrippa argues for the superiority of women based on their natural eloquence and their role in teaching speech, while asserting that man only received God's blessing after woman's creation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, likely from his "De Occulta Philosophia" or a related philosophical oration, focusing on the divine nature of the human soul and the moral failings of the ancient philosophers.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

The soul beholds all things. No gloom of the air confuses its focus, no density of the earth hinders its work, and no profound depth of water blunts its gaze. <margin>Pimander, Book 2</margin> <note>Agrippa is quoting the "Pimander," the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> And elsewhere it says: Command your soul, and swifter than your command, it will fly forth. Order it to cross over into the Ocean, and it will be there before you have finished the order. From where it is now, without departing at all, command it again to fly into heaven; it will need no wings, and nothing shall obstruct its course—not the burning of the sun, nor the vastness of the upper air, nor the turning of the heavens. It penetrates the bodies of all other stars until it transcends even the highest celestial body. Indeed, if you wish to transcend all the spheres of the heavens and investigate what lies beyond, that too is permitted to you.

Observe now how great is the power of the soul, how great its virtue, and how great its speed; for this reason, man is without excuse if he remains ignorant of God. But even more so is he who, knowing God in some way, neither worships nor venerates Him. For this is the most hateful and inexcusable impiety, which Paul, reproaching the Gentiles, describes thus: <margin>Romans 1</margin> "So that they are without excuse, because although they knew God, they did not glorify Him as God, nor gave thanks." <note>original: "ut sint inexcusabiles, quia cum cognovissent deum, non sicut deum glorificaverunt, aut gratias egerunt." A citation of Romans 1:20-21.</note> For the philosophers of the nations, being learned in various disciplines—Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, Astronomy, Physics, Metaphysics, Dialectic, and the rest—knew by their science the one, only, and true God. Yet, being impious and ungrateful for such a great benefit, they turned away from pure and holy knowledge toward a certain false image—

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion on the knowledge of God, transitioning from the theoretical recognition of a creator to the moral obligations of piety and justice.</meta>

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

Following a rash imagination rather than inspecting any true reason, they did not worship Him as the one and only true God, nor did they give thanks to Him who enlightened them with the light of recognizing His divinity. Therefore, all those shall be condemned for impiety, injustice, and ingratitude, according to the words of Paul, who says: *For the wrath of God is revealed from heaven against all ungodliness and unrighteousness of men* <margin>Romans 1 & 2</margin>. For impiety is a sin against God, while injustice is against men; however, the extreme form of impiety is not to know God. From impiety comes intemperance <note>Lack of self-control or moderation</note>, and this is the foundation of injustice. Indeed, we define intemperance as the depravation of the will arising from the slumber of reason, where the sensual affection dominates too much—namely, where reason is put to sleep and all things are done at the command of the senses. Whence this is called the "sterile soul," producing no good fruit in its time; and this is the soul's highest impiety: to be sterile. Concerning this, Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius," referring to the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note> says: *Impiety happens to him who departs from life without children* <margin>Pimander 2</margin> <note>In Hermetic thought, "children" often metaphorically represent divine knowledge or good deeds</note>, for which reason he pays the penalty to demons after death. But we know God in vain unless we worship Him rightly and live lawfully with other men. Whence Hermes says: *The contest of religious piety is to recognize God and to do injury to no one* <margin>Pimander 2</margin>. Christ also commanded this, saying: *Love the Lord your God, and your neighbor as yourself* <margin>Matthew 22, Mark 12, Luke 10</margin>. These two precepts are necessary for salvation and are the source of all good: the first belongs to piety, the second to justice. On the contrary, impiety and injustice are the source of all...

<summary>This passage argues that true knowledge of God must result in both religious piety and social justice, warning that a soul governed by senses rather than reason becomes "sterile" and faces divine judgment.</summary>
<keywords>impiety, justice, Hermes Trismegistus, St. Paul, reason vs. senses, sterility of the soul, dual commandment of love</keywords>

...would endure. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion on the Incarnation.</note> For indeed, because of woman, Christ was called the "Son of Man" <note>original: "filius hominis"</note>, not because of man. Indeed, he was even denied by his own [apostle] Peter, abandoned by the rest of the disciples, and accompanied to the cross and the tomb by women alone. <margin>Luke 24</margin> And even Pilate’s own wife, a Gentile, <margin>Matthew 27</margin> tried harder to save Jesus than any of the men, even those who had believed. To this we add that almost the entire school of theologians asserts that the Church then remained only in a single woman—namely, the Virgin Mary—and for this reason, the female sex is rightly and deservedly called "religious and sacred." <note>This refers to the "Silence of Saturday" tradition: the idea that between the Crucifixion and Resurrection, Mary was the only person on Earth who maintained perfect faith.</note> But if even <margin>Aristotle, *Book on Animals*</margin> anyone should say, along with Aristotle, that among all animals the males are stronger, wiser, and more noble: to this, the more excellent and learned Apostle Paul responds, saying: <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> "God has chosen the foolish things of the world to shame the wise; and God has chosen the weak things of the world to shame the strong; and the base things of the world and things which are despised God has chosen, and the things which are not..."

->ON NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<-

Yet a woman provoked him, being in this superior and more confident than the devil, for she irritated him into cursing. <note>Job 2:9—Job's wife famously told him to "Curse God and die."</note> Indeed, if it is now permissible to bring Christ himself into this comparison— <margin>Matthew 15</margin> than whom nothing is more powerful, nothing wiser, since he is the eternal wisdom and power of God—did he not allow himself to be overcome by that humble <term>Canaanite woman</term> <note>A non-Jewish woman who asked Jesus to heal her daughter; her persistence is often cited as a rare example of someone "besting" or persuading Jesus in a rhetorical exchange.</note> when he himself said: *It is not good to take the bread of the children and cast it to the dogs.* She, responding on the contrary: *Certainly, Lord, for even the little dogs eat of the crumbs that fall from the table of their masters.* And when Christ had seen this, because he could not overcome her in this argument, he blessed her, saying: *Let it be done for you as you wish.* Who among the Apostles was more fervent in faith than Peter, the first of them? <margin>John 18, Matthew 20, Mark 14, Luke 22</margin> A woman seduced him, the greatest shepherd of the church, into denying Christ. Let the <term>canonists</term> <note>Scholars or experts in Church law.</note> say whatever they wish—that their Church cannot err—yet a female Pope deluded it with a most remarkable imposture. <margin>24. q. 1. from the right; Platina on John 8 <note>Refers to the legend of Pope Joan, a woman who allegedly reigned as Pope; Platina was a Renaissance historian who recorded the tale.</note></margin>

But someone will say that these things tend more toward the reproach of women than toward their praise.

**HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

And for this remarkable betrayal, the Scripture says: "Blessed among women be Jael in her tent." Read the history of Judith, and note her words to Holofernes. "Accept," she said, "the words of your handmaid, for if you follow them, the Lord will make you perfect; I will come and announce everything to you, so that I may lead <margin>Judith 11</margin> [you]..."

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for the superiority of the female sex, transitioning from the cleverness of Biblical heroines to a theological comparison involving the Virgin Mary.</meta>

->ON THE NOBLE AND EXCELLENT<-
<note>The full title of this work is *De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus* (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex), written by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa in 1509 and published in 1529.</note>

...go through the middle of Jerusalem, and you will have all the people of Israel like sheep without a shepherd, and not even a single dog will bark against you, since these things were told to me through the providence of God. And having lulled Holofernes with flatteries, she struck him in the neck and cut off his head. <note>Agrippa is recounting the story of Judith from the Deuterocanonical Book of Judith. She deceived the Assyrian general Holofernes to save her people.</note> What wicked counsel, I ask you—what more cruel snare, what more captive treachery could be devised? And yet, for this very reason, Scripture blesses, praises, and extols her; thus, the wickedness of a woman is reputed far better than a man doing good. <note>This is a provocative reference to Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 42:14, which Agrippa interprets here to mean that even a "sinful" act by a woman for a good cause outweighs the "good" deeds of men.</note> But now let us return to the excellence of this most happy sex. This can be an most evident argument for all: that the most worthy of all creatures—of whom there never has been, nor shall be, a more worthy woman—was the most blessed Virgin Mary herself. Indeed, if she was conceived without original sin <note>Agrippa alludes to the doctrine of the Immaculate Conception, which was a subject of intense theological debate in the 16th century.</note>, not even Christ himself, as far as his humanity is concerned, shall be greater. For this is a valid argument from Aristotle: that the genus whose best member is nobler, is itself nobler than another genus. <note>A reference to Aristotle’s *Topics*, where he argues that classes can be compared by looking at their most excellent examples.</note> In the female genus, the best is the Virgin Mary; in the male genus, there has not arisen one greater than John the Baptist. <note>A reference to Matthew 11:11.</note> And no Catholic is ignorant of how far the divine Virgin—who is exalted above all the choirs of angels—excels him. Similarly, it will be permitted to argue that the genus whose worst member is worse than the worst of another, is the inferior genus. Now indeed we know that the most vicious and worst of all creatures is man—whether it was Judas who betrayed Christ, of whom Christ said: "It would have been good for that man if he had not been born." <margin>Mark 14</margin>

<summary>Agrippa argues for the superiority of women by comparing the highest and lowest members of each sex: the Virgin Mary excels John the Baptist, while the worst man, Judas, represents the ultimate iniquity.</summary>

To this testimony is also added a certain prerogative of brute nature; for the queen of all birds and the most noble, which is the eagle, is always found to be female and is never found to be of the male sex. <note>In Renaissance natural history, it was a common (though incorrect) belief that eagles were either all female or that the female was the only "true" noble eagle.</note> The Egyptians also reported that the unique bird, the Phoenix, is nothing but female. On the contrary, however, the little serpent-king which they call the <term>basilisk|A mythical reptile said to be the king of serpents, capable of killing with a single glance</term>, the most pestilential of all venomous creatures, is nothing but male, and it is moreover impossible for a female to be born. He was the first envious man, the first murderer, the first parricide, and the first to despair of God's mercy. Lamech was the first bigamist; <margin>Genesis 9</margin> Noah the first drunkard; <margin>Ibid.</margin> that son of Noah, Ham, was the first to expose the shame of his father; <margin>Genesis 10</margin> Nimrod was the first tyrant as well as the first idolater. The first adulterer was a man; the first incestuous person was a man; moreover, men were the first to enter into pacts with demons and invent profane arts. The men who were the sons of Jacob were the first to sell their brother; Pharaoh of Egypt... <margin>Genesis 37, Exodus 1</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the knowledge of God, transitioning from the limitations of the Old Law to the fulfillment found in Christ.</meta>

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

To communicate the most secret things to the foolish common people would be nothing other than giving what is holy to the dogs. Even Christ himself forbade this in his Gospel; he who was promised through the law and <margin>Matthew 7</margin> desired in the law, finally came at the opportune time as the fulfiller and perfecter of the law, just as he himself said: "For I have not come to abolish the law, but to fulfill it." <margin>Matthew 5</margin> For the entire law was divided into three parts: they were either shadowy figures of the light to come, or prophetic words of the truth to come, or precepts for living toward the perfection to come. But if you grasp only the literal sense of the law, without the spirit of the future light, truth, and perfection, nothing will be more ridiculous than the law, and it will be most similar to an old wives' tale or a Milesian story <note>original: "milesijq; sermonis"; a reference to Milesian tales, which were ancient Greek fables known for being low-brow or scurrilous</note>. Furthermore, Christ came as the sun of justice, the true light, the clearest truth, and the true perfection of life for all men who believe in his name. He himself fulfilled the law, so that from now on there is no need for the law; nor do we henceforth know God in the darkness of created things, nor in the shadow of the Jewish law, but in the light of the faith of Jesus Christ. This is true knowledge, <margin>Ephesians 1</margin> the wisdom of the Father, and the understanding of man, in whom—as Paul says—all things are summed up, both those in heaven and those on earth. Therefore, let us now speak consequently about the final and perfect knowledge of God, which is through the Gospel of Jesus Christ our Lord.

...tion, a certain law of life sanctifying the soul of man, disposing his life toward God, demonstrating what must be done and what must be omitted; this wisdom we call by another name, <term>Theology</term> <note>From the Greek 'theos' (God) and 'logos' (word/study)</note>. This wisdom, and the true knowledge of God—indeed, a certain essential contact with God that is better than mere knowledge—is handed down by divine means in the Gospel. For it is manifest that those things which have been handed down from God cannot be understood except through God, just as the Prophet says: <margin>Psalm 36:9 <note>Original: Psalm 35</note></margin> *In your light we shall see light.* This light <term>Hermes Trismegistus</term> <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretic combination of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus</note> <margin>Pimander 1 <note>The first book of the Corpus Hermeticum</note></margin> calls the Mind of the divine essence, the very light arising from God. For to believe, as <margin>Pimander 9</margin> Hermes says, is itself to understand; finally, by asking in hope with firm and certain expectation, praising and adoring Jesus Christ, from whom such most divine knowledge descends into our soul, so that he may illuminate us with the light of his Spirit. ->**ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE**<-

...and tragedies of this sort; if anyone with lynx-like eyes <note>original: "linceis oculis" — a common Renaissance idiom for having extremely sharp or penetrating sight, referring to the mythical keenness of the lynx</note> (as they say) were to look into them, he would find that wives are falsely accused. But on the contrary, women have emerged as the inventors of all the liberal arts, and of every virtue and benefit—which the feminine names of the arts and virtues themselves especially demonstrate. <note>The author is making a linguistic argument: in Latin, the names for most virtues (like Justice, Fortitude, and Wisdom) and arts (like Music or Geometry) are grammatically feminine nouns.</note> To this is added that notable fact: that the world itself is named after women—namely Asia after the nymph, Europe after the daughter of Agenor, and Libya after the daughter of Epaphus (which is also called Africa). Finally, if one travels through every kind of virtue, a woman will hold the first place everywhere. For it was a woman who first vowed her virginity to God—the Virgin Mary herself—who for this reason deserved to be the Mother of God. Women prophets have always been inspired by a more divine spirit than men, as <margin>Lactantius, Institutes</margin> Lactantius and <margin>Eusebius, Preparation for the Gospel</margin> Eusebius testify...

->### HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA###<-

...to this day, the Catholic faith and the continued works of piety shine forth. But lest anyone should doubt that women are capable of everything that men are: let us deal with the matter itself through examples. We shall find that no outstanding deed in any kind of virtue has ever been performed by men that has not been performed equally brilliantly by women. In ancient times, the Melissae <note>original: "Melissæ"; literally "bees," a title for the priestesses of the Earth Mother</note> of Cybele <note>an ancient Phrygian mother goddess</note> were famous in the priesthood among the nations; from her name, the priestesses of other goddesses were later called Melissae. 

Likewise, Hyperaustria was a priestess of Minerva, Mera a priestess of Venus, and Iphigenia of Diana. There were also female priestesses of Bacchus <note>the god of wine and ritual ecstasy</note>, famous by many names, such as the Thyades, Maenads, Bacchae, Eliades, Mimallonides, Aedonides, Euhyades, Bassarides, and Triaterides <note>these names refer to different groups of female worshippers of Dionysus/Bacchus known for their ecstatic rites</note>. Among the Jews also, Miriam <note>original: "Maria"</note>, the sister of Moses, entered the sanctuary together with Aaron and was regarded as a priestess. In our own religion, however, although women are forbidden from the function of the priesthood, it is nevertheless recorded in famous histories that a woman once, by feigning her sex, ascended to the peak of the supreme pontificate <note>a reference to the legendary Pope Joan, who was said to have reigned as Pope in the 9th century</note>. Nor are our many most holy abbesses and nuns obscure, whom antiquity did not disdain to call priestesses. 

They have also shone in prophecy among the peoples of every region: Cassandra, the Sibyls <note>ancient oracular prophetesses</note>, Miriam the sister of Moses, Deborah, Huldah, Anna, Elizabeth, the four daughters of Philip, and many other more recent holy women, such as Bridget <note>likely St. Bridget of Sweden</note> and Hildegard <note>Hildegard of Bingen, the 12th-century mystic and polymath</note>. Furthermore, in magic—whether in the unconquerable discipline of good or evil spirits <note>original: "daemonum"; in this context, spirits that could be helpful or harmful</note>—Circe and Medea were far more wonder[ful]... <note>the text cuts off mid-word "mirabili-"</note>

Nor should we forget **Queen Zenobia** in this place, the disciple of the philosopher **Longinus**; because of her vast and brilliant expertise in literature, she received the title *Ephitissa* <note>original: "Ephitissae" — likely a reference to her being a "letter-writer" or an orator of Epistles.</note>, whose sacred works **Nicomachus** translated into Greek.

What can be said of this fact: that by nature alone, women seem easily to surpass the practitioners of all disciplines? Do not the grammarians boast of being the masters of "speaking well"? Therefore, if the pontiffs, prelates, and our doctors of divine wisdom do not possess the prophetic spirit, and if they do not prove their admirable profession before the church by the effect of some divine power, then certainly their spirit is possessed by a worldly mind <note>original: "mentis liuinæ" likely a typo for "mundanae" or "liminae"</note>, their faith in Christ is weak, and it languishes because the flesh dominates too much over the spirit. For this reason, all those with such a sterile soul shall be judged and condemned by God for their impiety and injustice.

Now you have the description of what sort of person he ought to be who desires to attain the knowledge of God, and who truly deserves to be called a "theologian" <term>Theologian: literally 'one who speaks of God'</term>—one who desires to speak with God and to meditate on His law day and night. ->OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...doubted, for he knew that an empire of this kind would soon perish if women were not present. Finally, after the Capitol had been captured by the Sabines and a most bloody battle was being fought in the middle of the Forum with standards joined, the fighting ceased through the intervention of the women between the two battle lines. Once peace was finally made and a treaty struck, they entered into a perpetual friendship.

For this reason, Romulus gave their names to the <term>Curiae</term> <note>The *Curiae* were the thirty original political divisions of the Roman people; Agrippa argues here that they were named in honor of the Sabine women.</note>. Furthermore, by the will of the Romans, it was recorded in the public records that a woman should neither be forced to grind grain nor perform the work of a cook. It was also forbidden for a wife to receive any gift from her husband, or a husband from his wife, so that they might know that all their goods were held in common. 

From this, the custom finally emerged that those leading a bride into her new home were ordered to say, "Where you are, I am," <note>original: *ubi tu ego*</note> signifying "Where you are master, I am mistress; where you are lord, I am lady" <note>original: *ubi tu dominus, ego domina. Vbi tu herus, ego hera*—the traditional Roman marriage vow of equality in the household.</note>. Later, after the kings had been expelled, when the legions of the Volscians led by Marcius Coriolanus <note>A Roman general who defected to the enemy; according to legend, his mother and wife convinced him to call off his attack on Rome.</note> were encamped at the fifth milestone, they were turned back by the efforts of the women. In recognition of this service, a notable temple to Fortune...

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion on the nature of knowing God, specifically contrasting human logic with divine faith.</meta>

# **ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**

Whether they respond or offer a premise, or whether they accept, assume, confirm, and conclude, they call those people "contentious" who think one should litigate as one pleases, not by reason, but by sheer temper. If, therefore, those men do this—whose very craft is contention—what ought a Christian to do, if not flee contentions altogether? Thus he spoke. And so, if we wish to truly know God, we must set aside all turbulent reasoning, all sophisticated argumentation, and all dialectical <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions through logic.</note> inquiry. For reason and inquiry suggest a certain amount of despair and distrust. Faith, however, ought to be fixed and tranquil; for this reason, Ambrose says in his book *On the Trinity*: <margin>Ambrose in the book *On the Trinity*</margin> "Remove arguments where faith is sought; even in their own schools, let logic now be silent; we believe fishermen, not logicians." <margin>The same to Gratian on faith</margin> The same author, writing to Gratian regarding faith, says: <margin>Urban, and it is held in Distinction 37</margin> And Pope Urban, writing to Charles, says: "It did not please God to save His people through logic. For the Kingdom of God is in the simplicity of faith, not in the contention of speech." For there is no greater plague to the soul than reasoning, than altercation, than disputation concerning divine matters, which overturns reason, perverts the intellect, and casts down faith. <margin>To the Colossians 2; 1 Corinthians 1 and 2; 2 Timothy 2; To Titus 3</margin> Therefore, Paul commands that it be avoided above all, and James calls it "earthly, animalistic, and demonic wisdom." <margin>James 3</margin> From this source come errors, doubts, lies, and heresies; from here the first sin arose in the human race. Moreover, the inventor of this so pestilential faculty was the devil—that first cunning and ruinous sophist—who proposed little questions, invented disputations, and as if...

<summary>This passage argues that true knowledge of God is found through simple faith rather than logical debate or "dialectics," citing Church Fathers like Ambrose to warn that intellectual pride leads to heresy.</summary>
<keywords>faith vs. reason, dialectics, St. Ambrose, Pope Urban, logic, heresy, simplicity of faith, sophistry</keywords>

->ON NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<-

...pertaining to discipline: in the use of the bow, in the sling, in the throwing of stones, in <margin>In the Authenticum <note>A medieval collection of Roman laws</note> or so that no... If we read the trustworthy writers of antiquity, we find that among the Getulians <note>An ancient tribe of North Africa</note>, the Bactrians <note>A people from Central Asia, in modern-day Afghanistan/Uzbekistan</note>, and in Galletia <note>Likely referring to Gallaecia in Northwest Spain</note>, it was the custom for men to be devoted to softness, while the women farmed the fields, built structures, traded, rode horses, fought in battle, and did other things that among us now only men are accustomed to do. Among the Cantabrians <note>A tribe in northern ancient Spain</note>, the men gave dowries to the women, brothers were given in marriage by their sisters, and daughters were designated as heirs. Among the Scythians, Thracians, and Gauls, duties were common to both women and men. And when dealing with matters of war and peace, women were admitted to the judgment and consultations, which is demonstrated by the treaty struck by the Celts with Hannibal in these words:

*If any of the Celts complains that he has been wronged by any of the Carthaginians, the Carthaginian magistrates or the generals who are in Spain shall be the judges of that matter. If any of the Carthaginians suffers any injustice from any of the Celts, the Celtic women shall pass judgment on the matter.*

But because of the excessive tyranny of men, prevailing against divine right and the laws of nature, the liberty once granted to women has been seized. Now, you might say, it is forbidden by laws, abolished by custom and usage, and extinguished by education. For as soon as a woman is born, she is kept at home in idleness from her earliest years, and as if [incapable] of a higher province... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, suggesting women are restricted from broader intellectual or social "provinces" or spheres of influence.</note>

Now finally, to summarize my points as briefly as possible: we have shown the excellence of the female sex from her name, from the order of creation, from her place of origin, from the material of her body, and the dignity that woman was granted by God above man. Furthermore, we have demonstrated this excellence through religion, through nature, and through human laws, using various authorities, logic, and examples. Yet I have not said nearly as much as could be said; many things remain to be told, for I am not moved by ambition, or...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s "De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus" (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex), originally published in 1529.</meta>

### ->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

I have come to write not for the sake of my own praise, but out of duty and truth. I did not want to appear like a sacrilegious man, stealing away the praises owed to such a devoted sex through an impious silence—acting as if I were burying the talent <note>original: "talentum"; a reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), where a servant is punished for hiding/burying the money entrusted to him instead of using it to produce more.</note> entrusted to me. 

If anyone more diligent than I should find any argument I have overlooked, which they think belongs in the construction of this work, I will consider myself not criticized but helped by them, provided that they improve this good effort of ours with their own brilliance and learning. Therefore, lest the work itself expand into too large a volume, let this be the end of it.

<summary>In this concluding passage, Agrippa explains that his defense of women was a moral duty rather than a search for personal fame, and he invites future scholars to build upon his arguments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s defense against a critic, likely a member of the clergy or a rival scholar, who has been attacking Agrippa’s reputation in his absence.</meta>

->### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

Why do you, who should desire nothing but to attain honor through virtue, instead slander me in my absence—even across the vast distances that separate us—with such undeservedly bitter speech? <margin>Romans 12</margin> For you, by the very profession of your name <note>original: "tui nominis professione"; likely referring to his status as a Christian or a monk</note>, should follow what Paul teaches the Romans: you ought to hate evil and cling to what is good, love brotherly charity, and not speak evil, but rather bless even those who persecute you, and, as far as it is possible, live at peace with all men. Truly, you have not done anything worthy of your profession or of a Christian teacher—you who should have exhorted the people of the Christian name toward those things which belong to Christ: toward the works of the spirit, toward charity and peace, and those other things which Paul <margin>Galatians 5</margin> commemorates to the Galatians. For Christ, the author of our religion, as well as the Apostles and the whole of Holy Scripture (as you ought to know better than I), call out for peace and tranquility. <margin>John 14 <note>The original text cites "Ioan. 4," but the quote "Peace I leave with you" is found in John 14:27.</note></margin> Hence the Evangelist John relates that Christ said to his disciples: *My peace I give to you, my peace I leave with you.* <margin>Hebrews 12</margin> And Paul says to the Hebrews: *Follow peace with all men, and holiness, without which no one shall see God.* It should be your duty, as well as mine, not only to exhort others toward this peace, but also to pray for it. <margin>Ephesians 4</margin> Does not the Apostle, admonishing the Ephesians, say: *Let no evil speech proceed from your mouth*? ->REPROACH AGAINST CATILINET<-

The Epistle to the Corinthians so detests slander <note>original Latin: *maledicentia*; literally "evil-speaking," often translated as reviling or verbal abuse</note> that <margin>1 Corinthians 5</margin> it declares one should not even take food with a slanderer. Peter also commands that whoever wishes to <margin>1 Peter 3</margin> love life and see good days must restrain their tongue from evil. And James says: <margin>James 4</margin> "Do not speak ill of one another, brothers." 

Thus we are taught by the Apostles on all sides to abstain from curses and the offending of anyone, which are the seeds of envy and discord—the very kind you recently sowed against me among the common people and the Prince. This occurred <margin>The hypocrisy of Catilinet</margin> shortly before this most recent Easter festival in Ghent, Flanders, in the presence of our most illustrious Prince <note>The "Prince" likely refers to the young Charles V, who held court in Ghent during this period</note>, and of the whole...

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page appears to be part of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s "Apologia" (Defense) against charges of heresy leveled by the friar Jean Catilinet. It continues a defense of his 1509 lectures at the University of Dole.</meta>

->HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...the art, by which (it is claimed) I despise the holy fathers and Catholic doctors and prefer the Jewish rabbis, twisting the sacred scriptures toward heretical arts and the Jewish Talmud. <margin>Agrippa’s Profession of Faith</margin> But in truth, I am a Christian; neither death nor life shall separate me from the faith of Christ. I prefer Christian doctors above all others, yet I do not despise the Jewish rabbis. Even if I should happen to err in what I am able to do, I nonetheless have no wish to be a heretic, nor do I intend to "Judaize" <note>To "Judaize" was a serious accusation in the 16th century, suggesting that a Christian was adopting Jewish religious practices or interpretations deemed incompatible with Church doctrine.</note>. I am so far from teaching damned and prohibited arts that I do not even wish to learn them. I never twist the sacred scriptures; rather, I take them up as testimony in various ways, according to the different interpretations of various doctors. <margin>Agrippa’s lectures in Dole, Burgundy, in the year 1509.</margin> I did not teach heretical arts or Jewish errors. Instead, with much labor and many sleepless nights, I explained the Christian and Catholic book titled *On the Mirific Word* <note>original: "De Verbo Mirifico." This was a famous work by the scholar Johannes Reuchlin that explored the "wonder-working" power of the name of God through a Christian interpretation of the Jewish Kabbalah.</note> by the Christian doctor Johannes Reuchlin of Pforzheim. I did this not secretly in private chambers, but in public schools, in a public auditorium, through public lectures which I gave for free in honor of the most illustrious Princess Margaret and the University of Dole. Nor was my audience lacking in grave and learned men, including the venerable fathers of the senatorial rank from the Parliament of Dole, as well as the most erudite masters, doctors, and ordinary lecturers of that university. Among them was the Reverend Lord Simon Vernerius, Vice-Chancellor of that university and conservator of the Church of Dole, a Dean and Doctor of both laws <term>Doctor of both laws: A prestigious degree indicating mastery of both Civil Law and the Church's Canon Law.</term>, who never missed a single one of my lectures. <margin>The malice of Catilinet <note>Jean Catilinet was a Franciscan provincial superior who preached against Agrippa before Princess Margaret, accusing him of being a "Jewish heretic."</note></margin> But you—to whom I am entirely unknown, who were never present at my lectures, nor heard me speaking anywhere else, even in private...

<summary>In this passage, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa defends himself against accusations of heresy, asserting his Christian faith while explaining that his public lectures in Dole were based on Johannes Reuchlin’s scholarship rather than "prohibited arts." He emphasizes that his teachings were public, free of charge, and attended by the city's highest intellectual and legal authorities.</summary>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s defense against a critic, specifically a Franciscan friar who has been attacking his reputation. Agrippa appeals to their shared faith to end their public dispute.</meta>

->### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...you have professed the rule <note>The "Rule" refers to the specific lifestyle and vows—poverty, chastity, and obedience—followed by members of the Franciscan Order.</note>, and you would have been of great use to us, and would have preserved the grace and favor of the Prince, and of others toward me. Therefore, I pray, spare me henceforth; let the insults and <margin>Agrippa’s Remonstrance</margin> detractions cease, let the talk that generates hatred and wounds charity <note>In this context, "charity" (Latin: caritas) refers to the Christian principle of selfless love for others.</note> cease; and those whom you have made hostile to me, exhort them again toward mutual benevolence and unity. Restore to me the integrity of my reputation; restore to me my good and innocent name; restore publicly what you have publicly taken away; restore to me what you have stolen by evil deceit and most wicked injury, lest at any time, before you are reconciled to me, your brother in Jesus Christ, you celebrate the divine mysteries of the Mass <note>Agrippa warns that performing or attending the Eucharist while in a state of unrepentant sin was considered a "sin against the Holy Spirit."</note> in an irremissible sin against the Holy Spirit, and partake of the body of Christ to your judgment of eternal damnation. 

By this most sacred body of Christ, I implore you: restore it. For we are both Christians and members <margin>Romans 12</margin> of Christ, as Paul says to the Romans: *One body in Christ* (to separate ourselves, therefore, and to cause discord, what else is it but to tear the body of Christ?). And you in this body are a great and principal member, you who are a doctor of theology and profess the rule of the blessed Francis. 

I also, as part of the same body of Christ, endeavor to be at least some small member, for I am a Christian, and I daily and willingly learn from great masters—such as you are one—those things which pertain to our religion, in which I undoubtedly take great delight. Let us therefore love one another in this, as the Apostle says: "The fulfillment of the law" <margin>Romans 13</margin> is nothing better than truth and charity. For to the Galatians...

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

<note>original: "DE COGNOS. DEO." - De Cognoscendo Deo</note>

...you think you see most clearly, it is precisely there that you are most blind to the very light of truth. Just as Christ spoke of having come into the world so that those who do not see might see, and those who see might become blind <note>A reference to John 9:39</note>, are you not wandering in blindness if you believe you are honoring the Holy Scriptures with these things <note>referring to pagan philosophical ideas</note>, by which they are instead defiled and polluted?

But how can you judge yourselves able to bring these things together with those, <margin>2 Corinthians 6</margin> since there is no agreement between Christ and Belial, between light and darkness, or between a believer and infidels? Are you not like a sick man who invites both a doctor and poisoners to his cure? While the latter can indeed do harm, they cannot heal; the former refuses to mix his medicines with their poisons, lest either the destruction be attributed to the good man, or the health be credited to the harmful ones. Do you not yet understand that while you wish to join these things together, this very act is the subversion of your salvation? Granted, Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretic system of religion and philosophy</note> is a weighty author; suppose you also include Plato and Plotinus...
