--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s critique of human wisdom, specifically contrasting the authority of ancient philosophers with that of Christian scripture.</meta>

->HENRI. COR. AGRIP.<-

The wise are said to have spoken, and while their words may carry some weight in refuting the vanity of the pagans, they are not sufficient for us to embrace their authority. For as great a distance as there is between the preaching of the angels and the confession of demons regarding the coming of Christ, so great is the distance between the authority of the Prophets and the sacrilegious curiosity of the heathens. If we must grant much to the authority of the most serious men, to whom, I ask, do you think more is owed? To Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note>, Plato, Plotinus, and similar philosophers? Relying on their own devised opinions and with darkened intellects, they failed to perceive the highest secrets of God and nature; they handed down things concerning God and divine matters that are neither supported by reason nor suggested by the Holy Spirit, but are instead certain ravings, unworthy even of sensible men. Or is more owed to Moses, David, Solomon, the prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles? These men, filled with the divine spirit and through every kind of holiness, oracles, miracles, and even the peril of their own lives and the testimony of their shed blood, proved their doctrines. If, having set these aside, you run back to the philosophers and labor to learn from ethnic <note>original: "ethnicis," referring here to non-Christian or pagan sources</note> masters of error those things which you ought to receive from Christ and the Holy Spirit, will you not be insane as well as impious? If, therefore, you are now Christians, learn the letters of Christ. Indeed, He himself prescribed this mark to his own in **John 14**: "He who loves me," he says, "hears my words." From Him, therefore, one must learn what is to be understood concerning God; for the justice of God is found not in the traditions of philosophers, but in the Gospel.

<summary>Agrippa argues that the authority of Christian prophets and apostles far outweighs that of pagan philosophers like Plato or Hermes Trismegistus, urging readers to seek divine truth in the Gospels rather than human traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Hermeticism, Neoplatonism, Biblical authority, Gospel vs Philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Plotinus</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

This most holy bond is so connected by an indissoluble glue <note>original: "indissolubili glutino"</note> and is so enduring that the husband always lives and exists in the wife, and the wife in the husband: as bone of his bones and flesh of his flesh. For that was the first precept of God when He instituted marriage: <margin>Genesis 2</margin> a man shall leave his father and his mother and shall cleave to his wife, and the two shall be in one flesh. We read this not only in the Old Testament, but it is also confirmed in the Gospel itself. <margin>Matthew 19</margin> Therefore, it is not permitted for this bond to be dissolved by any difference in behavior, <margin>Mark 10</margin> nor by any satiety or boredom, nor by old age, nor by infertility, nor by sickness, <margin>Ecclesiastical Law: Case 28, Question 7</margin> nor by pestilence, nor by leprosy <note>original: "elephantico," referring to elephantiasis or a form of leprosy</note>, nor by any other contagious disease; <margin>only.</margin> finally, it may not be dissolved by any crime of heresy, nor by any human power, <margin>Ecclesiastical Law: Case 28, Question 5, "If indeed..."</margin> as Christ commanded: that those whom the omnipotence of God has joined together, <margin>referring to the same; chapter "Horrendous"; 2 chapters following</margin> no power should presume to separate. Nor is it permitted for any reason <margin>Ecclesiastical Law: Case 27, Question 2, Chapter 6</margin> for a wife to depart from her husband, or a husband from his wife, because no one is allowed to depart from themselves, and no one is allowed to leave themselves behind. For she who was made from the man's rib—flesh of the same flesh, the same bone from his bones—to be a helper to the man, God never wished her to be separated from the man, except for the sake of fornication <note>sexual immorality</note>, which is the only cause God excepted. But if the covenant and unity of marriage is dissolved for any other cause, <margin>Ecclesiastical Law: Case 28, Question 7, "Intervening"</margin> those who are separated nevertheless are and remain spouses to each other; <margin>2 chapters following; Case 28, Question 5, "What God..."</margin> nor can they be excused from adultery if they are joined to others after their divorce. And therefore Christ adds, saying: "Whoever marries another commits adultery, and he who marries her who is put away commits adultery." <margin>Matthew 19</margin>

<summary>This page features Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s theological argument for the indissolubility of marriage, asserting that the union creates a permanent physical and spiritual oneness that cannot be broken by disease, age, or legal decree.</summary>

### BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

**N**ow we shall describe for what ends and uses this venerable and most worthy sacrament was commanded; for it exists for the sake of mutual assistance, for the propagation of offspring, and for the avoidance of sexual immorality. The first of these is held to be where God instituted this holy mystery with these words: <margin>Genesis 1</margin> <note>While the marginal note cites Genesis 1, this famous quote is actually found in Genesis 2:18.</note> *“It is not good for man to be alone; let us make him a helper like unto himself.”* For man—since he is, above all, a <term>social animal<note>original: "animal sociabile." A concept from Aristotelian philosophy suggesting that humans are naturally designed to live in partnership and community.</note></term>—will only then truly and rightly fulfill the duty of humanity, and provide a guardian for his life and his affairs, when he has entered into the stable and indissoluble fellowship of marriage.

And for that reason, even for the elderly, the decrepit, and those in whom no power of procreation or hope of lineage is left, it is nonetheless permitted to marry. Indeed—if it is proper to say so—it is often necessary, so that they may lead the old age of their life more pleasantly, more safely, and more securely in the company of a beloved wife. This is shown by the example of David, <margin>3 Kings, Chapter 1</margin> <note>In older Bibles, the books of Kings were numbered differently; "3 Kings" refers to what is now commonly called 1 Kings.</note> who, when already an old man, was joined with the Shunammite girl <note>Abishag the Shunammite, a young woman brought to the elderly King David to provide him with warmth and companionship (1 Kings 1:1-4).</note>. 

For what holier or more pleasant fellowship could occur among men? What could be safer? What more secure? What more chaste than that of a husband and wife, when each of them is the same as the other: two bodies in one conforming soul; one soul and one consensus in two bodies. Spouses alone do not envy one another; they alone love each other infinitely, since each of them depends entirely upon the other and rests in the other. They are one flesh, one mind, one concord; the same things are sad to them, the same things are pleasant; they share an equal will, a common gain of profit, the same riches, the same poverty, and equal dignity. They are always of one bed-chamber, and share their life together by night and by day...

<summary>In this opening section, Agrippa argues that marriage is a holy sacrament intended for companionship and mutual support, asserting that it is a vital social bond even for the elderly who can no longer have children.</summary>

<meta>This page continues an introductory exhortation to students of theology, likely from a Renaissance-era preface or educational treatise. It transitions from the necessity of a pure mind to the specific methodology of study.</meta>

->AN EXHORTATION TO THE STUDENTS OF THEOLOGY<-

For indeed, to reach a truthful knowledge of divine matters, there is need of a pure mind, one not infected by corrupt opinions. For if the mind is preoccupied by certain less-than-right opinions, then through those—as if under the guise of some kind of knowledge—the error of ignorance becomes more deeply rooted in it than can be easily pulled out. It then happens that a person believes himself to know that which he is actually ignorant of, and he defends as true and universal <note>original: "catholicum"</note> that which is in fact an error or a heresy.

Therefore, in your theological studies, nothing ought to come before the Gospel doctrine; you must enter by this path <margin>2 Thessalonians 2</margin>, even if you do not understand every single mystery to perfection <note>original: "ad unguem," literally 'to the fingernail,' a Latin idiom for flawless precision</note>. Next, apply the commentaries of the ancient Holy Fathers who have been received by the Church <margin>To Titus 3</margin>; after that, consult the modern authors <note>original: "neotericos," referring to more recent scholastic or contemporary theologians</note>. However, you must entirely avoid curious inquiries about God and divine things, the wrangling of questions, and the battles of disputations, according to the precept of the Apostle.

But when your mind has finally been strengthened and fortified by universal doctrine, then, by its light, you shall walk securely through all the shadows of error. Then you will be able to freely penetrate all kinds of disciplines. Then, like Ulysses, if you should enter the cave of the Cyclops or descend to the underworld, you shall return unharmed; if you are carried away to the Lotus-eaters or to the quicksands of the Syrtes, you shall return safe; if you drink the cup of Circe, you shall not be transformed. If you sail past Scylla, you shall not be endangered; if you hear the Sirens, you shall not fall asleep. Instead, you shall be "judging all things," as the Apostle says <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin>, "and judged by no one." At that point, the teachings of the pagans <note>original: "ethnicorum," referring to classical Greek and Roman authors</note> will be of the greatest help to you, and their...

To the REVEREND FATHER IN CHRIST, and lord doctor of sacred theology, Lord Theodoric, Bishop of Cyrene, and most worthy administrator in sacred matters for the suffragan see of the Archbishopric of Cologne, Henry Cornelius Agrippa commends himself.

# ON ORIGINAL SIN

[Large decorative initial D] Regarding original sin and the first transgression of man, various opinions are offered among the most learned and excellent interpreters of sacred letters, both ancient and modern. When I frequently revolved these in my mind, it seemed to me that they had written nothing but certain obscure and ambiguous things. Therefore, not out of contempt, but having left their opinions behind, I shall bring forward another new opinion of my own (merely as a matter for debate, and perhaps not false). It would be a sin if anyone else had thought the same before me; but if such a person exists, I do him no injury if I call this opinion my own, insofar as I have rescued it from the darkness of neglect by no other means than the diligence of my own wit and the examination of the scriptures. I have therefore produced a brief and concise declamation of my opinion, which I dedicate to your reverend and sincere paternity, so that by your authority it may emerge more secure from the future storm of attackers. For I know how great a storm of syllogisms <note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note> will rush upon it among certain "theophists" <note>A derogatory term Agrippa uses for scholastic theologians who prioritize logic over spiritual insight.</note>—among those, I say, with whom there is no room for simplicity, but only ambitious contention reigns. This material will not collapse, nor is it provoked by their syllogisms, provided it is protected by your support against Egyptian gnats <margin>Psalm 143 <note>Likely a reference to the Vulgate numbering; Agrippa compares his critics to the stinging plagues of Egypt.</note></margin> of this kind. Farewell.

I 3

**BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

But he is the true heir and the true son whose mother is married with the dignity of marriage to a certain and proven father, without the disgrace of sin, without the insult of relatives, and without offending God. Such a mother remains forever with her husband without infamy, bearing certain fruit <note>original: "certo cum fructu," referring here to children born within the legal and moral protections of a marriage</note>. Therefore, marriage alone makes children certain and bestows unquestionable heirs; it makes the union of nature <note>a period euphemism for sexual intercourse</note> honorable, and turns what could be a sin into something meritorious. It increases progeny, brings relatives together in harmony, and both pleases and reveres God.
<margin>Canon Law, Case 32, Question 5</margin>

[¶] I have described the third use of marriage as being for the avoidance of fornication <note>general sexual immorality</note>, following the instruction of the Apostle Paul, who says that he who cannot practice self-control should marry. For marriage excuses a person lacking self-control from sin, from fornication, and from adultery. Because of this need to avoid such lack of control, Paul even instructs widows to marry again, saying: "I want the younger widows to marry, to be heads of households, to procreate children, and to give the adversary no opportunity for slander."
<margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>
<margin>1 Timothy 5</margin>

However, the power and dignity of marriage is no less for those lacking self-control, even if they enter into it with less worthy motives. They are joined without fault, and the marital debt <note>original: "debitum," the historical religious obligation for spouses to provide sexual satisfaction to one another to prevent them from straying into sin</note> is paid without sin, even if the demand is immoderate, and the spouses join together for reasons beyond the procreation of children.
<margin>Canon Law, Case 27, Question 2</margin>
<margin>Canon Law, Case 32, Question 2</margin>

This is because marriage was instituted not only for companionship and the procreation of children, but also for the sake of supporting one another’s weaknesses, for tolerating unnecessary intercourse while avoiding what is unlawful. It is commanded for mutual service, as the Apostle says: "It is better to marry than to burn" <note>referring to burning with passion or desire</note>, and elsewhere he says of widows: "Lest on account of..."
<margin>Canon Law, Case 32, Question 2</margin>
<margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the moral requirements for marriage, transitioning from the consequences of sin to the essential requirement of free will and mutual love between spouses.</meta>

# ON THE SACRAMENT OF MATRIMONY

...they should repent) they are unworthy of the kingdom of God. No one, however, is to be forced by any violence into contracting a marriage. <margin>Canon Law: Case 31, Question 2, "If a journey"</margin> <margin>Rule 2, following chapters</margin> For since this bond is contracted by the consent of love alone, it ought to be especially free, because love itself is subject to no one's command. <margin>Case 31, Question 2, Chapter 6</margin> Neither do the gifts of the rich purchase love, nor does any status, or even nobility, equal love; no threats or violence from the powerful or from kings can compel love, which even God himself does not force, but created free from the very beginning.

Moreover, no love is as intense or as holy as that which exists between a husband and a wife. No force is greater than marital love <note>original: "uxoria charitate"; while literally "wifely charity," in this period it refers to the deep, reciprocal Christian love between spouses</note>, which is corrupted by no betrayal, shaken by no misfortune, distracted by no outside association, and consumed by no span of time or accident. And for this reason, there are divine commands <margin>Genesis 2</margin> <margin>Matthew 19</margin> that the love for a spouse should be placed above parents, children, brothers, sisters, kinsmen, in-laws, and all other worldly things.

Therefore, those parents, relatives, guardians, and caretakers sin most gravely who—looking not toward a lifelong goodwill, the bearing of children, or <term>chastity</term> <note>In the context of marriage, "chastity" refers to the "marriage bed undefiled," or sexual fidelity to one's spouse.</note>, but rather through greed and ambition, toward earthly status, nobility, power, wealth, and similar things—beyond the obedience owed to parents by divine command <margin>Exodus 20</margin>, through a kind of tyranny <margin>Judges 14</margin>, imprison the free consent of their sons and daughters (which is due to this <term>sacrament</term> <note>A sacred rite; the author argues that because marriage is a holy sacrament, it requires a holy, unforced entry</note>). <margin>Genesis 24</margin> They force them to undergo hateful marriages, having no regard for age, love, social standing, character, goodwill, or even the divine...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the corruption of the institution of marriage, likely from Agrippa's "De incertitudine et vanitate scientiarum" (On the Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences) or a similar polemic. ->HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...of the precept. From this at last are born among spouses fornications, adulteries, dissensions, insults, continuous rages, perpetual lawsuits, discords, hatreds, divorces, and infinite other evils. Sometimes even poisonings, killings, and violent deaths follow, so that such spouses seem to have been joined not by God, <margin>Tobit 6</margin> but by Satan himself. <note>original: "Tobi. 6." Agrippa references the Book of Tobit, where a demon kills the husbands of a woman who did not marry with the right spiritual intent.</note>

Added to this is that in many places several earthly princes and lords—acting under a Christian name, yet enemies of God, blasphemers of Jesus Christ, overturners of the church, contaminators of sacred things, and arrogating to themselves the things that are God’s—compel the marriages of their subjects by the whim of their own tyranny, and sometimes even by official mandate. Furthermore, they apply the tithes of dowries to their own treasury, which is a most wicked sacrilege; and so, while finally letting the adulterers go free, they punish those <margin>Matthew 21</margin> entering into marriage. In the end, the Just Judge will bring a wretched end to these wicked men. <note>original: "Matth. 21." This refers to the parable of the wicked tenants who are destroyed for their greed and violence.</note>

There is, moreover, another no less damnable custom which has grown up among many nations: that they pursue second marriages with various insults throughout the neighborhoods. Furthermore, they fine those marrying a second time a steep sum of money, which is then gathered to be devoured by a certain "fellowship" of theirs. For this nefarious insult against the divine mystery of marriage, they even claim Joseph, the husband of the most blessed Virgin Mary, as their patron. But the devil himself invented these fellowships or "fraternities"; and the wrath of God has given them over to <margin>Romans 1</margin> a reprobate mind, so that while they applaud adulteries, concubinage, and fornications, they persecute second marriages, and... <note>original: "Ad Roma. 1." Agrippa cites St. Paul’s letter to the Romans regarding those whom God abandons to their own corrupt judgment. The "fellowship" described is the tradition of the *charivari* or "rough music," where communities mocked remarriages.</note>

->### BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...unless you obtain an excellent wife—for a bad wife is not usually the lot of anyone but bad husbands—and similar children.

**N**or indeed, without a wife, can a name be extended, nor offspring perpetuated, nor a lineage increased, nor a family exist. Without her, one cannot be called a <term>paterfamilias</term> <note>The legal head of a Roman household; the term implies social status and authority that a single man could not fully claim.</note>, nor can a place be called a home, nor a republic stand, nor any empire endure. This fact was not hidden even from the founder of the Roman Empire <note>Romulus, the legendary first king of Rome.</note>, who, when he lacked wives and the Sabines refused him their daughters, undertook a most savage war. <note>Original: "cum Sabinis." This refers to the "Rape of the Sabine Women," a foundation myth where the first Romans abducted women from the neighboring Sabine tribe to ensure the survival of their city.</note> For he knew that this empire would not last at all if wives were lacking. 

For since a city consists of households, and a republic consists of private affairs, and is established from the discipline of governing domestic and family matters: how shall he rule a city who has not learned to rule a home? How shall he govern a republic who has never known private and family business? 

Hence Socrates testified that he had learned moral philosophy more from wives <note>Socrates' wife, Xanthippe, was famously described as having a difficult temperament; Socrates allegedly claimed that by learning to live with her, he could live with anyone.</note> than he had learned natural philosophy from Anaxagoras and Archelaus. <note>Anaxagoras and Archelaus were Pre-Socratic philosophers who focused on the physical nature of the universe rather than human ethics.</note> Indeed, marriage provides great exercise for moral philosophy. For it has a certain "domestic republic" attached to it, in the governing of which a man can easily learn and experience prudence, temperance, piety, and all the other virtues. Through these—in loving his wife, educating children, ruling the family, protecting his property, governing the home, and propagating offspring—he may lead the happiest life of all. Furthermore, to look into the eyes of a beloved wife, and through children and the successions of family and name...

<summary>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa argues that marriage is the foundational unit of society, asserting that one cannot be a capable political leader without first learning to govern a household. He cites the Roman founder Romulus and the philosopher Socrates to illustrate that domestic life is the primary school for civic virtue.</summary>

# ON THE SACRAMENT OF MATRIMONY

->[of having a wife] is the greatest joy and happiness, and the sweet consolation of labors. He who lacks a wife has no home, because he does not establish a permanent household; and if he does have a house, he stays in it like a stranger in an inn. He who has no wife (even if he be very wealthy) has almost nothing that is truly his own: because he has nothing safe from treachery, nor does he have anyone to whom he can leave his legacy, or whom he can trust. He who has no wife lacks a family, lacks relatives, and without hope of offspring, he is always deserted and destitute: servants rob him, partners defraud him, neighbors despise him, friends neglect him, <margin>Wisdom 4 / Code on natural children / although it is permitted / 32nd Case, 4th Question, "someone may say" / The Abbot on the chapter "Venerabilem," concerning who are legitimate / Baldus on the law regarding legitimate marriages, in the Digest on the status of men / Code to the Orphitian decree, law: "if any illustrious woman"</margin> <note>These marginal notes cite various sources of Canon and Civil law, including the Book of Wisdom, the Decretum of Gratian, and legal commentaries by medieval jurists like Panormitanus (the Abbot) and Baldus.</note> relatives lie in wait for his estate. If he has any <term>natural sons</term> <note>original: "filios... naturales"; in this legal context, this refers to children born out of wedlock who lacked full inheritance rights.</note>, they are a source of disgrace to him and sharers in his iniquity; he can neither leave them his wealth nor his family name. But just as he remains in life without marriage, so when dead he perishes without a name; nor does he deserve to receive any goodwill in this life (which is the greatest consolation to mortals). Rather, as a deserter of human nature, he is worthy of every calamity, and is considered the most unhappy of all by everyone, and is deservedly abandoned. 

For this reason, Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta, known for his austere social reforms.</note> passed a law that those who neglected to take a wife at the proper time should be barred in summer from the games, the spectacles, and all comfort and pleasant company. In winter, however, they were to be led naked around the forum, subjected to insults, and loathed by all men. They were forced to confess they were suffering these things justly because they held religion in contempt, did not obey the laws, and transgressed against nature. Insofar as it was in their power, they prevented the state, the empire, and religion from standing—since they did not strive to procreate those by whom these institutions ought to be protected and propagated. <meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the inherent corruption of the flesh and the spiritual consequences of sexual generation, drawing on both Hermetic and Biblical sources.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

Therefore, that which is born of the flesh was corruptible and mortal flesh, participating in nothing of the intellect from the one who generates it, but only in carnal sensibility. For only the sensible and carnal soul comes from the progenitor; into this soul—already infected by the flesh—the intellect created by God is simultaneously immersed and suffocated. Hence, it is necessary that all who are begotten of the flesh, and who themselves beget through the propagation of the flesh and the senses, contract original guilt and corruption. <margin>Poimandres, chapter 1</margin> This is also what Hermes <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note> shows in the *Poimandres* in a sufficiently long discourse: namely, that man fell from divine contemplation into the nature of the generation of the world's mortal and irrational living creatures; whence he himself brought forth an irrational form, perceiving the evils of death through his senses.

But these things which have already been said might perhaps seem to have been explained by violently twisting the scriptures to strengthen our opinion, unless we bring forward still other testimonies, by which it may be more clearly seen <margin>Genesis 3</margin> that the mingling of the flesh was displeasing to God. This is now shown first from the curse of childbirth: that she who conceived a child in the pleasure of the flesh should bring it forth in pain. It is as if it were said: <margin>Psalm 7</margin> "Behold, he has conceived injustice, he has been in labor with sorrow, and has brought forth iniquity; he opened a pit and dug it out, and fell into the ditch which he made; let his sorrow be turned back upon his own head, and let his iniquity descend upon his own crown." <note>Agrippa applies the imagery of Psalm 7:14-16 to the act of procreation and the Fall</note> Added to this is a certain indignation of nature, since neither is a virgin overpowered <note>original: "opprimitur," likely referring to the loss of virginity</note> nor does a woman give birth without blood. But let us see what followed. Adam begat two sons: the first, Cain, <margin>Genesis 4</margin>

The contents of this discourse, as our chaplain wrote to me, have offended several people—men who nevertheless wish to be counted among the number of the learned. 

Truly, I do not see—perhaps being blind in my own affairs as is usually the case—what they are able to find so much to accuse in a free declamation. One point is that I seem to assert that spouses who have been separated because of adultery are able to marry others. I responded to him that this was not my intention; rather—as the words before and after that passage show—I am discussing the unity of marriage. According to the words of the Lord, marriage consists in the indivisible union of the flesh. I claim that this unity cannot be dissolved by any cause for divorce except for sexual immorality alone, for in that act the flesh is already divided among several partners.

Furthermore, if anyone should press me further regarding a different interpretation, I told him that I know those who also defended this view: Origen, Ambrose, Gratian <note>The 12th-century monk who compiled the *Decretum*, the foundational text of Canon Law</note>, and many legal experts, as well as Pollentius. <margin>Canon Law: Case 32, Question 7, chapter "Quid"</margin> Saint Augustine disputed against Pollentius on this matter (though without accusing him of the crime of heresy), and Pollentius, having published further books, defended his own opinion so effectively that it is difficult to judge who won the debate. <margin>In all things / section: Wife, etc.</margin> <margin>Some</margin>

->There is a seed for him, once the son of the slave woman—the son of the flesh—had been cast out. This is Isaac,<- <margin>Genesis 25</margin> who was finally fathered by a sterile wife. And when Moses had seen that great vision and the Lord speaking in the bush, <margin>Exodus 3</margin> he was by no means able to approach until he had loosened the strap of his sandal, which signifies the bond of marriage in the Holy Scriptures. ->To his friend **JOHN CAPELLANUS**,<-
->Royal Physician, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa sends greetings.<-

**S**<note>The original text begins with a decorative woodcut initial 'S'.</note>o you write to me, my dearest Capellanus, that there are some in the court—who are nevertheless counted among the number of the learned, and who frequently converse with the great ladies—who do not much approve of my declamation on marriage. Therefore, fearing that this matter might lead to dispute rather than commendation for me, you have so far delayed presenting those little books of mine to our princes until you could consult me again. 

Hear then now my opinion: among the masters of the court, there are those who write foul and filthy jokes, and comedies about the art of pimping, and lewd poems drawn from the innermost cabinets of Venus <note>original: "Veneris armarijs"; a metaphor for secret or private collections of erotic material</note>, as well as pestilential books about wars and loves. There are also those who translate these into various languages. 

But books of this kind are offered to ladies without offense or blame, and are read greedily even by young girls: Boccaccio’s *Novellas* <note>original: "Nouellæ Bocatij"; referring to the Decameron, often criticized for its bawdy content</note>, the *Jests* of Poggio <note>original: "facetiae Pogij"; a collection of humorous and often crude anecdotes by Poggio Bracciolini</note>, the adulteries of Euryalus and Lucretia <note>referring to 'The Tale of Two Lovers', a popular erotic epistolary novel by Aeneas Silvius Piccolomini, who later became Pope Pius II</note>, the wars and loves of Tristan and Lancelot, and similar things. In these, women grow accustomed to wickedness by reading; she who is most highly educated in these books, who knows their jokes by heart, and who, following their instruction, can talk eloquently with suitors for many hours—she at last is called a "properly courtly woman." Wherefore I wonder that wise...

<summary>In this letter to the Royal Physician Jean Capellanus, Agrippa defends his treatise on marriage against courtly critics. He points out the hypocrisy of a court that condemns his serious work while celebrating "obscene" and "pestilential" popular literature like Boccaccio and Arthurian romances.</summary>

...those most sharp-nosed <term>censors</term> <note>original: "nasutissimos censores." In Latin, having a "big nose" was a common metaphor for being overly critical or mocking.</note>, who often stir up such great tragedies over small matters. It is remarkable that they do not merely ignore such things, but are even deeply occupied in reading, translating, and explaining them—even leaders of religion and bishops, such as that man from Angoulême <note>This refers to Octavien de Saint-Gelais (1468–1502), the Bishop of Angoulême, who famously translated Ovid’s erotic poetry, the *Heroides*, into French.</note> who converted the amorous letters of Ovid's *Heroes* into the French tongue. Courtly ladies often have such men as the ministers of their sacred rites. Since these men have never learned from any good authors, nor possess even a tiny bit of true learning, how could they possibly approve of these works of mine, which are so far removed from their own way of life? 

To you, therefore, I now boldly offer these little books. Do not think your Agrippa—a name which many interpret as "sickly feet" <note>original: "ægris pedibus." Agrippa is playing on a popular (though likely incorrect) Renaissance etymology of his own name to make a joke about his ability to stand his ground.</note>—is so crippled by <term>gout</term> <note>original: "podagricum." Gout was a painful joint disease often associated in the Renaissance with a wealthy or sedentary scholarly lifestyle.</note> that he does not dare to descend into the arena with those "wise" courtly censors. I am not so poorly armed in the best literature that I am unable both to defend my own works and, at the same time, to run through my enemies and adversaries. 

Farewell most happily. From Lyon, the first of May, in the year 1526.

# THE BOOK OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
*On the Threefold Way of Knowing God.*
CHAPTER ONE.

The Lord of Eternity, the beginning, middle, and end of all things, the renewal, the fountain of piety, the origin of justice, the Father, and Goodness itself—Almighty God—by His most excellent <note>original: *bonissima*; Agrippa uses a superlative form of "good" to emphasize God's supreme nature.</note> (if I may say so) will, for His own infinite glory, created all things good. He did this so that all things might glorify Him and act in a holy and incorruptible manner regarding His honor. <margin>Genesis 1</margin> He established for all things a specific end and a beautiful order within prescribed limits, which He forbade them to cross. 

This was the will of God in His creation. God created the angels, the heavens, the stars, the elements, <margin>Genesis 1</margin> plants, and living creatures—the four-footed beasts, reptiles, those of the water, and those of the air. As the prince and the ultimate purpose <term>end</term> <note>In classical philosophy, the "end" (*finis*) refers to the goal or the highest purpose for which something exists.</note> of all these, He created man in His own image, rejoicing over him as a son. 

Then, first among all, the angelic nature—not content with its own high status and ambitiously seeking even higher things—set itself in opposition to God and said: "I will ascend into heaven; <margin>Isaiah 14</margin> I will exalt my throne above the stars of God; I will sit upon the mount of the covenant in the sides of the north; I will ascend above the heights..." <note>Agrippa is quoting the traditional prideful boast of Lucifer from Isaiah 14:13-14, which explains the origin of evil as a rejection of God's established order.</note>

<summary>In this opening chapter, Agrippa describes the divine order of creation, where God establishes a "beautiful order" for all things, culminating in the creation of humanity as his "son." The passage concludes by describing the first disruption of this order: the prideful rebellion of the angels.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Theology, Creation, Divine Order, Fall of Lucifer, Genesis, Isaiah, Angelology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa's discourse on the origins of evil, specifically moving from the rebellion of Satan to the subsequent fall of humanity in the Garden of Eden. It likely follows a section describing the pride of the fallen angels.</meta>

->HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...[above the height] of the clouds, and I will be like the Most High. <note>Agrippa is quoting Isaiah 14:14, a passage traditionally interpreted as the boast of Lucifer before his fall.</note> Satan was the leader of this ambition; he was the first transgressor of the divine will. Because of such great pride and injustice, he was cast out from heaven and thrown down into this infectious valley <note>original: *vallem contagiosam*, a metaphor for the earthly realm as a place of spiritual and physical decay</note>, where from then on he lives miserably with his hateful army, hostile to all. He does not cease to propagate his own injustice within God's creation, persisting stubbornly in such great pride. Nor does he wish to acknowledge his own sin, but instead he never stops constantly accusing God Himself for his own sin. For this reason, in Greek he is called *diabolos*, which means "the accuser" <note>original: *criminator*; Agrippa is explaining the etymology of the word "Devil."</note>. In him began all injustice, iniquity, malice, death, and deformity; and from him proceeds every evil, and nothing but evil. 

But man, created on the earth and placed in paradise so that he might obey the divine will— <margin>Genesis 2 & 3</margin> by which he had been gifted with both wisdom and eternal life—was targeted by the devil with a hostile temptation. By listening to him, man likewise became a transgressor of the divine will. For which reason he also was driven out from this garden of delights <note>original: *orto* (garden)</note> into this valley of misery and ignorance, and was made subject to death, dying at every hour and being neglected. And then, with the knowledge of God neglected, the fountain of sins was opened; crimes flowed forth, the companions of darkness slipped in, and man, ignoring God, was ignored by God. Abandoning the knowledge of God, he was abandoned by God—as the Apostle Paul says— <margin>Romans 1</margin> [given up] to his own desires, to shameful passions, and to a depraved mind <note>A reference to Romans 1:24-28, describing the moral decline of those who turn from God.</note>. Thus he was corrupted, having become abominable in all his pursuits. You see now how...

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...because ignorance of God is the source of all evils, and the origin of all sins, and the root of crimes, and the tree of destruction, and the height of impiety and injustice, through which all vices grow strong and increase. This <note>ignorance</note> perverts the soul itself, corrupts nature, subverts man, and entangles him in the most shameful sins; it plunges the body itself into every deformity and insult to nature, in which the soul, simultaneously steeped <note>original Latin: *madens*, meaning dripping or soaked</note>, is suffocated, and men are transformed into the nature of wild beasts and the habits of brutes; they often suffer worse things than even the irrational animals. From this, they are cast headlong into the shameful allurements of the senses and the deadly filth of sins; they fall into every outrage and perversion of nature, and are subjected to the command of desires <margin>*Poimandres*, Chapter 12 <note>A core text of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, traditionally attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note></margin>, toward the fulfillment of which (as Hermes says) they are carried forth by a certain burning impulse, and in the immoderate manner of wild beasts, they are both angry and they lust; and what is worse, they set no limit to their lust, nor do they find an end to their evil passions. Paul clearly teaches all of these things in his Epistle to the Romans <margin>Romans 1 & 3</margin>. For this reason, unclean spirits—the avengers of crimes—slip into such a wicked soul and lash it with the whips of sins. By violent punishment, they drag, seize, and compel it toward murders, robberies, lusts, and every means by which men transgress. They wound the soul with incurable vices, so that, tainted by these as if infected by poisons, it swells up. Regarding this, the grieving Hermes exclaims <margin>*Asclepius*, 9 <note>The *Asclepius* is a Latin dialogue describing the end of Egyptian religion</note></margin>: "Let there be a lamentable withdrawal of the gods from men; [only the harmful angels will remain]..."

## CHAPTER TWO.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how God reveals Himself through different "books" or stages of revelation, transitioning from the Hebrew prophets to the Christian Gospel, and finally beginning a philosophical inquiry into knowing God through the natural world.</meta>

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<margin>Psalm 147</margin> God made the book of the law and of divine utterances, which he gave to the Jews, announcing his word to Jacob, and his justice and judgments to the people of Israel. He has not done so for every nation, and he has not made his judgments manifest to them <note>original: "Non fecit taliter omni nationi, & iudicia sua non manifestauit eis"</note>; for the Jews themselves possessed prophets who were more highly instructed than the philosophers, being taught by spiritual and angelic creatures, and through these they came to know God.

<margin>Acts 7</margin> For this reason, Stephen the first martyr said to them: "You who have received the law through the arrangement of angels" <note>original: "qui accepistis legem in dispositione angelorū"</note>. <margin>Dionysius</margin> And Dionysius <note>Likely Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher highly regarded in the Middle Ages and Renaissance for his writings on the celestial hierarchy</note> says that the glorious seers of the Hebrews achieved prophecy and divine vision through the mediation of celestial powers. From this, the entire school of the Kabbalists <term>Cabalistarum: practitioners of Kabbalah, a Jewish mystical and esoteric tradition that seeks to understand the relationship between the eternal God and the mortal universe</term> teaches that the primary intention of the Law is concerned only with the angelic choir, and that the sublime and ineffable Trinity of the divine essence would remain unknown until the coming of the Messiah. 

Finally, therefore, God sent us a third book, namely the book of the Gospel given to Christians, we who have known God through the very Son of God—co-eternal with the Father, made man, our Lord Jesus Christ. For this reason, Paul says: "In these last days, God has spoken to us through his Son, whom he appointed heir of all things, through whom he made all things" <note>original: "nouissime diebus istis locutus est nobis deus in filio suo..."</note>, and we have teachers and apostles who were taught by the Son of God, Jesus Christ.

**N**ow, therefore, let us examine each point in detail, and first let us see how God may be known through created things <note>This refers to "Natural Theology," the idea that one can find evidence of God by observing the design and beauty of the physical world</note>. However, we should not understand this to mean that we can know God here as he is in his ultimate and solitary nature...

->### ON KNOWING GOD<-

...by a voluntary separation of himself from all things and a drawing back into himself, nor can we discern what his substance is in the deepest retreat of his divinity. For this is impossible and incomprehensible to all intellect. Therefore the Apostle says that God **dwells in unapproachable light**, <margin>1 Timothy 6</margin> and the prophet says: **He made darkness his hiding place.** <margin>Psalm 17 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 18:11.</note></margin> And <margin>John 1</margin> John says: **No one has seen God, nor can see him.** <margin>1 John 4</margin> And <margin>Dionysius, *On the Divine Names*</margin> Dionysius says: Furthermore, no sense reaches, no substance and no knowledge penetrates those divine things themselves—whatever they are like in their own principle and their own seat. Finally, whether we call that hidden reality <term>supersubstantial</term> <note>original: "supersubstantiale"; a philosophical term for something that exists beyond the category of "substance" or "being."</note>, or call it God, or life, or substance, or light, or the Word, we understand nothing other than the <term>participations</term> <note>original: "participationes"; the philosophical idea that created things reflect or "share in" the qualities of God.</note> and powers emanating from him into us, by which we are taken up into God, and which bestow upon us either substance, or life, or wisdom.

Therefore, let us know God through certain participations emanating from him into those things which have been created. By understanding these through a certain "reflection," so to speak, we may know God—or as Hermes says: <margin>*Asclepius*, book 2</margin> It happens to us humans that we see those things which are in heaven as if through a mist, as much as is possible through the condition of human sense. This effort of seeing through to such great goods is very narrow [at first], but it becomes very wide when you see it through the happiness of conscience. Therefore, because of a certain participation in God within his creatures, God himself can be sensed and searched for: for God shines everywhere through individual created things. And he freely shows himself to all, as Mercury <note>The author refers to Hermes Trismegistus, often identified with the god Mercury.</note> says.

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s exploration of how God is known through the created world, drawing heavily from the Hermetic tradition and Neoplatonism.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

<margin>Asclepius, chapter 10</margin> ...[God is] not found in a specific place, nor defined by a specific quality, nor measured by quantity, but he illuminates man through the intelligence of the mind alone. <margin>Pimander, chapter 2</margin> And elsewhere he says: God crafted all things for this very reason, so that you might perceive Him through every individual thing. This is the goodness of God, this is His power: that He shines through all things. There is nothing, even among incorporeal or invisible things, that does not reflect Him. The mind is seen in the act of <term>intellection</term><note>The process of understanding or using the intellect, as opposed to physical sensation.</note>, but God is witnessed in His works. <margin>Pimander, chapter 5</note> Therefore, <term>Mercurius</term><note>Also known as Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many mystical and "Hermetic" texts were attributed.</note> says in another place: "Finally, when you wish to see God, look up at the sun, my son; observe the courses of the moon; look up at the motions of the remaining stars. Who maintains their perpetual order? Who assigns the measure to each individual motion? Who moves the machinery of the world? Who uses this instrument? Who circumscribed the sea within its boundaries? Who steadies and balances the weight of the earth? In the midst of these things, there is an author and master of them through His art." Whence also <margin>Dionysius, On the Divine Names</margin> <term>Dionysius</term><note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher whose work bridged the gap between Greek philosophy and Christian mysticism.</note> says: "Perhaps we speak truly when we say that we do not know God from His own nature—for that is indeed unknown and surpasses all reason and sense—but we know Him from the most orderly arrangement of all creatures produced by Him. As these creatures present certain images and likenesses of His own divine <term>exemplars</term><note>The ideal forms or patterns in the mind of God according to which all things were created.</note>, we climb toward that which transcends all things, following a path and order according to our strength." To this height the philosophers of the nations <note>Referring to the "Gentile" or non-Christian philosophers, such as the Greeks.</note> ascended by the mere apprehension of creatures; that is, they embraced through their own intelligence all things created on earth, in the waters, in the elements, in heaven, and even those things that are above the heavens. At last, they arrived at the first mover, and the true [source of] all...

->**ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**<-

...the first principle, and through the intellect they saw God the almighty, the one, the eternal, the creator of all, and the highest good, as well as his everlasting power, and divinity, goodness, wisdom, truth, justice, beauty, and the rest, which Paul calls the <term>invisible things of God</term><note>original: "inuisibilia dei"; referring to the theological concept that God's hidden nature can be understood through his visible creations.</note>. For they saw God in heaven and in earth, in fire, <margin>Romans 1</margin> in water, in spirit, in animals, in trees, in every body, and in all creatures, just as Lucan sings: *Jupiter is whatever you see, wherever <margin>Lucan</margin> you move.* Virgil also sings among the cattle: *All things are full of Jove.* <margin>Virgil</margin> Hence Hermes says: *man was made a contemplator of divine <margin>Pimander 4</margin> works,* which, while he truly admired them, he recognized their author. For he who easily perceives each of His works with the mind, knows God easily and completely (as far as human readiness allows). Therefore, every human can know God, if they so wish. Because of this, the person who is ignorant of God is without excuse, as is everyone who, submerging their soul into the body, despairs of being able to know God. But let this impiety be far from you; return into yourself, emerge from the body, assume nothing in yourself is impossible, have confidence and you will understand, wish it and you will achieve it. In this way, finally, you may know God, if you do not despair of yourself. For the human soul (as Hermes says) grasps all things, penetrates all things, mixes with the elements with speed, and with the sharpness of the mind, it descends into the depths <margin>Asclepius 3</margin> of the sea; all things shine for it, and heaven does not seem too high, for it reaches out as if from close proximity by its sagacity...

It emphasizes that ignorance of the divine is a choice, as the soul has the inherent capacity to transcend the body and perceive the creator.</summary>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

[The soul] gazes upon everything. No darkness of the air confuses its focus, no density of the earth hinders its work, and no deep altitude of water dulls its sight. <margin>Pimander, Book 2</margin> <note>Agrippa is quoting from the *Pimander* (or *Poimandres*), a foundational text of Hermeticism attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus. It describes the mind's ability to transcend physical limits.</note> And elsewhere he says: command your soul, and it will fly forth faster than you can command it. Command it to cross over to the Ocean; before you have finished the command, it will be there. From there where it is now, without departing at all, command it again to fly to heaven; it will need no wings, nothing will obstruct its course—not the fire of the sun, nor the vastness of the ether, nor the turning of the heavens. Not even the bodies of the other stars will stop it; instead, penetrating all things, it may transcend even to the highest body. Moreover, if you wish to transcend all the spheres of the heavens themselves and investigate what lies above, that too will be permitted to you. 

Notice now how great is the power of the soul, how great its <term>virtue</term> <note>The Latin *virtus* here refers to an inherent power or functional excellence, rather than just moral goodness.</note>, and how great its speed: for this reason, man is inexcusable if he is ignorant of God. But even more so is he who, knowing God in whatever way, does not worship or venerate Him. For this is a most hateful and inexcusable impiety, which Paul, reproaching the nations <note>original: *gentibus*, referring to the Gentiles or non-Christian peoples of antiquity.</note>, speaks of thus: <margin>Romans 1</margin> "so that they are without excuse, because when they knew God, they did not glorify him as God, nor gave thanks." For the philosophers of the nations, knowing various disciplines—Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, Astronomy, <term>Physics</term> <note>In the 16th century, "Physics" referred to "Natural Philosophy," the study of the physical workings of the entire natural world.</note>, Metaphysics, Dialectic, and the rest—knew through their science the one, sole, and true God; but the impious and ungrateful, turned away from pure and holy knowledge despite such a great benefit, [created] a certain false image... <note>The text cuts off here at "ima-", likely "imagination" or "image" (imaginem).</note>

<summary>Agrippa uses Hermetic texts to describe the soul's limitless power to transcend the physical world, arguing that because the soul can "see" God through the sciences, those who fail to worship Him are without excuse.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, soul, Hermeticism, Pimander, Hermes Trismegistus, Romans 1, natural philosophy, liberal arts, impiety, transcendence</keywords>

->ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...[ima]ge <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "image," continuing from the previous page.</note> with reckless thought, having looked upon no true reason; following this, they did not worship Him as the one and only true God, nor did they give thanks to Him who enlightened them to recognize His divinity. For this reason, all those people will be condemned for impiety, injustice, and ingratitude, according to the words of Paul, who says: *For the wrath of God is revealed from heaven against all impiety and injustice of men* <margin>Romans 1 & 2</margin>, for impiety is a sin against God, and injustice is against men; moreover, the extreme form of impiety is not to know God. From impiety comes intemperance, and this is the foundation of injustice. Indeed, we call intemperance the depravity of the will arising from the slumber of reason, where the sensual impulse dominates excessively—where, namely, reason is lulled to sleep, and all things are driven by the command of the senses. Hence this barren soul is said to produce no good fruit in its time, and this is the soul’s greatest impiety: to be sterile. Concerning this, Mercury <note>The author uses the name Mercury for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the "Hermetica" texts.</note> says: *Impiety befalls him who departs from life without children* <margin>Pimander 2 <note>Referring to the "Poimandres," the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum.</note></margin>, for which reason he suffers punishments from demons after death; but we know God in vain unless we worship Him rightly and live lawfully among men. <margin>Pimander 2</margin> Hence Hermes says: *The struggle of religious piety is to recognize God and to do harm to no one,* which is also what Christ commands, saying: *Love the Lord your God, and your neighbor as yourself.* <margin>Matthew 22. Mark 12. Luke 10.</margin> These two precepts are necessary for salvation and are the source of all good: the first of these belongs to piety, the other to justice. On the contrary, impiety and injustice [are the source] of all <note>The text breaks off here at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page explores the moral and spiritual consequences of failing to know God, blending Christian scripture with Hermetic philosophy to define impiety as a lack of spiritual "fruit" or children. It argues that true piety requires both the recognition of God and just behavior toward other people.</summary>

<keywords>impiety, injustice, Hermes Trismegistus, Apostle Paul, knowledge of God, intemperance, salvation, Poimandres, Great Commandment</keywords>

->### [The Works] of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa<-

...are the root of all evils. Upon these the wrath of God is revealed from heaven—specifically upon those who pollute and violate the truth of God within themselves. These are the people who do not love God in their understanding, who bear no fruit in their knowledge, who are not devout in their wisdom, and who do not help their fellow man through their prudence. But now, following this, let us speak of the secondary knowledge of God, which is found through the book of the law.

### CHAPTER FOUR

The second book was given to the Jews: the book of the law, which was placed openly before all, and the book of divine utterances <term>eloquiorum: refers to the "Logia" or divine oracles</term> which was handed down only to the wise. For they were the first people with whom God spoke in many different ways through his angels, and to whom the oracles and secrets of God were given, as the Psalmist says: 
<margin>Psalm 147</margin> 
"He has not done so for every nation, nor has he revealed his judgments to them." <note>original Latin: "Non fecit taliter omni nationi, & iudicia sua nō manifestauit illis."</note>

It is well established, however, by the opinions of both the Hebrew masters and the Christian doctors, that Moses himself—the great lawgiver of the Hebrews—received more than just the law which God gave him on Mount Sinai (and which he left behind written and contained in five books). It is held that God also revealed to Moses the true explanation of the law, manifesting all the mysteries and secrets that are hidden beneath the "bark" <term>cortice: the outer shell or literal surface of a text, as opposed to its spiritual "pith"</term> and the rough appearance of the law's words. Thus we read that God said to Ezra: 
<margin>4 Ezra 14</margin> 
"I revealed myself clearly in the bush and spoke to Moses when my people were serving in Egypt; I sent him and led them out of Egypt, brought him up to Mount Sinai, and kept him with me for many days, and..."

<summary>Agrippa introduces the "secondary knowledge of God" found in the Jewish Law, arguing that Moses received both a public written text and a secret, mystical interpretation of that law directly from God.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Mount Sinai, Law of God, 4 Ezra, Hebrew masters, divine mysteries, revelation, Jewish law</keywords>

# CONCERNING THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

I told him many wonders, and I showed him the secrets of
the times and the end, and I commanded him, saying: "These words
you shall make public, and these you shall hide." <note>This quote is from the apocryphal book of 2 Esdras (4 Ezra) 14:5–6, where God speaks to Moses about the hidden nature of certain revelations.</note> It is therefore well established
that Moses received a twofold law on the mountain, namely, a literal
and a spiritual one; and in accordance with God’s command, he
communicated both to the Jewish people. The former, it is clear, he
placed openly in writing for the common people, but the other he
shared only with seventy wise men. This was done neither in writing,
nor so that they themselves might write it down, but by the living voice <note>original: *viva voce*; referring to oral instruction as opposed to written text</note>,
and in such a way that each of them might reveal it in a continuous
order to their successors by the living voice. Because of this
successive tradition of the living voice, it is called the science
of the oracles, which the Hebrews call <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>Literally "reception." Here it refers to the Jewish mystical tradition believed to have been passed down orally from Moses.</note>,
due to its being received from one to another as if by a
right of inheritance. This opinion is also supported by that of Hilary <margin>Hilary on Psalm 2.</margin> <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent early Christian bishop and theologian.</note>
in his exposition of the Psalm: *Why did the nations rage?* <note>The opening line of Psalm 2.</note>
Where Hilary says that it was established by Moses that in
every synagogue there were to be seventy elders, to whom Moses

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's discourse on the mystical traditions of the Kabbalah, specifically focusing on the philosophical justifications for the use of divine names as found in the works of Maimonides and Nahmanides.</meta>

-># **OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

<margin>Rabbi Moses the Egyptian <note>This refers to Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and jurist.</note></margin>
And Rabbi Moses, in the second treatise of the *Guide* <note>original: "moræ," referring to Maimonides' major work, *Moreh Nevukhim* or *The Guide for the Perplexed*.</note>, says that the entire concern of the Law <note>The Torah or divine law.</note> consists in this: that it teaches truthful opinions concerning God and the choirs of angels, by which men, being so instructed, may know even the world itself in its proper order. Therefore, the principal learning of the prophetic <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>Agrippa uses "Cabalæ," the mystical Jewish tradition of scriptural interpretation and divine magic.</note> concerns those knowable things which can be understood regarding God and the angels. From this source, it teaches that the multiform sacred names of both God and the angels are to be invoked, and it enumerates various physical acts through which men—made as if similar to the gods—ascend to the lights of the eternal Father by conforming themselves to divine things through certain degrees. Being filled with these lights, they attain a knowledge of God that goes beyond the ordinary custom of nature. For the invoked sacred names work more powerfully upon our mind (when it is properly prepared for them) than any physical body works upon another when it comes into contact with it—much like fire acting upon <term>flax</term> <note>original: "stuppam," the coarse part of flax or hemp used as tinder; here a metaphor for immediate, powerful ignition or transformation.</note>.

Furthermore, the Hebrew Law possesses this spark of divinity: that besides the multiform names of God and the angels hidden there, not even a single <term>element</term> <note>In this context, "elementum" refers to the individual letters of the Hebrew alphabet, which Kabbalists believe hold cosmic power.</note> passes by without some prophetic mystery. By the <term>revolving</term> <note>A reference to the Kabbalistic practice of *temurah* or *gematria*, where letters are rearranged or substituted to find hidden meanings.</note> of these letters according to the rules of the Kabbalists, stupendous oracles are often revealed. 

<margin>Rabbi Moses the Egyptian</margin>
Whence Rabbi Moses says in his second book: Through the divine names and the words of the prophets—by transposing the order of the letters and by other unusual <term>signs</term> <note>original: "signaculis," referring to mystical marks, vowel points, or scribal flourishes in the Torah scrolls.</note>—the great oracles of divine wisdom are often determined. 

<margin>Rabbi Moses [the 29th?]</margin>
Which also the other Moses of Gerona <note>Moses ben Nahman, also known as Nahmanides (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar and Kabbalist.</note> in the beginning of his commentary on Genesis, and the entire school of the elder Kabbalists confirm; for indeed, the powers of God are present even more closely within the divine names.

<summary>This page discusses the Kabbalistic belief that the Hebrew Law and divine names contain spiritual power capable of elevating the human mind to a divine state. Agrippa cites prominent Jewish scholars like Maimonides and Nahmanides to justify the mystical practice of rearranging Hebrew letters to uncover hidden prophecies.</summary>

->### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...and prophetic characters, than in any physical body of the world. Therefore, Dionysius says <margin>Dionysius, *On the Divine Names*</margin> <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th to early 6th century.</note> that through the veneration and contemplation of these things we may more easily ascend to God the Father, and be made partakers of His radiance more than through the observation of natural things and created beings. We have written extensively and deeply on this matter in the book *On Occult Philosophy*, <margin>Book 3 of *Occult Philosophy*</margin> <note>This refers to the author Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s own famous work, *De Occulta Philosophia libri tres*.</note> in that section where the mysteries and ceremonies of hidden operations are discussed. 

But it is enough for us to know here that the knowledge of the Jews concerning God was much more sublime and perfect through the Law than that of the Gentiles <note>A term used here to refer to non-Jewish people, particularly the ancient Greeks and Romans who relied on "natural reason."</note> through created things. However, they were only able to have a certain shadowy knowledge of God; but the true and perfect knowledge of God (as the whole school of the <term>Kabbalists<note>Followers of Kabbalah, a Jewish mystical tradition that interprets the inner meaning of the Hebrew Bible.</note></term> testifies) was reserved for the coming of the Messiah, who at last came as our Lord Jesus Christ, in whom all things are perfected and are being brought to completion. 

But let us return to the Kabbalah, which is the spiritual law, lying hidden beneath the words of the literal law, which was handed down only by "living voice" <note>original: "viva voce"; meaning it was an oral tradition passed from teacher to student.</note> from one to another. This spiritual law—after the restoration of the Jews from the Babylonian captivity by Cyrus, King of the Persians, and after the Temple was restored under Zerubbabel by Ezra (who was then the head of the Jewish assembly)—was first committed to writing. This occurred when the wise men were called together in a council, so that each might bring into the open whatever he held in his memory concerning the mysteries of the Law. With scribes being employed, it was set down in seventy volumes—for there were that many wise men in that assembly—about which Ezra speaks thus:

G <note>This "G" at the bottom of the page is a "signature mark," used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Jewish Kabbalah provides a higher understanding of God than natural philosophy and describes how this formerly oral tradition was first written down in seventy volumes by Ezra and his council.</summary>

->OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

<note>original: "4. Esdr. 14." This refers to the apocryphal Book of 2 Esdras (also known as 4 Ezra), chapter 14, where the prophet is told to hide certain books for the wise.</note> When forty days had passed, the Most High spoke, saying: "Put the first things that you wrote in public, so that the worthy and the unworthy may read them. But you shall preserve the final seventy books, so that you may hand them over to the wise among the people, whose hearts you know are able to open and keep these secrets. For in these is the vein of understanding, the fountain of wisdom, and the river of knowledge."

For the law itself contains an ineffable <term>Theology</term> <note>In this context, the study of the divine essence itself.</note> concerning the <term>supersubstantial</term> <note>A philosophical term for a reality that exists beyond or above all physical substance or categories of being.</note> deity, an exact <term>Metaphysics</term> concerning the <term>intelligible</term> <note>Things that can be understood by the mind but have no physical form, such as ideas or spirits.</note> and angelic forms, and a most firm philosophy regarding the corporeal world and natural things.

And from this it has come into use, so that among more recent Hebrews, even whatever knowledge is more hidden and concealed, or which deals with the secret operations of miraculous effects, is called <term>Cabala</term> <note>original: "Cabala." Now commonly spelled Kabbalah, referring to the Jewish mystical and esoteric tradition.</note>. 

From this it has happened that even those who boasted of performing stupendous deeds through a certain secret league, pact, and agreement entered into with demons, called themselves by the more honorable name of "Cabalists" in order to cover the wickedness of their cursed craft and superstition. 

Consequently, the holy name of Cabala eventually fell into suspicion, just as the sacred name of Magic did; both are suspected, and both have been profaned, according to the old proverb which says: "Sacred things are profaned when they are seized by the profane." 

Therefore, the Jews possessed a written law, published for the common people; they also possessed the utterances of God—namely, the hidden mysteries of the highest divinity, which lay concealed beneath the "shell" <note>The "cortex" or outer literal meaning of the scripture.</note> of the words of the written law. These were delivered only to the wise, and it was not permitted to reveal them to the common people. For mysteries so full of divinity...

<summary>Agrippa explains the origin of the term "Cabala," citing the biblical prophet Esdras to argue that a secret, oral tradition exists alongside the written Law. He laments that the names of Cabala and Magic have been tarnished by people using them to hide demonic pacts.</summary>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a discussion from the previous page regarding the dangers of sharing sacred mysteries with the uninitiated.</meta>

->### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD<-

...[to share] the most secret things with the foolish common people, what else would it be than to give what is holy to the dogs? This is the very thing Christ himself forbade in his Gospel. He, who was promised through the law and longed for within the law, finally came at the favorable time as the fulfiller and perfecter of the law, just as he himself said: <margin>Matthew 7</margin> "For I have not come to abolish the law, but to fulfill it." <margin>Matthew 5</margin> <note>original: "Non enim ueni soluere legem, sed adimplere."</note>

For the whole law was divided into three parts: they were either shadowy figures of the light to come, or prophetic words of the truth to come, or precepts for living of the perfection to come. But if you grasp only the literal sense of the law, without the spirit of the light, truth, and perfection to come, nothing will be more ridiculous than the law; it would be most similar to an old woman’s tale or a "Milesian story." <note>A "Milesian story" refers to the *Milesiaka*, ancient Greek tales known for being frivolous, satirical, or indecent—the opposite of sacred scripture.</note>

Furthermore, Christ has come as the sun of justice, the true light, the clearest truth, and the true perfection of life for all people who believe in his name. He himself fulfilled the law, so that from now on there is no need for the law. Nor do we henceforth know God in the darkness of created things, nor in the shadow of the Jewish law, but in the light of faith in Jesus Christ. This is true knowledge: the wisdom of the Father and the understanding of man, in whom—as Paul says—all things are summed up, both those in the heavens and those on the earth. <margin>Ephesians 1</margin> <note>The author is referencing the concept of "recapitulation," where Christ is seen as the focal point of all history and creation.</note>

Therefore, let us now consequently speak of the final and perfect knowledge of God, which is through the Gospel of Christ Jesus our Lord.

**A**ll... <note>The text breaks here at the large initial letter "O", beginning the new chapter's first word, likely "Omne" (All/Every).</note>

<summary>This page argues that Christ is the fulfillment of the Old Testament law, transforming its "shadowy" rituals into clear spiritual truth and providing the ultimate way to know God.</summary>
